# Extracted Student Misconceptions

**Model:** gpt-5.4-mini
**Topic group:** mds_sn1_sn2_reduction
**Total misconceptions:** 449
**Papers processed:** 48

## Misconception 1: Students think organic reactions can be understood simply by assuming that the most nearby groups on a two-dimensional drawing react with each other directly, with the hydrogen of one hydroxyl and the whole hydroxyl group of the other combining to form water.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Lasso chemistry in ester formation
**Correct concept:** Mechanisms must be explained by electron movement and actual bond-making/bond-breaking, not by merely connecting nearby groups on a drawing.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify nucleophiles, electrophiles, and electron-pair movements, then show which bonds break and form in a sequence that is chemically and stereochemically plausible rather than relying on proximity in the formula drawing.
**Concept category:** mechanistic representation / proximity-based reasoning
**Source:** ORGANIC REACTION MECHANISMS (10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf/10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "the most proximate groups react with each other"

## Misconception 2: Students may fail to see how an epoxide ring is formed if they reason only from the starting structures without tracking the stepwise electron movement.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Epoxide formation from Darzens condensation
**Correct concept:** The epoxide forms through stepwise acid/base deprotonation, nucleophilic attack, then intramolecular ring closure with bromide leaving.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should follow each intermediate: base removes the acidic proton, the resulting anion attacks the carbonyl, and the alkoxide then attacks the carbon bearing bromine to close the three-membered ring and expel bromide.
**Concept category:** mechanistic pathway recognition
**Source:** ORGANIC REACTION MECHANISMS (10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf/10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "it is not immediately obvious how the epoxide ring has been formed"

## Misconception 3: Students may predict the product stereochemistry by a direct substitution/lasso-style argument and conclude that (2S,3S)-2,3-dibromobutane should be the product.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Stereochemistry in bromination of 3-bromobutan-2-ol
**Correct concept:** The reaction proceeds through a bridged bromonium ion, leading to racemization at the relevant center and formation of a racemic mixture.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the predicted stereochemical outcome with experiment and infer that a bridged intermediate is required because it explains backside opening from either end and the observed racemic product.
**Concept category:** stereochemical reasoning / substitution mechanism
**Source:** ORGANIC REACTION MECHANISMS (10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf/10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "this analysis would predict that when (2S,3S)-3-bromobutan-2-ol reacts with HBr, the stereochemistry of the resultant product would be (2S,3S)-2,3-dibromobutane"

## Misconception 4: Students may assume that if a mechanism predicts a single stereoisomer, the product will be optically active, and may overlook the possibility of a racemic mixture with no optical activity.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Reaction outcomes and optical activity
**Correct concept:** A reaction can yield an equal mixture of enantiomers, which is optically inactive overall.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should consider whether a reaction creates enantiomers in equal amounts; if both are formed equally, the product mixture is racemic and has no net optical rotation.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry / racemic mixtures
**Source:** ORGANIC REACTION MECHANISMS (10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf/10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "the product is a mixture, which comprises an equal amount of (2S,3S)-2,3-dibromobutane and (2R,3R)-2,3-dibromobutane, and which displays no optical activity"

## Misconception 5: Students may not understand that curved arrows represent electron-pair movement and therefore may miss the actual sequence of bond changes in a mechanism.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Curly-arrow chemistry and bond rearrangement
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows indicate the movement of electron pairs between molecular orbitals and allow one to track bond making and bond breaking.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret each curved arrow as the movement of two electrons from a donor orbital/lone pair or bond to an acceptor site, then update formal charges and connectivity step by step.
**Concept category:** electron-pair movement notation
**Source:** ORGANIC REACTION MECHANISMS (10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf/10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "The movement of the electron pairs is indicated by the use of a curved arrow"

## Misconception 6: Students may overlook that carbon cannot exceed four covalent bonds during a substitution or ring-closing step.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Bonding limits in substitution/ring closure
**Correct concept:** Carbon should not be shown with more than four covalent bonds at any instant in a valid mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should ensure that as one bond forms, another bond breaks if necessary, so carbon’s valence is never exceeded during intermediate steps.
**Concept category:** valence / bonding constraints
**Source:** ORGANIC REACTION MECHANISMS (10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf/10.1201_9781315273181_previewpdf.md)
**Evidence:** "the carbon never has more than four covalent bonds around its nucleus at any one time"

## Misconception 7: Students treat SN2 reactions as if they proceed by an SN1 pathway with a carbocation intermediate.

**Topic:** Organic substitution reaction mechanisms / SN1 vs SN2 mechanism identification
**Correct concept:** SN2 reactions occur in a single concerted step with no carbocation intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that SN2 involves backside attack by the nucleophile and simultaneous leaving-group departure, so there is no discrete carbocation stage to draw or describe.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanism type
**Prevalence:** Observed as a common error; the paper reports that students' correct SN2 arrow responses were 17.1% and 14.3%, while SN1 correct arrow responses were only 1.4% and 2.9%.
**Population:** 70 third-year undergraduate Chemistry major students
**Source:** Analysis of undergraduate chemistry students' responses to substitution reaction mechanisms: a road to mastery (10.1515_cti-2023-0075/10.1515_cti-2023-0075.md)
**Evidence:** "Most students ignored the carbocation intermediates provided in the questions for the SN1 reaction mechanism and drew SN2 mechanisms for these questions."

## Misconception 8: Students believe SN2 mechanisms include carbocation formation and nucleophilic attack on that carbocation.

**Topic:** Organic substitution reaction mechanisms / SN2 explanation of steps
**Correct concept:** SN2 mechanisms do not form a carbocation; the nucleophile attacks as the leaving group departs in one step.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain SN2 as a concerted substitution where the nucleophile attacks the electrophilic carbon while the leaving group leaves, all in the same step, without any carbocation intermediate.
**Concept category:** intermediate formation
**Prevalence:** Described as a pattern in the incorrect explanations; incorrect explanation rates for SN2 explanation items were 45.7% and 57.1%.
**Population:** 70 third-year undergraduate Chemistry major students
**Source:** Analysis of undergraduate chemistry students' responses to substitution reaction mechanisms: a road to mastery (10.1515_cti-2023-0075/10.1515_cti-2023-0075.md)
**Evidence:** "Again, SN2 reaction mechanisms do not proceed with the formation of a carbocation intermediate, but students were talking about the formation of carbocations and nucleophilic attack of carbocations in their explanations of SN2 reaction mechanisms."

## Misconception 9: Students think that leaving-group departure means elimination and nucleophilic attack means substitution, treating the two as separate events in the same reaction.

**Topic:** Organic substitution reaction mechanisms / Substitution vs elimination
**Correct concept:** In substitution reactions, the nucleophile replaces the leaving group; elimination is a different reaction pathway involving loss of groups to form unsaturation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish reaction types by the overall outcome and electron flow: substitution replaces one group with another, whereas elimination forms a new pi bond by removing atoms/groups.
**Concept category:** reaction type classification
**Prevalence:** Reported as a common feature of incorrect responses; no exact count provided.
**Population:** Subset of students who provided incorrect explanations
**Source:** Analysis of undergraduate chemistry students' responses to substitution reaction mechanisms: a road to mastery (10.1515_cti-2023-0075/10.1515_cti-2023-0075.md)
**Evidence:** "This elimination and substitution reaction occurs simultaneously. As the bromine atom is cleaved away, a nucleophile attaches to the carbocation formed at the same time"

## Misconception 10: Students use the wrong named mechanism, for example calling an SN2 reaction an E1 or SN1 process, based on surface cues.

**Topic:** Organic substitution reaction mechanisms / SN1 vs SN2 mechanism identification in explanations
**Correct concept:** Students should identify the mechanism from the actual reaction pathway and electron movement, not from isolated cues like leaving-group quality.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should match the described reactants, products, intermediates, and electron-pushing pattern to the correct mechanism name before explaining it.
**Concept category:** mechanism naming
**Prevalence:** Presented as an example of the incorrect explanation category; incorrect explanations were 45.7% to 65.7% across items.
**Population:** 70 third-year undergraduate Chemistry major students
**Source:** Analysis of undergraduate chemistry students' responses to substitution reaction mechanisms: a road to mastery (10.1515_cti-2023-0075/10.1515_cti-2023-0075.md)
**Evidence:** "Akosua's explanation was as follows: 'The mechanism undergoes E1 substitution. The bromine atom is a better leaving group'."

## Misconception 11: Students interpret the first SN1 step as if the leaving-group departure itself is the whole reaction and stop there.

**Topic:** Organic substitution reaction mechanisms / Leaving-group step interpretation in SN1
**Correct concept:** SN1 is a two-step process: leaving-group departure to form a carbocation, followed by nucleophilic attack, and often a deprotonation step if needed.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should describe both the formation of the carbocation intermediate and the subsequent nucleophilic attack, showing the full multistep pathway.
**Concept category:** stepwise reaction pathway
**Prevalence:** A significant number of students gave only partial explanations; partial explanations ranged from 10% to 21.4%.
**Population:** 70 third-year undergraduate Chemistry major students
**Source:** Analysis of undergraduate chemistry students' responses to substitution reaction mechanisms: a road to mastery (10.1515_cti-2023-0075/10.1515_cti-2023-0075.md)
**Evidence:** "a lot of students described only the first part of the given SN1 reaction mechanisms (the leaving of the leaving group leading to the formation of a carbocation intermediate)"

## Misconception 12: Students draw curved arrows without assigning correct electron-flow meaning to them and may use arrows from the wrong direction or omit required arrows.

**Topic:** Organic substitution reaction mechanisms / Electron-pushing formalism
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows must represent electron movement from an electron-rich source to an electron-poor site, with all necessary steps shown.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should trace electrons from a lone pair, bond, or negative charge to the electrophilic site and include each step of the mechanism without reverse or missing arrows.
**Concept category:** electron-pushing formalism
**Prevalence:** Common across arrow-type responses; correct mechanism rates ranged from 1.4% to 17.1%, while incorrect mechanism rates ranged from 71.4% to 78.6%.
**Population:** 70 third-year undergraduate Chemistry major students
**Source:** Analysis of undergraduate chemistry students' responses to substitution reaction mechanisms: a road to mastery (10.1515_cti-2023-0075/10.1515_cti-2023-0075.md)
**Evidence:** "students did not attribute meaning to the electron-pushing arrows and did not consider whether the mechanisms they had drawn were chemically reasonable or not"

## Misconception 13: Students believe solvolysis proceeds by a two-step SN1 mechanism in which a planar free carbocation forms first and then the solvent attacks from either side.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / SN1 solvolysis
**Correct concept:** In these solvolysis reactions, a free solvated carbocation intermediate is not required; the paper argues for a bimolecular one-step process with inversion in each elementary substitution step and reversibility overall.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use stereochemical and isotopic evidence to reason that if every substitution step occurs with inversion and exchange/racemization and isotope incorporation occur together, a reversible substitution pathway can explain the observations without invoking a long-lived free carbocation.
**Concept category:** carbocation intermediate
**Population:** Textbook-authored student/teaching context; the paper reports "many" students later accepted textbook views
**Source:** Inadequacies of the S<sub>N</sub>1 Mechanism (1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism/1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism.md)
**Evidence:** "the slow formation of a planar carbocation intermediate, followed by fast addition of solvent from either of the two sides"

## Misconception 14: Students believe complete racemization in SN1 solvolysis is explained by a carbocation being attacked equally from both sides.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / SN1 racemization
**Correct concept:** The paper argues that racemization can arise from reversible substitution steps with inversion in each direction, not necessarily from a free carbocation attacked equally from both faces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish overall racemization from the mechanism of a single step and recognize that forward and reverse inversions can generate racemate even when each substitution step is stereospecific.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry / racemization
**Source:** Inadequacies of the S<sub>N</sub>1 Mechanism (1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism/1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism.md)
**Evidence:** "Still, this leaves unexplained why there is racemization and why it is not complete."

## Misconception 15: Students believe SN1 means first the leaving group departs and then the nucleophile attacks, with SN2 being the only simultaneous process.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / SN1 versus SN2 timing
**Correct concept:** The paper argues that both classes can be understood as one-step bond-making/bond-breaking processes with similar trigonal-bipyramidal transition states; the distinction is better framed by reversibility, reagents, and stereochemical outcome than by an oversimplified step order.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from transition-state geometry and stereochemical inversion that bond breaking and bond forming are coupled in the elementary step rather than occurring as isolated sequential events.
**Concept category:** mechanism timing
**Population:** Textbook learners; the author reports many students accepted this textbook summary
**Source:** Inadequacies of the S<sub>N</sub>1 Mechanism (1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism/1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism.md)
**Evidence:** "The difference between the SN1 and SN2 mechanisms is in the timing of the steps. In the SN1 mechanism, first X leaves, then Y attacks. In the SN2 case, the two things happen simultaneously."

## Misconception 16: Students expect stereoisomeric substrates that form the same free carbocation to give identical solvolytic products.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / effect of substrate stereoisomerism
**Correct concept:** The paper argues that different stereoisomeric substrates can give different products because the reaction does not proceed through a common free carbocation; stereochemistry of the reacting molecule influences the pathway.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should predict that if stereochemistry is retained in the reacting system and each step requires a specific anti arrangement, then different isomers can follow different trajectories and produce different products.
**Concept category:** carbocation intermediate
**Source:** Inadequacies of the S<sub>N</sub>1 Mechanism (1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism/1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism.md)
**Evidence:** "Such pairs should give identical free carbocations, hence identical solvolytic products, but they give instead dramatically different products"

## Misconception 17: Students assume isotope incorporation under solvolytic conditions only supports an SN1 carbocation mechanism and not a substitution process with inversion.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / degenerate substitution and isotope exchange
**Correct concept:** The paper presents isotope-exchange experiments as evidence that degenerate substitution can occur with inversion, consistent with a reversible bimolecular process.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect equal rates of racemization and isotope incorporation to repeated substitution events, each proceeding with inversion, rather than to long-lived carbocations.
**Concept category:** isotope exchange / inversion
**Source:** Inadequacies of the S<sub>N</sub>1 Mechanism (1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism/1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism.md)
**Evidence:** "each step should occur with inversion, and if the three R groups are different... the rate of isotope incorporation and the rate of racemization should be the same."

## Misconception 18: Students think alkyl carbocations are common reactive intermediates in nucleophilic solvolysis in water or alcohol.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / carbocation stability and substitution
**Correct concept:** The paper argues that alkyl carbocations are extremely reactive and generally do not persist in nucleophilic solvents; observed solvolysis need not involve free carbocations.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the high reactivity of carbocations and the nucleophilicity of the solvent to infer that a long-lived free cation is unlikely in water or alcohol.
**Concept category:** carbocation intermediate
**Population:** Students in organic chemistry courses; the author states he warned students against believing in free carbocation intermediates
**Source:** Inadequacies of the S<sub>N</sub>1 Mechanism (1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism/1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism.md)
**Evidence:** "alkyl cations are extremely reactive and can only exist in the vacuum of a mass spectrometer or in non nucleophilic solvents"

## Misconception 19: Students believe the tertiary-to-primary reactivity trend in solvolysis is best explained by electronic stabilization of a carbocation intermediate.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / SN1 versus SN2 reactivity order
**Correct concept:** The paper argues that the opposite reactivity trends under SN2 and solvolysis can be explained by steric effects, bond loosening, reversibility, and nucleofuge behavior without needing carbocation stabilization as the central explanation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare how steric hindrance slows direct attack in SN2 and how favorable leaving-group behavior and bond loosening can make tertiary substrates react faster in solvolysis.
**Concept category:** reactivity trends
**Source:** Inadequacies of the S<sub>N</sub>1 Mechanism (1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism/1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism.md)
**Evidence:** "One argument for the SN1 mechanism in solvolysis has been that a reactivity sequence ... is observed that runs parallel with the electronic stabilization of cations"

## Misconception 20: Students may think that adding tight ion pairs, solvated ion pairs, solvent-separated ion pairs, simultaneous SN1/SN2, and borderline cases resolves the mechanism.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / SN1 complexity
**Correct concept:** The paper argues these ad hoc categories do not solve the underlying problem of explaining solvolysis without unnecessary carbocation intermediates; a unified reversible bimolecular model is preferred.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate whether added intermediates improve explanatory power or merely complicate the mechanism, and prefer a model that accounts for stereochemistry, kinetics, and isotopic exchange consistently.
**Concept category:** ion pairs / mechanistic models
**Source:** Inadequacies of the S<sub>N</sub>1 Mechanism (1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism/1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism.md)
**Evidence:** "Winstein introduced a modification of Ingold's mechanism based on tight ion pairs, solvated ion pairs, and solvent-separated ion pairs"

## Misconception 21: Students may believe that if a solvolytic reaction is reversible, reversibility alone explains racemization.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / racemization and inversion
**Correct concept:** The paper states that reversibility by itself does not explain racemization; one must consider that both forward and backward steps can invert configuration and thus partially cancel or produce net racemization depending on pathways.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should analyze the stereochemical consequence of both forward and reverse substitutions rather than assuming that equilibrium automatically gives racemate.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry / reversibility
**Source:** Inadequacies of the S<sub>N</sub>1 Mechanism (1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism/1998-inadequacies-of-the-sn1-mechanism.md)
**Evidence:** "However, this reversibility alone does not explain the observed racemization, since inversion in the forward reaction is compensated by inversion in the backward reaction"

## Misconception 22: Secondary alkyl compounds can undergo solvolysis with significant S_N1 involvement, or as 'borderline' cases between S_N1 and S_N2.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / secondary alkyl compounds in solvolysis
**Correct concept:** For the secondary alkyl substrates discussed in the paper, solvolysis proceeds only via S_N2 under the documented conditions; there is no support here for significant S_N1 involvement or borderline behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the stereochemical evidence and the later kinetic/competition studies: when products show inversion rather than racemization, the mechanism is backside attack (S_N2). If all substitution products from secondary substrates are inverted under the studied conditions, then the reaction is not proceeding through a carbenium-ion S_N1 pathway.
**Concept category:** mechanism assignment
**Population:** Students using introductory organic chemistry texts and mechanistic interpretations of secondary-substrate solvolysis
**Source:** Absence of S<sub>N</sub>1 Involvement in the Solvolysis of Secondary Alkyl Compounds (2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds/2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds.md)
**Evidence:** "secondary alkyl compounds go only via the S_N2 mechanism"

## Misconception 23: Secondary substrates in solvolysis form carbenium-ion intermediates that then give the substitution product.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / secondary substrates forming carbocations
**Correct concept:** For the secondary systems reviewed in the paper, the evidence supports direct substitution by S_N2 pathways rather than carbenium-ion intermediates.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect complete or near-complete inversion with direct backside attack and recognize that a carbocation intermediate would be expected to lead to racemization or loss of stereochemical information. Thus retained or inverted stereochemistry rules out a carbenium-ion mechanism for these cases.
**Concept category:** intermediates
**Population:** Students interpreting secondary-substrate substitution mechanisms
**Source:** Absence of S<sub>N</sub>1 Involvement in the Solvolysis of Secondary Alkyl Compounds (2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds/2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds.md)
**Evidence:** "There is no support in the Ingold textbook (3) or in the literature for secondary systems exhibiting 'borderline' behavior or carbenium ion-type intermediates"

## Misconception 24: Solvolysis of secondary alkyl compounds should give racemization because it is diagnostic of an S_N1 mechanism.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution stereochemistry / solvolysis stereochemistry of secondary substrates
**Correct concept:** The documented secondary-substrate examples here give inversion, often essentially complete inversion, consistent with S_N2; racemization is not the expected outcome for these secondary systems under the conditions discussed.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from stereochemistry: S_N2 gives inversion by backside attack, while S_N1 through a planar carbocation tends toward racemization. Therefore an inverted alcohol product indicates S_N2 participation rather than S_N1.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry
**Population:** Students learning mechanistic stereochemistry from examples of sec-octyl bromide and related substrates
**Source:** Absence of S<sub>N</sub>1 Involvement in the Solvolysis of Secondary Alkyl Compounds (2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds/2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds.md)
**Evidence:** "the solvent reactions of sec-octyl bromide exhibit in considerable amount the racemization which is diagnostic of the unimolecular mechanism"

## Misconception 25: A partially optically active product from secondary-substrate solvolysis is evidence for a mixed S_N1/S_N2 mechanism.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution stereochemistry / interpretation of product optical purity
**Correct concept:** The paper argues that apparent intermediate optical purity can arise from competing stereospecific displacement pathways, not from S_N1 involvement. In the cited studies, even when optical purity is less than 100%, the underlying pathways can still be S_N2.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish product optical purity from mechanism. Intermediate ee can result from two S_N2 pathways with different stereochemical outcomes or from racemization before/after substitution, so ee alone does not prove S_N1. Mechanistic assignment requires considering the whole reaction network and stereochemical pathway.
**Concept category:** stereochemical interpretation
**Population:** Students interpreting optical purity data in substitution reactions
**Source:** Absence of S<sub>N</sub>1 Involvement in the Solvolysis of Secondary Alkyl Compounds (2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds/2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds.md)
**Evidence:** "Apparent racemization in mixed solvents is but the result of competitive but stereospecific displacement processes."

## Misconception 26: Mixed-solvent solvolysis of secondary sulfonates must proceed via S_N1 if racemization or apparent racemization is observed.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / secondary sulfonates in mixed solvents
**Correct concept:** For the secondary sulfonates discussed, the observed behavior is explained by consecutive S_N2 displacement steps and competing pathways, not by an S_N1 carbocation mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should analyze the sequence of substitution steps: if one solvent molecule displaces the leaving group by S_N2 and then another nucleophile displaces the first, the net optical purity can change without any S_N1 step. Thus racemization-like results do not automatically imply carbocation formation.
**Concept category:** mechanism assignment
**Population:** Students studying mixed-solvent solvolysis examples such as 2-octyl brosylate
**Source:** Absence of S<sub>N</sub>1 Involvement in the Solvolysis of Secondary Alkyl Compounds (2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds/2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds.md)
**Evidence:** "all of the reactions went via S_N2 mechanisms with 100% inversion, but the alcohol was of intermediate optical purity because it was formed by two different pathways"

## Misconception 27: Primary substrates undergo about 20% S_N1 and tertiary substrates about 15% S_N2 in the general case.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution trends across substrate classes / primary and tertiary substrate mechanism percentages in the Ingold diagram
**Correct concept:** The paper rejects these percentages as unsupported; under the simplified scheme presented, primary substrates go only via S_N2 and tertiary substrates go only via S_N1 under ionizing conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should not treat a textbook diagram as a universal rule when the paper explicitly states that later evidence does not support those partial percentages. Mechanistic assignment should follow substrate class and conditions: primary and methyl substrates are S_N2-only, tertiary substrates are S_N1-only under the relevant ionizing conditions.
**Concept category:** mechanism trends
**Population:** Students relying on the Ingold textbook diagram for substitution mechanisms
**Source:** Absence of S<sub>N</sub>1 Involvement in the Solvolysis of Secondary Alkyl Compounds (2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds/2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds.md)
**Evidence:** "There is no support ... for tertiary systems going ≈15% by an S_N2 mechanism; or for primary systems going ≈20% via an S_N1 mechanism."

## Misconception 28: Any kinetically first-order substitution reaction must be S_N1, and first-order order alone proves a unimolecular mechanism.

**Topic:** Kinetics versus mechanism / equating first-order kinetics with S_N1
**Correct concept:** Kinetic order alone does not prove mechanism; first-order behavior can arise from reaction conditions or mixed processes, and mechanism must be established by additional evidence such as stereochemistry and reaction pathway analysis.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate rate law from molecular mechanism. First-order kinetics may describe the rate-determining step, but one must still test whether the structure, stereochemistry, and competition experiments support a carbocation intermediate or some other pathway.
**Concept category:** kinetics and mechanism
**Population:** Students who infer mechanism directly from rate-law order
**Source:** Absence of S<sub>N</sub>1 Involvement in the Solvolysis of Secondary Alkyl Compounds (2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds/2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds.md)
**Evidence:** "they equate the kinetic order of a reaction with the molecularity of the reaction transition state"

## Misconception 29: Secondary substrate solvolysis occurs without any nucleophilic participation only when an S_N1 carbocation intermediate is formed.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / nucleophilic participation in secondary solvolysis
**Correct concept:** The paper concludes that secondary substrates can show substantial nucleophilic participation and still proceed by S_N2, including solvent-assisted S_N2 or competing S_N2 pathways.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that solvent participation does not automatically mean S_N1. If the transition state involves backside attack and inversion, nucleophilic participation is part of an S_N2 process, not evidence for a carbocation intermediate.
**Concept category:** solvent participation
**Population:** Students interpreting solvent-assisted substitution in secondary systems
**Source:** Absence of S<sub>N</sub>1 Involvement in the Solvolysis of Secondary Alkyl Compounds (2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds/2009-absence-of-sn1-involvement-in-the-solvolysis-of-secondary-alkyl-compounds.md)
**Evidence:** "This interpretation suggests a variation in the magnitude of nucleophilic solvent assistance in S_N2 transition states"

## Misconception 30: Students believe reaction mechanisms are generally easy to understand, but the paper documents that many actually struggle to understand and write them.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Overall understanding and writing mechanisms
**Correct concept:** A reaction mechanism should be understood as a stepwise explanation of how bonds break and form and how intermediates and arrows represent electron movement.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect each step of the mechanism to bond changes, electron movement, intermediates, and the overall transformation, rather than memorizing isolated steps.
**Concept category:** general reaction mechanism understanding
**Prevalence:** 62.5% strongly agreed; 7.5% agreed (70% total)
**Population:** 40 bachelor-level chemistry students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "I feel difficulty understanding the reaction mechanism."

## Misconception 31: Students can easily write reaction mechanisms taught in class.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Writing mechanisms
**Correct concept:** Students should be able to represent the mechanism step-by-step using correct arrows, intermediates, and bond changes.
**Expected reasoning:** To write a mechanism, a student must identify the reacting species, follow electron flow with curly arrows, and show each transformation in the correct sequence.
**Concept category:** mechanism representation / arrow pushing
**Prevalence:** 37.5% strongly agreed; 10% agreed (47.5% total)
**Population:** 40 bachelor-level chemistry students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "I find it challenging to write reaction mechanisms taught in class."

## Misconception 32: Students think SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms are straightforward or easy to learn.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / SN1 and SN2 reactions
**Correct concept:** SN1 and SN2 mechanisms must be distinguished by their different rate laws, steps, intermediates, and substitution pathways.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare substrate, nucleophile, leaving group, solvent, and the presence or absence of a carbocation intermediate to determine the correct mechanism.
**Concept category:** substitution mechanisms
**Prevalence:** 50% strongly agreed; 25% disagreed (75% total reporting difficulty)
**Population:** 40 bachelor-level chemistry students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "The concept of SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms is difficult for me."

## Misconception 33: Students do not recognize inductive, mesomeric, and electrometric effects as important factors in reaction mechanisms.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Inductive, mesomeric, and electrometric effects
**Correct concept:** These electronic effects influence electron distribution, reactivity, and reaction pathways in organic mechanisms.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should link substituent effects and electron movement to reactivity and mechanistic outcome, using resonance and inductive reasoning where appropriate.
**Concept category:** electron effects / reactivity
**Prevalence:** Reported qualitatively; no percentage given
**Population:** Interviewed student participants (e.g., S7)
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "The factors that affect the reaction rate, like inductive, mesomeric, and electrometric effects of the reaction mechanism, are challenging to learn."

## Misconception 34: Students confuse the condition of bond fission and do not distinguish how bonds break in mechanisms.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Bond fission
**Correct concept:** Students should distinguish homolytic from heterolytic bond fission and relate each to the products formed.
**Expected reasoning:** A correct explanation requires identifying whether electrons split equally or unequally during bond breaking and predicting the resulting radicals or ions.
**Concept category:** bond fission
**Prevalence:** Reported qualitatively; no percentage given
**Population:** Interviewed student participants (e.g., S10)
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "The condition of bond fission ... confuses me"

## Misconception 35: Students cannot identify the attacking reagents in a mechanism and are unsure which species is the electrophile or nucleophile.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Attacking reagents identification
**Correct concept:** Students should identify the electron-pair donor as the nucleophile and the electron-pair acceptor as the electrophile.
**Expected reasoning:** By examining electron density and charge, a student should determine which species donates electrons and which species accepts them at each step.
**Concept category:** electrophiles and nucleophiles
**Prevalence:** Reported qualitatively; no percentage given
**Population:** Interviewed student participants (e.g., S10)
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "the formation of attacking reagents identification confuses me"

## Misconception 36: Students cannot define or identify electrophiles and nucleophiles before studying the reaction mechanism.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Electrophiles and nucleophiles
**Correct concept:** Electrophiles are electron-pair acceptors and nucleophiles are electron-pair donors; identifying them is foundational for mechanism analysis.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use electron density, charge, and lone pairs to decide which species will attack and which will be attacked.
**Concept category:** electrophiles and nucleophiles
**Prevalence:** 75% strongly agreed; 25% disagreed (teacher responses indicating student difficulty)
**Population:** Teachers responding about their students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "Students are confused in identifying electrophiles and nucleophiles before studying reaction mechanism."

## Misconception 37: Students can easily define reaction intermediates.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Reaction intermediates
**Correct concept:** Reaction intermediates are transient species formed during a multi-step mechanism and must be distinguished from reactants and products.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify intermediates as species that appear in one step and are consumed in a later step, rather than final products or starting materials.
**Concept category:** reaction intermediates
**Prevalence:** 25% strongly agreed; 50% agreed; 25% disagreed (teachers' responses show divided views)
**Population:** Teachers' responses about students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "students themselves can define reaction intermediates easily"

## Misconception 38: Students have adequate foundations in modulation and visualization for understanding reaction mechanisms.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Visualization and modulation
**Correct concept:** Students need support to visualize abstract molecular processes and represent them across macroscopic, microscopic, and symbolic levels.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should mentally or graphically map particle-level events to symbolic arrows and structures, aided by models or simulations.
**Concept category:** visualization / 3D representation
**Prevalence:** Reported qualitatively; no percentage given
**Population:** Teachers (e.g., T1, T2) discussing students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "students' poor foundations in modulation and visualization make teaching reaction mechanisms challenging"

## Misconception 39: Students can understand reaction mechanisms without connecting them to experimental evidence.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Linking mechanisms to experiments
**Correct concept:** Mechanistic explanations should be supported by experimental observations and evidence.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use experimental results, rate data, and observed products to justify the proposed mechanism rather than relying only on memorization.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reasoning / evidence-based explanation
**Prevalence:** Reported in conclusion; no percentage given
**Population:** Students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (3%2E/3..md)
**Evidence:** "One of the students' most significant difficulties is linking reaction mechanisms with experiments."

## Misconception 40: Students may think the SN1 reaction does not proceed through a carbocation intermediate, or do not understand that the intermediate is planar and can be attacked from either side.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution reactions / SN1 reaction mechanism
**Correct concept:** In an SN1 reaction, the leaving group departs first to form a planar carbocation intermediate, and the nucleophile can attack from either side, often leading to racemization if the substrate is optically active.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that the reaction occurs in two steps: first ionization to a carbocation, then nucleophilic attack. Because the carbocation is trigonal planar, the nucleophile can approach from either face, so stereochemical scrambling/racemization can occur.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanism / stereochemistry
**Population:** University students in a basic organic chemistry course; practice session with four third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Development of Electronic Lab-book for College Chemistry-Experiment - SN1 & SN2 Reactions - (63813/63813.md)
**Evidence:** "In SN1 reactions ... a carbocation is first formed, which then reacts with the nucleophile. The carbocation is planar, and the anion can attack from either side."

## Misconception 41: Students may think an SN1 reaction gives a single stereochemical product with retention of configuration rather than a racemic mixture.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution reactions / SN1 stereochemical outcome
**Correct concept:** If the original substrate is optically active, SN1 reaction through a planar carbocation gives attack from either face and therefore a racemic mixture of products.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the planar intermediate with equal likelihood of attack on either side, which explains loss of optical purity and formation of racemate.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry
**Population:** University students in a basic organic chemistry course
**Source:** Development of Electronic Lab-book for College Chemistry-Experiment - SN1 & SN2 Reactions - (63813/63813.md)
**Evidence:** "Consequently, if the original molecule is optically active, a racemic mixture of products is obtained."

## Misconception 42: Students may think SN2 is not a concerted process, or may not know that the nucleophile attacks from one side while the leaving group departs from the opposite side.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution reactions / SN2 reaction mechanism
**Correct concept:** An SN2 reaction is concerted: bond formation and bond cleavage occur simultaneously, with backside attack by the nucleophile opposite the leaving group.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that the nucleophile approaches the electrophilic carbon as the leaving group leaves in one step; the geometry requires attack from the backside, opposite the leaving group, producing inversion.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanism
**Population:** University students in a basic organic chemistry course; practice session with four third-year chemistry students
**Source:** Development of Electronic Lab-book for College Chemistry-Experiment - SN1 & SN2 Reactions - (63813/63813.md)
**Evidence:** "the SN2 reaction ... is a concerted reaction in which the nucleophile approaches from the left side of the central carbon as the other group leaves to the right."

## Misconception 43: Students may not know that SN2 causes inversion of configuration, or may think the product retains the original configuration.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution reactions / SN2 stereochemical outcome
**Correct concept:** SN2 reactions proceed with inversion of configuration at the reacting stereocenter, known as Walden inversion.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from backside attack that the incoming nucleophile must displace the leaving group from the opposite side, flipping the three-dimensional arrangement and inverting configuration.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry
**Population:** University students in a basic organic chemistry course
**Source:** Development of Electronic Lab-book for College Chemistry-Experiment - SN1 & SN2 Reactions - (63813/63813.md)
**Evidence:** "In this case, the configuration of the molecule is inverted. ... an effect known as the Walden inversion"

## Misconception 44: Students may confuse which reaction proceeds by stepwise carbocation formation versus which is concerted backside attack, or may not be able to distinguish SN1 from SN2 from textbook schemes alone.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution reactions / SN1 vs SN2 comparison
**Correct concept:** SN1 and SN2 have distinct mechanisms: SN1 is stepwise with a carbocation intermediate, while SN2 is concerted with backside attack and inversion.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the mechanistic sequence and stereochemical consequences: if there is a carbocation intermediate and possible racemization, it is SN1; if there is simultaneous bond-making/breaking and inversion, it is SN2.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanism / stereochemistry
**Prevalence:** Pre-test average 2.00/6.00, post-test average 5.25/6.00
**Population:** Four third-year chemistry students of the teachers' training course at Tokyo Gakugei University
**Source:** Development of Electronic Lab-book for College Chemistry-Experiment - SN1 & SN2 Reactions - (63813/63813.md)
**Evidence:** "A schematic representation of the reaction is often used in textbooks, but it is not always easy for the student to obtain the configuration of the molecule or the dynamics of the reaction."

## Misconception 45: Students may not correctly interpret the reaction energy profile or understand energy change along the reaction coordinate for SN1 and SN2 reactions.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution reactions / Reaction energy / reaction profile
**Correct concept:** Reaction profiles show how potential energy changes during the reaction progress, including activation energy and transition-state region.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate the position on the reaction coordinate to molecular changes and recognize the energy barrier at the transition state and the overall energy change between reactants and products.
**Concept category:** energy profile
**Prevalence:** Pre-test: 0/6 correct on question 6 (reaction energy in SN1, SN2); post-test: 4/6 correct
**Population:** Four third-year chemistry students of the teachers' training course at Tokyo Gakugei University
**Source:** Development of Electronic Lab-book for College Chemistry-Experiment - SN1 & SN2 Reactions - (63813/63813.md)
**Evidence:** "After using the electronic lab-book, students' knowledge in terms of 'reactivity,' 'attacking direction,' and 'energy change' in the SN1 and SN2 reactions was improved."

## Misconception 46: Students may not understand the attacking direction of the nucleophile in SN2, i.e. backside attack relative to the leaving group.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution reactions / Attacking direction in SN2
**Correct concept:** In SN2, the nucleophile approaches the central carbon from the side opposite the leaving group.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from orbital/geometric requirements that the nucleophile must attack from the backside to overlap properly and displace the leaving group in one step.
**Concept category:** attacking direction
**Prevalence:** Pre-test: 3/6 correct on question 4 (attacking direction and product in SN2); post-test: 3/6 correct
**Population:** Four third-year chemistry students of the teachers' training course at Tokyo Gakugei University
**Source:** Development of Electronic Lab-book for College Chemistry-Experiment - SN1 & SN2 Reactions - (63813/63813.md)
**Evidence:** "Figure 5: Quiz, Approaching Direction of Nucleophile to Central Carbon for SN2 Reaction in STEP 3."

## Misconception 47: Students cannot distinguish when nucleophilic substitution should be favored over elimination because they treat the two reaction classes as essentially the same or equally plausible in all cases.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution vs elimination / Overall mechanism selection
**Correct concept:** Nucleophilic substitution and elimination are competing pathways, and the dominant mechanism depends on substrate, reagent, and conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the reaction conditions and substrate features systematically, then decide whether substitution or elimination is more favorable under those conditions rather than assuming both are interchangeable.
**Concept category:** mechanism competition
**Prevalence:** "Many"; no numeric prevalence given
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students
**Source:** Comparing Nucleophilic Substitutions Versus Elimination Reactions in Comprehensive Introductory Organic Chemistry Textbooks (9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105/9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105.md)
**Evidence:** "Many students find the concept of nucleophilicity versus basicity confusing."

## Misconception 48: Students have difficulty predicting which pathway predominates when substitution and elimination are both possible under similar reaction conditions.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution vs elimination / Predicting dominant pathway from reagents/conditions
**Correct concept:** The major pathway is determined by evaluating how substrate structure, nucleophile/base strength, temperature, and steric effects favor one mechanism over the other.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should weigh the relevant factors for the specific substrate and reagent set, then infer which mechanism is kinetically or thermodynamically favored in that situation.
**Concept category:** mechanism competition
**Prevalence:** "Many" organic chemistry students; no numeric prevalence given
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students
**Source:** Comparing Nucleophilic Substitutions Versus Elimination Reactions in Comprehensive Introductory Organic Chemistry Textbooks (9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105/9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105.md)
**Evidence:** "predicting the predominant reaction"

## Misconception 49: Students confuse nucleophilicity with basicity and do not distinguish the two when deciding between substitution and elimination.

**Topic:** Nucleophilicity vs basicity / Kinetic vs thermodynamic roles
**Correct concept:** Nucleophilicity is a kinetic concept, while basicity is a thermodynamic concept; both must be considered separately in mechanism selection.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should ask whether the reagent is a better nucleophile or a stronger base, then connect nucleophilicity to substitution and basicity to elimination tendencies in the given context.
**Concept category:** nucleophilicity/basicity
**Prevalence:** Some students; no numeric prevalence given
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students
**Source:** Comparing Nucleophilic Substitutions Versus Elimination Reactions in Comprehensive Introductory Organic Chemistry Textbooks (9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105/9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105.md)
**Evidence:** "Some students find the concept of nucleophilicity versus basicity confusing."

## Misconception 50: Students may not recognize that SN2 should be considered before SN1, and substitution before elimination, because they do not organize mechanisms from simpler to more complex.

**Topic:** Reaction order and mechanism comparison / Mechanistic complexity ordering
**Correct concept:** SN2 is generally introduced before SN1, and substitution before elimination, because the simpler mechanism helps build understanding of the more complex ones.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should learn and compare mechanisms in an order that starts with the simplest pathway and then moves to more complex competing pathways.
**Concept category:** mechanism ordering
**Population:** Textbook comparison; student misconception inferred from learning difficulty mentioned for undergraduates
**Source:** Comparing Nucleophilic Substitutions Versus Elimination Reactions in Comprehensive Introductory Organic Chemistry Textbooks (9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105/9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105.md)
**Evidence:** "The SN2 mechanism is relatively straightforward and therefore should be presented before the SN1"

## Misconception 51: Students may fail to contrast substrate reactivity toward SN2 and SN1 using an ordered trend or may not see how the same substrate set can be ranked oppositely for the two mechanisms.

**Topic:** SN2 vs SN1 / Contrast of substrate reactivity trends
**Correct concept:** Substrates should be ranked by how reactive they are toward SN2 and SN1, with trends showing opposite extremes for the two mechanisms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the same series of substrates under both mechanisms and note that substrates most favorable for SN2 are least favorable for SN1, and vice versa.
**Concept category:** substrate reactivity trends
**Prevalence:** Majority of texts; no student percentage given
**Population:** Introductory organic chemistry students
**Source:** Comparing Nucleophilic Substitutions Versus Elimination Reactions in Comprehensive Introductory Organic Chemistry Textbooks (9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105/9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105.md)
**Evidence:** "The majority of texts examined herein compare the reactivity of substrates toward SN2 versus SN1 reactions by listing the substrates as a series."

## Misconception 52: Students do not consider the full set of factors needed to choose between SN2 and E2 or between SN1 and E1, relying on only one or two cues.

**Topic:** SN2 vs E2 and SN1 vs E1 / Competing factors for pathway selection
**Correct concept:** Pathway selection should be based on multiple factors, especially base strength, temperature, steric effects, and nucleophilicity, because these competing reactions are similar in reagents and conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate all relevant determinants together rather than using a single shortcut, then decide which mechanism is most favored by the combination of factors.
**Concept category:** mechanism competition
**Prevalence:** "many" organic chemistry students; no numeric prevalence given
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students
**Source:** Comparing Nucleophilic Substitutions Versus Elimination Reactions in Comprehensive Introductory Organic Chemistry Textbooks (9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105/9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105.md)
**Evidence:** "Predicting whether nucleophilic substitution or elimination predominates in these reactions eludes many organic chemistry students"

## Misconception 53: Students may not understand that Zaitsev and Hofmann rules are part of E2 elimination and are needed to predict the alkene product regiochemistry.

**Topic:** E2 elimination / Zaitsev vs Hofmann product prediction
**Correct concept:** Zaitsev and Hofmann eliminations are subcases of E2 that help predict the regio- and stereochemistry of the alkene product.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine which alkene is favored by applying Zaitsev or Hofmann reasoning within an E2 framework, using substrate and reagent features to predict the major product.
**Concept category:** regiochemistry
**Population:** Introductory organic chemistry students
**Source:** Comparing Nucleophilic Substitutions Versus Elimination Reactions in Comprehensive Introductory Organic Chemistry Textbooks (9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105/9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105.md)
**Evidence:** "Zaitsev rules and Hofmann rules predict the regio- and stereochemistry of the new double bond resulting from E2 elimination."

## Misconception 54: Students may rely on only one criterion, such as steric hindrance, when deciding between Zaitsev and Hofmann elimination, instead of considering multiple determinants.

**Topic:** E2 elimination / Determinants of Zaitsev vs Hofmann outcome
**Correct concept:** The Zaitsev vs Hofmann outcome depends on substrate steric requirements, carbanion stability, proton acidity, and leaving-group basicity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should analyze all four determinants and use them together to infer whether the more substituted alkene or the Hofmann product is preferred.
**Concept category:** regiochemistry
**Population:** Introductory organic chemistry students
**Source:** Comparing Nucleophilic Substitutions Versus Elimination Reactions in Comprehensive Introductory Organic Chemistry Textbooks (9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105/9447-Article Text-19888-1-10-20230105.md)
**Evidence:** "Comparing the Zaitsev and the Hofmann eliminations uses substrate steric requirements, carbanion stability, proton acidity, and leaving group basicity as regiochemical determinants"

## Misconception 55: Students cannot correctly identify or apply the reaction chemistry of functional groups when asked to write reactions, syntheses, or mechanisms; their answers lack coherent step-by-step chemical reasoning.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry functional groups / Functional group reactions and synthesis
**Correct concept:** A correct solution requires recognizing the functional group, its typical reactivity, and using appropriate reagents and a logical mechanism to transform starting materials into products.
**Expected reasoning:** First identify the functional groups present and the target functional group. Then determine the relevant reaction type and choose suitable reagents/conditions. Finally, write a coherent stepwise mechanism that accounts for bond changes and product formation.
**Concept category:** functional groups
**Prevalence:** 164/177 (92.65%) could not give appropriate and right answers on the functional-groups synthesis question
**Population:** 177 first year Chemistry students
**Source:** ASSESSMENT OF UNDERGRADUATE CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' DIFFICULTIES IN ORGANIC CHEMISTRY (AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor/AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor.md)
**Evidence:** "most of the students' answers showed lack of organization (coherence), and also without algorithms or step by step problem solving approach."

## Misconception 56: Students determine the R/S sequence incorrectly by tracing priorities from 1 to 2 to 3 instead of orienting the lowest-priority group to the rear and tracing 1 to 2 to 4.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / R/S configuration assignment
**Correct concept:** R/S assignment uses Cahn-Ingold-Prelog priority rules: with the lowest-priority group pointing away, the 1→2→3 direction determines R or S; if the lowest-priority group is not away, the molecule must be reoriented or corrected by interchange rules.
**Expected reasoning:** Assign priorities to the substituents, place the lowest priority group behind the stereocenter, then trace the sequence 1→2→3 to determine the configuration according to CIP rules.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry / CIP sequence rules
**Prevalence:** 158/177 (89.26%) had difficulty visualizing and addressing the spatial orientation of the molecule
**Population:** 177 first year Chemistry students
**Source:** ASSESSMENT OF UNDERGRADUATE CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' DIFFICULTIES IN ORGANIC CHEMISTRY (AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor/AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor.md)
**Evidence:** "majority of students started from 1 and moved to 2 through 3 rather than starting from 1 and move to 2 through 4."

## Misconception 57: Students cannot reliably identify axial and equatorial positions or decide the most stable chair conformation of cis- and trans-disubstituted cyclohexanes.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Conformational analysis of disubstituted cyclohexanes
**Correct concept:** Conformation stability depends on whether substituents occupy axial or equatorial positions; the most stable conformation minimizes steric strain, especially placing bulky groups equatorial when possible.
**Expected reasoning:** Draw both chair conformations, locate axial and equatorial positions, then compare steric interactions. Choose the conformation with the least strain and explain stability in terms of substituent placement.
**Concept category:** conformational analysis
**Prevalence:** 152/177 (85.87%) failed to give the appropriate structural formula for the most stable conformation
**Population:** 177 first year Chemistry students
**Source:** ASSESSMENT OF UNDERGRADUATE CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' DIFFICULTIES IN ORGANIC CHEMISTRY (AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor/AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor.md)
**Evidence:** "Majority of students have difficulty in identify the axial and equatorial positions for different conformations of di-substituted cyclohexane"

## Misconception 58: Students do not correctly visualize stereoisomers in three dimensions or relate 2-D drawings to 3-D molecular structures.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Three-dimensional visualization of stereoisomers
**Correct concept:** Stereoisomers have the same connectivity but different three-dimensional arrangements, and students must mentally represent and compare those spatial structures.
**Expected reasoning:** Interpret the 2-D representation as a 3-D object, identify how substituents are oriented in space, and use that spatial model to compare or classify stereoisomers.
**Concept category:** three-dimensional visualization
**Population:** undergraduate Chemistry students / 177 first year Chemistry students
**Source:** ASSESSMENT OF UNDERGRADUATE CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' DIFFICULTIES IN ORGANIC CHEMISTRY (AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor/AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor.md)
**Evidence:** "many students have difficulties in visualizing structures of stereoisomers in three-dimensional forms; in relating three-dimensional with two-dimensional shapes"

## Misconception 59: Students cannot correctly classify organic reactions as substitution, elimination, addition, or rearrangement, nor write the corresponding mechanism.

**Topic:** Organic reactions and mechanisms / Reaction-type classification and mechanism writing
**Correct concept:** Students should identify the reaction type from the substrates, reagents, and conditions, then use the appropriate mechanistic pathway to explain product formation.
**Expected reasoning:** Examine the reactant structure and reaction conditions, determine whether the change is substitution, elimination, addition, or rearrangement, and then draw the mechanism consistent with that reaction class.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanisms
**Prevalence:** Only 11/177 (6.21%) gave appropriate answer; 104/177 (58.75%) scored 1 or less out of 3
**Population:** 177 first year Chemistry students
**Source:** ASSESSMENT OF UNDERGRADUATE CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' DIFFICULTIES IN ORGANIC CHEMISTRY (AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor/AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor.md)
**Evidence:** "students have difficulty in giving appropriate answers in organic reaction and mechanisms topics."

## Misconception 60: Students cannot correctly explain the dehydration of 3,3-dimethylbutan-2-ol to 2,3-dimethylbut-2-ene, including the need to write the correct formula, conditions, and elimination mechanism.

**Topic:** Organic reactions and mechanisms / Elimination mechanism for alcohol dehydration
**Correct concept:** Under acidic conditions, dehydration of the alcohol proceeds by the appropriate elimination mechanism to yield the alkene product, with correct structural representation and mechanistic steps.
**Expected reasoning:** Write the alcohol structure correctly, identify the reaction as acid-catalyzed dehydration, then show the elimination mechanism leading to the stated alkene product under the given conditions.
**Concept category:** elimination reactions
**Population:** 177 first year Chemistry students
**Source:** ASSESSMENT OF UNDERGRADUATE CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' DIFFICULTIES IN ORGANIC CHEMISTRY (AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor/AyalewTemesgenEtichaCEOchonogor.md)
**Evidence:** "Dehydration of 3, 3-dimethylbutan-2-ol results in the formation of 2, 3 dimethylbut-2-ene as the major product. Write the equation and show the mechanism of reaction clearly."

## Misconception 61: Students view reaction coordinate diagrams as static pictures rather than representations of molecular motion and change along the reaction path.

**Topic:** Energy profiles and reaction coordinates / Interpretation of reaction coordinate diagrams
**Correct concept:** Reaction coordinate diagrams represent the energetic progression of a reaction as molecular structures change dynamically from reactants to products.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should interpret the horizontal axis as reaction progress and the vertical axis as energy, recognizing that points on the diagram correspond to changing structures and energies along the pathway, not fixed isolated objects.
**Concept category:** reaction coordinate diagrams
**Population:** students
**Source:** Energy Profiles and Transition States in Organic Reactions: A Computational Study (Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025/Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "Many students incorrectly view reaction coordinate diagrams as static rather than dynamic representations of molecular motion"

## Misconception 62: Students misinterpret reaction coordinate diagrams and thereby confuse activation energy with reaction spontaneity.

**Topic:** Energy profiles and reaction coordinates / Activation energy and spontaneity
**Correct concept:** Activation energy is the energy barrier that must be overcome for a reaction to proceed; spontaneity is determined by thermodynamic factors, not by the size of the activation barrier alone.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish kinetic concepts from thermodynamic ones: a reaction can have a high or low activation energy regardless of whether it is spontaneous, and spontaneity depends on free-energy change.
**Concept category:** activation energy
**Population:** students
**Source:** Energy Profiles and Transition States in Organic Reactions: A Computational Study (Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025/Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "leading to misinterpretation of activation energy and reaction spontaneity"

## Misconception 63: Students confuse intermediates with transition states.

**Topic:** Transition states versus intermediates / Stationary points on the potential energy surface
**Correct concept:** Intermediates are local minima on the potential energy surface, whereas transition states are maxima corresponding to transient configurations.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify intermediates as relatively stable species that may exist between steps and transition states as the highest-energy points that connect minima; a transition state cannot be isolated as a stable species.
**Concept category:** potential energy surface
**Population:** students
**Source:** Energy Profiles and Transition States in Organic Reactions: A Computational Study (Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025/Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "Another common error involves confusing intermediates with transition states"

## Misconception 64: Students treat transition states as if they were ordinary species that can be isolated like intermediates.

**Topic:** Transition states versus intermediates / Isolation and stability of transition states
**Correct concept:** Transition states are fleeting, high-energy configurations and are not isolable under normal conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason that a species at a maximum on the reaction coordinate is not a stable minimum; therefore it cannot be bottled or isolated, only inferred computationally or indirectly.
**Concept category:** transition state theory
**Population:** students
**Source:** Energy Profiles and Transition States in Organic Reactions: A Computational Study (Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025/Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "the latter are energy maxima representing transient configurations that cannot be isolated"

## Misconception 65: Students assume computed energy minima and maxima are static structures rather than dynamic states influenced by vibrational and entropic effects.

**Topic:** Potential energy surfaces / Energy minima and maxima
**Correct concept:** Minima and maxima on a potential energy surface are approximations to structures along a reaction pathway and real systems also involve vibrational motion and entropy.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect the PES to molecular motion: a minimum corresponds to a stable structure, but the actual molecule still vibrates and its behavior is affected by entropy and temperature.
**Concept category:** potential energy surface
**Population:** students and practitioners
**Source:** Energy Profiles and Transition States in Organic Reactions: A Computational Study (Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025/Energy_Profiles_Transition_States_Organic_Reactions_Computational_Study_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "many assume that computed energy minima and maxima represent static structures"

## Misconception 66: Students treat functional groups as isolated entities and ignore the influence of the rest of the molecule on reactivity and selectivity.

**Topic:** Functional group transformations / Functional groups as isolated units
**Correct concept:** Functional groups behave as part of an interdependent electronic system whose reactivity depends on the molecular context.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that substituents, electronic effects, steric effects, and the surrounding molecular framework alter electron density and therefore change how a functional group reacts.
**Concept category:** electronic effects / molecular context
**Population:** students
**Source:** Functional Group Transformations in Organic Chemistry: Reactivity and Selectivity (Functional_Group_Transformations_Organic_Chemistry_Reactivity_Selectivity_Salah_2025/Functional_Group_Transformations_Organic_Chemistry_Reactivity_Selectivity_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “Many learners mistakenly interpret functional groups as isolated entities rather than as interdependent electronic systems influenced by molecular context.”

## Misconception 67: Students confuse kinetic control with thermodynamic control when predicting products in reversible reactions.

**Topic:** Reaction control / Kinetic vs thermodynamic control
**Correct concept:** Kinetic control favors the fastest-forming product, while thermodynamic control favors the most stable product under equilibrium conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify whether the reaction is under kinetic or thermodynamic conditions by considering temperature, reversibility, and equilibration, then predict the appropriate product accordingly.
**Concept category:** kinetic vs thermodynamic control
**Population:** students
**Source:** Functional Group Transformations in Organic Chemistry: Reactivity and Selectivity (Functional_Group_Transformations_Organic_Chemistry_Reactivity_Selectivity_Salah_2025/Functional_Group_Transformations_Organic_Chemistry_Reactivity_Selectivity_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “Another common error lies in confusing kinetic and thermodynamic control, particularly when predicting product ratios in reversible reactions.”

## Misconception 68: Students conflate SN1 and SN2 mechanisms.

**Topic:** Mechanistic classification / SN1 and SN2 mechanisms
**Correct concept:** SN1 and SN2 are distinct substitution mechanisms with different rate laws, intermediates, stereochemical outcomes, and substrate preferences.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the substrate structure, nucleophile strength, solvent, and stereochemical evidence to determine whether a reaction proceeds by SN1 or SN2.
**Concept category:** mechanistic models
**Population:** students
**Source:** Functional Group Transformations in Organic Chemistry: Reactivity and Selectivity (Functional_Group_Transformations_Organic_Chemistry_Reactivity_Selectivity_Salah_2025/Functional_Group_Transformations_Organic_Chemistry_Reactivity_Selectivity_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “Misapplication of mechanistic models—such as conflating SN1 with SN2 processes...”

## Misconception 69: Students misunderstand electrophilic addition mechanisms.

**Topic:** Mechanistic classification / Electrophilic addition mechanisms
**Correct concept:** Electrophilic addition follows specific mechanistic steps involving attack by an electrophile on a π system and subsequent formation of intermediates/products according to the substrate and conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should analyze the π bond, identify the electrophile and nucleophile roles, and trace electron movement step by step rather than memorizing the reaction name only.
**Concept category:** mechanistic models
**Population:** students
**Source:** Functional Group Transformations in Organic Chemistry: Reactivity and Selectivity (Functional_Group_Transformations_Organic_Chemistry_Reactivity_Selectivity_Salah_2025/Functional_Group_Transformations_Organic_Chemistry_Reactivity_Selectivity_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “Misapplication of mechanistic models—such as conflating SN1 with SN2 processes or misunderstanding electrophilic addition mechanisms—further complicates comprehension.”

## Misconception 70: Students think the halide leaves first to form a carbocation intermediate, leading them to apply an SN1-like pathway in an acyl substitution reaction.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic acyl substitution / Leaving group departure vs carbocation formation
**Correct concept:** In nucleophilic acyl substitution, the nucleophile attacks the acyl carbon to form a tetrahedral intermediate; the leaving group is expelled from that intermediate, not by prior carbocation formation.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize the carbonyl carbon as the electrophile, add the nucleophile to form the tetrahedral intermediate, then collapse the intermediate to expel the leaving group. Do not generate a carbocation at the acyl carbon.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanism / leaving group departure
**Prevalence:** 26.9% of students who answered October 19th incorrectly; 91 students made an SN1-type conceptual error
**Population:** 524 students total; 432 analyzed for October 19th question
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "26.9% of them expelled the bromo-halide group first which created a highly unstable carbocation in intermediate 1."

## Misconception 71: Students fail to dissociate the anionic nucleophile salt (e.g., NaO2CCH3 or NaOCH3) before using it in the mechanism.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic acyl substitution / Using salt nucleophile without dissociation
**Correct concept:** A salt nucleophile should be recognized as dissociated in solution, with the reactive anion acting as the nucleophile and sodium as a spectator ion.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify the salt as an ionic compound in solution, separate it into ions, and use the anion as the nucleophile while leaving out the counterion from the electron-pushing steps.
**Concept category:** ionic dissociation / nucleophile source
**Prevalence:** 21.7% on October 19th; 23.4% on October 21st failed to dissociate the nucleophile
**Population:** October 19th: students who completed the activity; October 21st: students who completed the activity
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "About 21.7% of students who completed the activity did not recognize that NaO2CCH3 was a salt that could easily be dissociated in an aqueous solution."

## Misconception 72: Students do not use the acid catalyst in the mechanism, implying they do not recognize that the reaction is acid-catalyzed or do not connect the given conditions to the mechanism.

**Topic:** Acid-catalyzed esterification / nucleophilic acyl substitution / Neglecting acid catalyst role
**Correct concept:** Under acidic conditions, the mechanism must include protonation steps that activate the electrophile and facilitate subsequent substitution steps.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the H+ condition to protonate the appropriate oxygen or carbonyl site first, then proceed with nucleophilic attack and later deprotonation steps.
**Concept category:** acid catalysis / electrophile activation
**Prevalence:** 56.0% of students who completed October 26th made this error
**Population:** October 26th CCP question; 459 student assignments analyzed
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "The most frequent mistake with this mechanism was failure to use the acid catalyst when carrying out the reaction."

## Misconception 73: Students protonate the wrong oxygen in the initial protonation step, often the hydroxyl group farther from the carbonyl or the carboxylic-acid hydroxyl group.
