**Topic:** Acid-catalyzed intramolecular substitution / Choosing the wrong protonation site
**Correct concept:** The correct protonation site is the oxygen that best activates the electrophile for the intramolecular substitution pathway shown by the mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** Inspect the functional groups and choose the oxygen whose protonation makes the best leaving group and leads to the intended intramolecular cyclization/substitution pathway.
**Concept category:** acid catalysis / protonation site selection
**Prevalence:** 18.6% protonated the wrong oxygen; among those, 68.0% protonated the hydroxyl farther from the carbonyl and 31.9% protonated the carboxylic-acid hydroxyl
**Population:** October 28th CCP question; 388 students analyzed
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "The most frequent conceptual error made by students in this question was during the initial protonation step where students had difficulty in choosing the correct protonation site."

## Misconception 74: After protonating the wrong hydroxyl, students either let that protonated hydroxyl leave without nucleophilic attack or perform nucleophilic attack on the incorrectly protonated intermediate.

**Topic:** Acid-catalyzed intramolecular substitution / Dehydration or attack after incorrect protonation
**Correct concept:** Once the correct site is protonated, the mechanism should proceed through the intended nucleophilic attack and subsequent elimination steps; dehydration should not be shown as a substitute pathway here.
**Expected reasoning:** First protonate the correct oxygen to create the right leaving group, then follow the specific intramolecular substitution sequence rather than ejecting water prematurely or attacking the wrong intermediate.
**Concept category:** mechanism branching / leaving group behavior
**Prevalence:** About one third chose the nucleophilic attack route and another third chose the dehydration route among those making the protonation-site error
**Population:** Students who made the protonation-site error on October 28th
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "The most common erroneous mechanistic path that students took after that point was either one of two routes: they performed a nucleophilic attack which expels either a protonated or nonprotonated hydroxyl group ... or made the protonated hydroxyl group leave without a nucleophilic attack (dehydration)."

## Misconception 75: Students eliminate the amine group without simultaneously forming the carbonyl C=O bond, treating these as separate rather than concerted events.

**Topic:** Imine hydrolysis / Order of elimination in tetrahedral intermediate collapse
**Correct concept:** When the tetrahedral intermediate collapses, the C=O bond reforms as the leaving group departs in the same step.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize the collapse of the tetrahedral intermediate as a coupled electron-flow step: electrons from oxygen form the carbonyl while the amine leaves at the same time.
**Concept category:** tetrahedral intermediate collapse / elimination
**Prevalence:** Close to 15%
**Population:** November 9th CCP question; 450 students analyzed
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "Close to 15% of students eliminated the amine group without simultaneous formation of the C-O double bond."

## Misconception 76: Students deprotonate the hydroxyl group of the intermediate before the elimination step instead of collapsing the intermediate first.

**Topic:** Imine hydrolysis / Premature deprotonation before elimination
**Correct concept:** The hydroxyl-bearing intermediate must collapse appropriately to re-form the carbonyl before or as leaving-group departure occurs; simple deprotonation alone is not the correct next step.
**Expected reasoning:** Follow the mechanism sequence: activate the intermediate as needed, then allow electron flow to reform the C=O and expel the leaving group rather than stopping after deprotonation.
**Concept category:** proton transfer / step order in mechanism
**Prevalence:** About 10%
**Population:** November 9th CCP question; 450 students analyzed
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "about 10% of students simply just deprotonated the hydroxyl group of intermediate 3 before the elimination step"

## Misconception 77: Students do not indicate stereochemistry in the key intermediate and/or final product, treating the anomeric center as if stereochemistry does not matter.

**Topic:** Acetal/hemiacetal hydrolysis / Ignoring stereochemistry at the anomeric carbon
**Correct concept:** The anomeric carbon can be attacked from either face, so stereochemistry must be shown because it determines the axial/equatorial outcome and possible racemic products.
**Expected reasoning:** Track the 3D configuration at the anomeric center, indicate whether attack occurs from the front or back, and draw the corresponding stereochemical outcome in the intermediate and product.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry / carbohydrate mechanisms
**Prevalence:** 75.5% of students who obtained a wrong intermediate 4 lacked stereochemistry indication; 31.6% of those also had correct final product stereochemistry
**Population:** November 23rd CCP question; 443 students analyzed
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "One of the most common concepts lacking in this question was the indication of stereochemistry within intermediate 4 of the mechanism"

## Misconception 78: Students omit formal charges on intermediates, even when the charged species should be shown.

**Topic:** Acetal/hemiacetal hydrolysis / Not indicating formal charge on intermediates
**Correct concept:** Intermediates should display the correct formal charges wherever charge is present in the mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** After each protonation/deprotonation or bond-making/bond-breaking step, assign formal charges to atoms according to valence and electron count, and show them in the drawn intermediate.
**Concept category:** formal charge / intermediate representation
**Prevalence:** Trend observed across intermediates; exact percentage not given in text
**Population:** November 23rd CCP question; intermediates attempted by students
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "there was an increase in incidences of the lack of formal charges as the mechanism progresses"

## Misconception 79: Students draw an arrow starting from hydrogen or the wrong atom instead of from the oxygen lone pair/bond electrons during deprotonation.

**Topic:** Acetal/hemiacetal hydrolysis / Wrong source for deprotonation arrow
**Correct concept:** Electron-pushing arrows must start at the electron source, such as the O-H bond or an oxygen lone pair, and end at the atom or bond receiving electrons.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify where electrons are located, start the arrow at that bond or lone pair, and point it to the correct atom so the deprotonation step reflects actual electron movement.
**Concept category:** electron pushing / arrow notation
**Prevalence:** Frequency not quantified; described as a frequent error
**Population:** November 23rd CCP question
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "there was a 'wrong source, right end' point in a deprotonation step."

## Misconception 80: Students remove an incorrect α-hydrogen, producing an unlikely or unstable ring-containing product.

**Topic:** Aldol condensation / Deprotonating the wrong alpha-hydrogen
**Correct concept:** Base should remove the α-hydrogen that leads to the correct enolate and the most plausible aldol condensation product.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare the available α-hydrogens, choose the one that gives the reactive enolate/enol consistent with the product pathway, and avoid generating highly strained or implausible cyclic products.
**Concept category:** enolate formation / alpha-hydrogen selection
**Prevalence:** 17.7%
**Population:** November 30th CCP question; 417 students analyzed
**Source:** Analysis of Common Conceptual Errors of CCP Questions (Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf/Guo_Eunice_CHEM 415_Type_frequency_2012.pdf.md)
**Evidence:** "About 17.7% of students had deprotonated the wrong hydrogen, which usually lead to highly unlikely and unstable four and seven membered ring structures."

## Misconception 81: Students believe heteroatom incorporation always enhances reactivity or selectivity.

**Topic:** heteroatom substitution / overall effect on reactivity
**Correct concept:** Heteroatom substitution can either increase or decrease reactivity/selectivity depending on electronic, steric, and environmental factors.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the specific heteroatom, its position, and the reaction conditions, then consider inductive, resonance, and steric effects before predicting whether reactivity or selectivity will improve.
**Concept category:** general heteroatom effects
**Population:** students
**Source:** Heteroatom Substitutions in Organic Frameworks: Effects on Reactivity and Function (Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025/Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “A common misconception is that heteroatom incorporation always enhances reactivity or selectivity”

## Misconception 82: Students think electronegativity alone determines substituent effects.

**Topic:** heteroatom substitution / electronegativity
**Correct concept:** Substituent effects are determined by multiple factors, including resonance, hybridization, molecular environment, and electronegativity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should not rely only on electronegativity; they should analyze whether resonance donation/withdrawal, hybridization, and the local molecular context also influence the outcome.
**Concept category:** electronegativity and substituent effects
**Population:** students
**Source:** Heteroatom Substitutions in Organic Frameworks: Effects on Reactivity and Function (Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025/Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “One common error is assuming that electronegativity alone determines substituent effects”

## Misconception 83: Students misread orbital diagrams and overlook the role of lone pairs and d-orbitals in delocalization or conjugation.

**Topic:** heteroatom substitution / orbital interpretation
**Correct concept:** Orbital diagrams must be interpreted in terms of actual participation of lone pairs and available orbitals in delocalization/conjugation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify which orbitals can overlap and whether lone pairs are available for conjugation or delocalization, rather than assuming a diagram gives only static electron placement.
**Concept category:** orbital interactions
**Population:** students and even researchers
**Source:** Heteroatom Substitutions in Organic Frameworks: Effects on Reactivity and Function (Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025/Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “Students and even researchers may misinterpret orbital diagrams, overlooking how lone pairs and d-orbitals participate in delocalization or conjugation.”

## Misconception 84: Students apply inductive and mesomeric effects incorrectly, leading to wrong mechanistic predictions.

**Topic:** heteroatom substitution / inductive vs mesomeric effects
**Correct concept:** Inductive and mesomeric effects must be distinguished and applied according to the actual electronic structure and reaction context.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separately evaluate σ-withdrawal/donation (inductive) and π-resonance effects (mesomeric), then combine them to predict the mechanism and product outcome.
**Concept category:** inductive and resonance effects
**Population:** students
**Source:** Heteroatom Substitutions in Organic Frameworks: Effects on Reactivity and Function (Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025/Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “Misapplications of inductive versus mesomeric effects often lead to incorrect mechanistic predictions.”

## Misconception 85: Students treat molecular behavior as fixed by electronegativity values rather than as dynamic and context-dependent.

**Topic:** heteroatom substitution / electronic effects
**Correct concept:** Molecular behavior depends on multiple interacting parameters and changes with molecular environment and conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason dynamically, considering how resonance, sterics, solvent, and bonding environment modify electron distribution and reactivity beyond simple periodic trends.
**Concept category:** modeling of molecular behavior
**Population:** students
**Source:** Heteroatom Substitutions in Organic Frameworks: Effects on Reactivity and Function (Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025/Heteroatom_Substitutions_Organic_Frameworks_Effects_Reactivity_Function_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “teaching molecular behavior as a dynamic system governed by multiple interacting parameters rather than fixed electronegativity values.”

## Misconception 86: Students think a small KIE means the isotopic atom is not mechanistically involved in the reaction.

**Topic:** Kinetic isotope effects / Interpretation of KIE magnitude
**Correct concept:** A small KIE can still reflect mechanistic involvement; it may arise from competing equilibria, multiple parallel pathways, or indirect isotope effects rather than direct bond cleavage alone.
**Expected reasoning:** Interpret the measured KIE in the full mechanistic context. Consider whether the isotope is in a primary or secondary position, and whether equilibria, parallel pathways, or other factors are damping the observed effect before concluding about mechanism.
**Concept category:** interpretation of KIE values
**Population:** students / organic chemistry learners (general student misconception discussed in review)
**Source:** Kinetic Isotope Effects in Organic and Biological Reactions (Kinetic_Isotope_Effects_Organic_Biological_Reactions_Salah_2025/Kinetic_Isotope_Effects_Organic_Biological_Reactions_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “Another misconception involves equating small KIE values with the absence of mechanistic involvement, whereas they may reflect competing equilibria or multiple parallel pathways.”

## Misconception 87: Students think kinetic isotope effects only reflect bond-breaking events.

**Topic:** Kinetic isotope effects / Bond cleavage interpretation
**Correct concept:** KIEs can reflect bond breaking, but also vibrational coupling and environmental interactions that influence the transition state.
**Expected reasoning:** Use KIEs as probes of transition-state structure and reaction dynamics, not just bond cleavage. Evaluate how vibrational frequencies, coupling, and the reaction environment change upon isotopic substitution.
**Concept category:** transition-state effects
**Population:** students / organic chemistry learners (general student misconception discussed in review)
**Source:** Kinetic Isotope Effects in Organic and Biological Reactions (Kinetic_Isotope_Effects_Organic_Biological_Reactions_Salah_2025/Kinetic_Isotope_Effects_Organic_Biological_Reactions_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “A common misconception is that KIEs solely reflect bond-breaking events, while in reality, they also depend on complex vibrational coupling and environmental interactions that influence the transition-state structure.”

## Misconception 88: Students assume every isotope effect directly corresponds to bond cleavage involving the isotopic atom.

**Topic:** Kinetic isotope effects / Primary versus secondary isotope effects
**Correct concept:** Primary isotope effects occur when the isotopic atom is directly involved in bond breaking or bond forming; secondary isotope effects arise when substitution occurs at a site not directly involved in the rate-determining step but still changes the vibrational/electronic environment.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify whether the isotope is directly in the bond-changing step. If not, consider secondary effects from neighboring vibrations or electronic redistribution instead of treating every KIE as a primary effect.
**Concept category:** primary vs secondary isotope effects
**Population:** students / organic chemistry learners (general student misconception discussed in review)
**Source:** Kinetic Isotope Effects in Organic and Biological Reactions (Kinetic_Isotope_Effects_Organic_Biological_Reactions_Salah_2025/Kinetic_Isotope_Effects_Organic_Biological_Reactions_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “A frequent error arises from assuming that all isotope effects directly correspond to bond cleavage involving the isotopic atom, neglecting secondary effects or vibrational coupling.”

## Misconception 89: Students interpret hybrid orbitals as physical entities that actually exist as objects in the atom.

**Topic:** Hybridization / Nature of hybrid orbitals
**Correct concept:** Hybrid orbitals are a model or mathematical construct used to describe bonding and electron distribution, not directly observable physical objects.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should treat hybridization as a descriptive framework: use it to rationalize geometry and bonding patterns, while recognizing that the underlying quantum description involves orbitals/wavefunctions and electron density rather than literal hybrid objects.
**Concept category:** hybridization model
**Population:** chemistry students
**Source:** Molecular Geometry and Hybridization:  Predicting Reactivity and Stability (Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025/Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "A common error involves interpreting hybrid orbitals as physical entities rather than mathematical constructs that describe electron distribution."

## Misconception 90: Students assume hybridization is fixed for a given atom and does not change with bonding context.

**Topic:** Hybridization / Context dependence of hybridization
**Correct concept:** Hybridization is context-dependent and can change depending on the bonding environment and molecular structure.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should examine the local bonding situation, electron domains, and molecular environment before assigning hybridization, rather than assuming one atom has one permanent hybridization state.
**Concept category:** hybridization model
**Population:** chemistry students
**Source:** Molecular Geometry and Hybridization:  Predicting Reactivity and Stability (Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025/Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "Many learners also assume that hybridization remains fixed for a given atom, ignoring the fact that hybridization adapts dynamically depending on bonding context."

## Misconception 91: Students rely on idealized VSEPR geometries and ignore factors that distort actual bond angles.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry / Idealized VSEPR geometry
**Correct concept:** Real molecular geometry can deviate from ideal VSEPR predictions because of electronic delocalization, steric strain, and non-bonded interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first predict the ideal electron-pair geometry with VSEPR, then consider additional effects such as lone-pair repulsion, conjugation, steric strain, and delocalization to explain deviations in measured bond angles.
**Concept category:** VSEPR / molecular geometry
**Population:** chemistry students
**Source:** Molecular Geometry and Hybridization:  Predicting Reactivity and Stability (Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025/Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "Another frequent misconception is overreliance on idealized geometries from VSEPR without considering electronic delocalization, steric strain, or non-bonded interactions that modify actual bond angles."

## Misconception 92: Students fail to connect changes in hybridization with transition-state geometry when analyzing mechanisms.

**Topic:** Hybridization and reaction mechanisms / Geometry change during reactions
**Correct concept:** Hybridization changes are linked to reaction-center geometry and can help explain transition states, reactivity, and selectivity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should track the geometry of the reacting atom(s) across the mechanism, recognizing that changes in bonding and orbital arrangement influence the transition state and therefore the reaction outcome.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reasoning
**Population:** chemistry students
**Source:** Molecular Geometry and Hybridization:  Predicting Reactivity and Stability (Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025/Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "In reaction mechanisms, students often fail to correlate hybridization changes with transition-state geometry, missing key insights into reactivity and selectivity."

## Misconception 93: Students think orbitals combine mechanically rather than through quantum-mechanical wavefunction mixing.

**Topic:** Bonding theory / Orbital mixing in textbook diagrams
**Correct concept:** Orbital hybridization is a quantum-mechanical mixing of wavefunctions, not a literal mechanical joining of orbitals.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should understand orbital mixing as a mathematical quantum process and use diagrams as representations of electron probability distributions, not as physical assembly instructions.
**Concept category:** orbital theory
**Population:** chemistry students
**Source:** Molecular Geometry and Hybridization:  Predicting Reactivity and Stability (Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025/Molecular_Geometry_Hybridization_Predicting_Reactivity_Stability_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "Simplified diagrams in textbooks may reinforce the notion that orbitals combine mechanically rather than quantum-mechanically through wavefunction mixing."

## Misconception 94: Students believe nucleophile strength controls the rate of an SN1 reaction.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / SN1 reaction rate and nucleophile role
**Correct concept:** SN1 rate is primarily determined by the ionization step that forms the carbocation, not by nucleophile strength.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the rate-determining step in SN1 as carbocation formation after leaving-group departure. Because the nucleophile attacks after the slow step, nucleophile strength does not control the SN1 rate in the same way it does for SN2.
**Concept category:** nucleophile role
**Prevalence:** 47%
**Population:** 30 third-year undergraduate chemistry students; percentage based on students showing the misconception at least once (N=30)
**Source:** Diagnosing Misconceptions in SN1 and SN2 Mechanisms Using a Five-Tier Diagnostic Test (FTDAT): Implications for Curriculum Improvement (NAJAH_~1/NAJAH_~1.md)
**Evidence:** many students assumed that nucleophile strength controls the SN1 rate, although the SN1 rate is primarily determined by the ionization (carbocation-forming) step.

## Misconception 95: Students treat activation-energy peaks in reaction energy diagrams as a general sign of reaction difficulty rather than as indicators of mechanistic steps.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Energy profiles and mechanistic steps
**Correct concept:** Energy diagrams should be interpreted to distinguish mechanistic pathways, such as stepwise SN1 versus concerted SN2, and to connect peaks to specific steps and intermediates.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should read the energy diagram by linking each peak and valley to bond breaking/forming events, intermediates, and the number of steps. This allows them to tell whether the pathway is stepwise or concerted rather than just judging how 'difficult' the reaction looks.
**Concept category:** energy profile
**Prevalence:** 45%
**Population:** 30 third-year undergraduate chemistry students; percentage based on students showing the misconception at least once (N=30)
**Source:** Diagnosing Misconceptions in SN1 and SN2 Mechanisms Using a Five-Tier Diagnostic Test (FTDAT): Implications for Curriculum Improvement (NAJAH_~1/NAJAH_~1.md)
**Evidence:** many students treated activation-energy peaks as indicators of general "difficulty" rather than using the diagram to distinguish mechanistic steps (stepwise SN1 versus concerted SN2).

## Misconception 96: Students believe solvent choice affects SN1 and SN2 reactions equally.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Solvent effects in SN1 and SN2
**Correct concept:** Solvent effects differ between SN1 and SN2 because solvent polarity and proticity stabilize intermediates and transition states differently in each mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare how a solvent influences carbocation/intermediate stabilization in SN1 versus nucleophile availability and transition-state stabilization in SN2. The mechanism selected depends on which species are stabilized or hindered by the solvent.
**Concept category:** solvent effects
**Prevalence:** 41%
**Population:** 30 third-year undergraduate chemistry students; percentage based on students showing the misconception at least once (N=30)
**Source:** Diagnosing Misconceptions in SN1 and SN2 Mechanisms Using a Five-Tier Diagnostic Test (FTDAT): Implications for Curriculum Improvement (NAJAH_~1/NAJAH_~1.md)
**Evidence:** students often believing that solvent choice influences SN1 and SN2 reactions equally.

## Misconception 97: Students assume primary substrates favor SN1.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Substrate effects and mechanism selection
**Correct concept:** Primary substrates generally favor SN2, while SN1 is favored by substrates that can form stable carbocations, typically tertiary substrates.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from carbocation stability and steric effects: if a substrate cannot form a stable carbocation, SN1 is unlikely, and if steric hindrance is low, SN2 is more feasible. Substrate structure must be matched to the mechanism’s requirements.
**Concept category:** substrate effects
**Prevalence:** 38%
**Population:** 30 third-year undergraduate chemistry students; percentage based on students showing the misconception at least once (N=30)
**Source:** Diagnosing Misconceptions in SN1 and SN2 Mechanisms Using a Five-Tier Diagnostic Test (FTDAT): Implications for Curriculum Improvement (NAJAH_~1/NAJAH_~1.md)
**Evidence:** students selected SN1 for primary substrates and overlooked the roles of carbocation stability and steric effects.

## Misconception 98: Students think reaction order is equal to the stoichiometric coefficients of the balanced equation.

**Topic:** Reaction kinetics / Rate law determination
**Correct concept:** Reaction order must be determined experimentally and does not generally match stoichiometric coefficients.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should infer order from kinetic data such as initial rates or integrated rate laws, not from the balanced chemical equation; stoichiometry shows amounts consumed, while rate law shows the mechanism-dependent dependence of rate on concentration.
**Concept category:** rate law order
**Population:** students and researchers
**Source:** Reaction Kinetics and Rate Laws in Organic Systems (Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025/Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "Many students and researchers mistakenly assume that reaction order equals stoichiometric coefficients, despite empirical determination being essential."

## Misconception 99: Students believe rate laws always have simple integer orders.

**Topic:** Reaction kinetics / Rate law form
**Correct concept:** Real reactions can show fractional, mixed, or otherwise complex rate dependences.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should consider that multi-step, parallel, or chain mechanisms can produce non-integer or complex kinetics, so observed data must be analyzed rather than forcing a simple integer-order model.
**Concept category:** rate law order
**Source:** Reaction Kinetics and Rate Laws in Organic Systems (Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025/Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "One major misconception involves assuming that rate laws always follow simple integer orders, whereas many reactions display fractional or complex dependencies due to parallel or chain mechanisms."

## Misconception 100: Students think linear Arrhenius behavior is universal for all organic reactions.

**Topic:** Reaction kinetics / Arrhenius behavior
**Correct concept:** Some reactions deviate from simple Arrhenius linearity because of multi-step mechanisms or complex transition states.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use Arrhenius plots as a model to test, but recognize deviations as evidence that the mechanism may not be elementary or may involve multiple steps or unusual transition-state behavior.
**Concept category:** temperature dependence
**Source:** Reaction Kinetics and Rate Laws in Organic Systems (Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025/Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "Another misconception is interpreting linear Arrhenius behavior as universal; some organic reactions exhibit deviations due to multi-step mechanisms or complex transition states."

## Misconception 101: Students misunderstand the steady-state approximation and apply it incorrectly when intermediate concentrations change substantially.

**Topic:** Reaction kinetics / Steady-state approximation
**Correct concept:** The steady-state approximation is only valid when intermediate concentrations remain approximately constant over the relevant time period.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check whether formation and consumption of intermediates balance so that their concentration changes little; if intermediates fluctuate significantly, the approximation is not valid and another kinetic treatment is needed.
**Concept category:** mechanism analysis
**Source:** Reaction Kinetics and Rate Laws in Organic Systems (Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025/Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "Additionally, misunderstanding the steady-state approximation can lead to incorrect mechanistic conclusions when intermediate concentrations fluctuate significantly."

## Misconception 102: Students oversimplify multi-step mechanisms into a single rate-determining step.

**Topic:** Reaction kinetics / Mechanistic simplification
**Correct concept:** Multi-step reactions may require considering several elementary steps and the full energetic landscape, not just one slow step.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should analyze the complete mechanism, including intermediates and possible parallel or consecutive steps, because rate behavior may be influenced by more than one step or by coupled processes.
**Concept category:** mechanism analysis
**Source:** Reaction Kinetics and Rate Laws in Organic Systems (Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025/Reaction_Kinetics_Rate_Laws_Organic_Systems_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** "Another limitation is the oversimplification of multi-step mechanisms into single ratedetermining steps, which can misrepresent the true energetic landscape."

## Misconception 103: Students believe SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms are inherently difficult, overly abstract, and hard to visualize mechanistically.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Overall perceived difficulty
**Correct concept:** SN1 and SN2 mechanisms can be understood by tracking electron flow, nucleophile/electrophile interactions, and bond changes.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect the reaction outcome to electron movement, identify reactive centers, and use mechanistic steps to explain how products form.
**Concept category:** general mechanistic understanding
**Prevalence:** 50% strongly agreed; 25% agreed
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "The concept of SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms is difficult for me."

## Misconception 104: Students cannot correctly identify nucleophiles and electrophiles, including which species attacks in a mechanism.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Identification of attacking species
**Correct concept:** Nucleophiles are electron-rich species that donate electron pairs; electrophiles are electron-poor species that accept electron pairs.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should inspect electron density, charges, lone pairs, and partial charges to determine which species donates electrons and which species accepts them.
**Concept category:** nucleophile/electrophile identification
**Population:** students (as reported by teachers and discussion)
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Students are confused in identifying electrophiles and nucleophiles before studying reaction mechanism."

## Misconception 105: Students confuse homolytic bond fission with heterolytic bond fission and do not track electron-pair movement correctly.

**Topic:** Bond fission / Homolytic vs heterolytic cleavage
**Correct concept:** Homolytic cleavage splits bonding electrons evenly; heterolytic cleavage gives both electrons to one fragment and is the bond-breaking pattern central to many SN1 processes.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should determine whether bond breaking produces radicals or ions and follow where the electrons go in the cleavage step.
**Concept category:** bond breaking / electron movement
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Students struggle to understand the differences between homolytic and heterolytic bond fission"

## Misconception 106: Students cannot write reaction mechanisms stepwise and have incomplete sequential mechanistic reasoning.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Writing mechanisms stepwise
**Correct concept:** A mechanism should be represented as an ordered sequence of elementary steps with correct electron movement for each step.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify each elementary step, place arrows correctly, and include intermediates and transition states in the right order.
**Concept category:** mechanistic representation / arrow pushing
**Prevalence:** 37.5% strongly agreed; 10% agreed
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Writing the reaction mechanism stepwise is also complicated for me."

## Misconception 107: Students do not understand how inductive, mesomeric, resonance, and related electronic effects influence reaction mechanisms.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Electronic effects on reactivity
**Correct concept:** Electronic effects such as inductive effects and resonance stabilize or destabilize intermediates and transition states and therefore affect mechanism and rate.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate how substituents and electron delocalization change the stability of intermediates such as carbocations and the feasibility of each pathway.
**Concept category:** stability and electronic effects
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "The factors that affect the reaction rate, like inductive, mesomeric, and electrometric effects of the reaction mechanism, are challenging to learn."

## Misconception 108: Students memorize mechanisms instead of connecting them to experimental observations, products, and rates.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Linking mechanisms to experiments
**Correct concept:** Mechanistic claims should be justified by experimental data such as kinetics, products, stereochemistry, and other observable outcomes.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use experimental evidence to support whether SN1 or SN2 occurs and explain the observed products and rates mechanistically.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reasoning and evidence
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "One of the students' most significant difficulties is linking reaction mechanisms with experiments."

## Misconception 109: Students struggle to predict the direction of nucleophilic attack and to evaluate product/intermediate stability correctly.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Direction of attack and product stability
**Correct concept:** The nucleophile attacks the electrophilic center from the geometrically and electronically appropriate direction, and stability should be assessed using carbocation stability, sterics, and stereochemical outcomes.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should determine the electrophilic site, consider steric accessibility and orbital alignment, and then predict the product and any stability effects.
**Concept category:** attack geometry and stability
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Students often face is understanding the less prior concept of the direction of attack and the stability of the products."

## Misconception 110: Students rely on rote memorization of reaction patterns rather than conceptual understanding of electron flow and mechanism.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Rote memorization
**Correct concept:** Students should reason from mechanistic principles, not merely memorize reaction templates.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain why a mechanism occurs by relating substrate structure, nucleophile strength, leaving-group behavior, and electron movement.
**Concept category:** memorization vs understanding
**Population:** student S5 and students generally
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Memorization and recall do not foster creative and critical thinking on reaction mechanisms. It is not conceptualized due to rote memorization."

## Misconception 111: Students cannot easily visualize molecular-level processes such as bond breaking/forming and transition states.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Molecular visualization
**Correct concept:** Students should construct accurate submicroscopic mental models or representations of how molecules change during a reaction.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use 3D and symbolic representations to track atoms, bonds, and electron movement through the reaction coordinate.
**Concept category:** visualization / submicroscopic representation
**Population:** teacher T4 describing students' difficulty
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "The content of the reaction mechanism is also abstract. It is not easily visualized."

## Misconception 112: Students misunderstand substitution and elimination reactions and may treat them as simultaneous or interchangeable.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Distinguishing substitution from elimination
**Correct concept:** Substitution and elimination are distinct reaction pathways with different mechanistic requirements and products.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify whether the nucleophile substitutes or the base removes a proton, and then classify the mechanism accordingly.
**Concept category:** reaction classification
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "This elimination and substitution reaction occurs simultaneously."

## Misconception 113: Students incorrectly believe SN2 reactions proceed through a carbocation intermediate.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / SN2 incorrectly treated as carbocation-based
**Correct concept:** SN2 is a concerted mechanism with simultaneous bond making and bond breaking and no carbocation intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should recognize that the nucleophile attacks as the leaving group departs in a single step, without a discrete intermediate.
**Concept category:** mechanism type and intermediates
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "SN2 reaction mechanisms do not proceed with the formation of a carbocation intermediate, but students were talking about the formation of carbocations and nucleophilic attack of carbocations in their explanations."

## Misconception 114: Students can use terms like carbocation, SN1, SN2, and leaving group without understanding their mechanistic meaning.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Chemical vocabulary without meaning
**Correct concept:** Chemical terms should be tied to electron movement, intermediates, and causal mechanistic roles.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain how each term fits into the mechanism, including why intermediates form and how electrons move.
**Concept category:** terminology vs mechanistic understanding
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Students' use of 'chemical vocabulary' did not translate into an understanding of electron movement and causal mechanistic explanation."

## Misconception 115: Students rely on memorized descriptions or surface features rather than mechanistic reasoning.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Surface features and memorization
**Correct concept:** Mechanistic classification should be based on electron flow, intermediates, and reaction conditions rather than superficial cues.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare substrate structure, nucleophile/base properties, solvent, and stereochemical consequences to decide mechanism.
**Concept category:** surface features
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students had memorized descriptions of the types of reaction mechanisms or simply relied on surface features to explain the mechanisms."

## Misconception 116: Students do not understand what curved arrows represent or how they indicate electron flow.

**Topic:** Electron-pushing formalism / Curved arrows
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows represent movement of electron pairs from electron-rich sources to electron-poor sinks.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should start arrows at electron sources such as lone pairs or bonds and end them at electron sinks to show bond formation or bond breaking.
**Concept category:** mechanistic arrows / electron flow
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students have not mastered the concepts of nucleophiles and electrophiles and therefore, could not successfully apply these concepts when drawing electron-pushing arrows."

## Misconception 117: Students cannot apply nucleophile/electrophile concepts mechanistically when drawing arrows or explaining reactions.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Mechanistic application
**Correct concept:** Nucleophile/electrophile identification should guide arrow pushing and mechanistic predictions.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify electron-rich and electron-poor regions, then use that information to justify attack direction and bond changes.
**Concept category:** nucleophile/electrophile identification
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students have not mastered the concepts of nucleophiles and electrophiles and therefore, could not successfully apply these concepts when drawing electron-pushing arrows."

## Misconception 118: Students can describe reaction steps but cannot explain why the reaction occurs.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Describing what happened versus why
**Correct concept:** Mechanistic explanations should include causal reasons such as electrostatic attraction, stability, and electronic effects.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should go beyond naming steps to explain the driving forces behind each step and why the pathway is favored.
**Concept category:** descriptive vs causal mechanistic reasoning
**Prevalence:** About 97% of students who gave a correct/partially correct description
**Population:** students who gave a correct/partially correct description
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "About 97 % of the students who gave a correct/partially correct description provided a description of what was happening in the reaction without any further explanation of why the reaction occurred."

## Misconception 119: Students can draw mechanisms or arrows without providing causal mechanistic reasoning.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Electron-pushing and causality
**Correct concept:** Correct arrows must be accompanied by an explanation of why electrons move, based on charge distribution and reactivity.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect arrow movement to electrophilicity, nucleophilicity, and stabilization of products or intermediates.
**Concept category:** causal mechanistic reasoning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "None of the explanations provided by the students was at level 3"

## Misconception 120: Students confuse SN1 and SN2 pathways and may draw SN2 mechanisms when SN1 is required.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / SN2 vs SN1 confusion
**Correct concept:** SN1 and SN2 are distinct mechanisms: SN1 proceeds via carbocation formation, whereas SN2 is concerted and does not involve a carbocation.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should inspect the presence or absence of carbocation intermediates and use substrate structure and reaction conditions to distinguish the pathways.
**Concept category:** mechanism discrimination
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students drew SN2 reaction mechanisms as responses to the questions involving SN1 mechanism."

## Misconception 121: Students only draw the first step of an SN1 mechanism and omit subsequent steps after carbocation formation.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution / Omitting later SN1 steps
**Correct concept:** An SN1 mechanism must include leaving-group departure, carbocation formation, nucleophilic attack, and any proton-transfer steps if applicable.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should continue the mechanism after ionization by showing nucleophilic attack on the carbocation and then any final deprotonation/protonation step.
**Concept category:** multistep mechanism
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "A lot of students only drew the mechanistic arrows for the first step of the SN1 reactions and omitted the arrows for the other steps."

## Misconception 122: Students default to SN2 reasoning and find SN1 mechanisms harder, indicating confusion about the distinctive features of SN1 versus SN2.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Speed of mechanisms
**Correct concept:** SN1 and SN2 differ in step count, intermediates, stereochemistry, and rate laws; these differences determine which mechanism applies.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate substrate class, nucleophile strength, solvent, and possible intermediates to decide whether SN1 or SN2 is more likely.
**Concept category:** mechanism comparison
**Population:** undergraduate chemistry students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Students were more familiar with SN2 reaction mechanisms and could answer questions on SN2 reaction mechanisms better than SN1 reaction mechanisms."

## Misconception 123: Students struggle to distinguish SN1 from SN2 because they see overlapping conditions and competing pathways as the same problem.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Distinguishing reaction classes
**Correct concept:** Students should distinguish the mechanistic features that separate SN1 from SN2 even when reagents or conditions overlap.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare whether the reaction is unimolecular or bimolecular, whether a carbocation forms, and whether the nucleophile attacks in a concerted step.
**Concept category:** reaction classification
**Population:** introductory organic chemistry students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "This is due to the concurrent nature of these reactions and their similarities in reagents and reaction conditions"

## Misconception 124: Students confuse nucleophilicity with basicity and may assume strong base means strong nucleophile or vice versa.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Nucleophilicity versus basicity
**Correct concept:** Nucleophilicity is a kinetic concept related to attack on an electrophile, while basicity is a thermodynamic concept related to proton acceptance.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should decide whether a reagent is acting as a nucleophile or a base by considering the reaction partner and the type of transformation, not just its strength.
**Concept category:** nucleophilicity/basicity
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Some students find the concept of nucleophilicity versus basicity confusing."

## Misconception 125: Students struggle to select the favored pathway when substitution and elimination reactions compete.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Predicting dominant pathway
**Correct concept:** Mechanism choice should be based on substrate structure, nucleophile/base strength, solvent, temperature, and steric factors.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare all plausible pathways and evaluate which one is favored under the given conditions.
**Concept category:** mechanism selection
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "When competing nucleophilic substitutions and elimination reactions are plausible, it can be difficult to select the favored reaction pathway."

## Misconception 126: Students have difficulty choosing between SN2 and E2 reactions even when the substrate and reagents suggest both are possible.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / SN2 versus E2 competition
**Correct concept:** SN2 and E2 must be distinguished by assessing sterics, nucleophilicity/basicity, and other conditions that favor one pathway over the other.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should consider whether the reagent behaves more as a nucleophile or base and whether steric hindrance makes substitution or elimination more likely.
**Concept category:** competing pathways
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "It can be especially difficult to choose between SN2 versus E2 reactions"

## Misconception 127: Students cannot distinguish SN1 from E1 when both proceed through a carbocation intermediate.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / SN1 versus E1 competition
**Correct concept:** SN1 and E1 share carbocation formation but differ in whether substitution or elimination occurs after that intermediate forms.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify the shared ionization step, then determine whether a nucleophile captures the carbocation or elimination occurs instead.
**Concept category:** competing pathways / carbocation intermediates
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "SN1 and E1 reactions proceed through a common first step and cationic intermediate."

## Misconception 128: Students struggle to understand stereochemical inversion in SN2 and racemization in SN1.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Stereochemical outcomes
**Correct concept:** SN2 gives inversion due to backside attack, whereas SN1 typically leads to racemization because the carbocation is planar.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect molecular geometry and attack direction to the stereochemical outcome of the reaction.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "backside attack by the nucleophile, producing stereochemical inversion"

## Misconception 129: Students struggle to visualize molecular-level rearrangements and three-dimensional geometry in substitution reactions.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / 3D visualization of molecules
**Correct concept:** Students should connect 2D drawings to 3D molecular geometry, including attack direction and stereochemical consequences.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should mentally rotate structures, track front-side versus backside approach, and map 3D changes onto the drawn mechanism.
**Concept category:** visualization / molecular geometry
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Students' difficulties and misconceptions in chemistry often stem from inadequate or inaccurate models at the molecular level."

## Misconception 130: Students struggle with SN1 racemization, SN2 inversion, and the geometry of nucleophilic attack.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Stereochemistry and attack direction
**Correct concept:** In SN1, a planar carbocation allows attack from either side and can give racemization; in SN2, backside attack causes inversion.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use the geometry of the intermediate or transition state to predict whether the product retains, inverts, or racemizes configuration.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "The carbocation is planar, and the anion can attack from either side."

## Misconception 131: Students struggle to understand backside attack in SN2 reactions.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Backside attack
**Correct concept:** SN2 requires nucleophilic attack from the side opposite the leaving group, producing inversion of configuration.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use orbital/geometry reasoning to place the nucleophile opposite the leaving group and predict the inverted product.
**Concept category:** attack geometry / stereochemistry
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "the nucleophile approaches from the left side of the central carbon as the other group leaves to the right."

## Misconception 132: Students struggle to understand relative reactivity trends for SN1 and SN2 reactions.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Reactivity trends
**Correct concept:** Reactivity depends on substrate structure, steric hindrance, carbocation stability, leaving-group ability, nucleophile strength, and solvent.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare these variables across substrates and conditions to predict which reaction is faster or more likely.
**Concept category:** reactivity trends
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students initially had difficulty understanding: relative reactivity, mechanistic differences, and reaction conditions."

## Misconception 133: Students struggle to connect energy diagrams, transition states, and mechanistic pathways in SN1 and SN2 reactions.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Energy profiles and reaction coordinates
**Correct concept:** SN1 has multiple energy barriers and a carbocation intermediate; SN2 has one concerted transition state and no intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should match each step of the mechanism with the corresponding barrier or valley on the reaction coordinate diagram.
**Concept category:** reaction coordinate / thermodynamics
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Students struggle to connect: energy profiles, transition states, and mechanistic pathways."

## Misconception 134: Students can solve routine or drill-like SN1/SN2 problems but lack deep conceptual understanding and struggle in abstract contexts.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Abstract transfer and procedural knowledge
**Correct concept:** Students should be able to transfer substitution concepts to new substrates, abstract reagents, and unfamiliar representations.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should apply mechanistic principles flexibly rather than relying only on memorized examples.
**Concept category:** transfer and abstraction
**Population:** students in the lab study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students could solve drill-like quiz problems using knowledge gained in the standard lecture−lab format."

## Misconception 135: Students can predict products more easily than they can explain how those products are formed.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Product prediction versus mechanism
**Correct concept:** Product prediction should be linked to a stepwise mechanistic explanation that justifies each bond change.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should show how the mechanism leads to the product through electron movement, intermediates, and stereochemical change.
**Concept category:** product prediction vs mechanism
**Population:** undergraduate organic chemistry students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students could predict the products of organic reactions, but struggled to provide reaction mechanisms for those same reactions"

## Misconception 136: Students do not understand carbocation stability in SN1 reactions well enough to use it for predicting reactivity.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Carbocation stability
**Correct concept:** More substituted carbocations are generally more stable and therefore favor SN1 pathways.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate substitution pattern and electronic stabilization to determine whether carbocation formation is favorable.
**Concept category:** carbocation stability
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "learn the trend for carbocation stability."

## Misconception 137: Students struggle to represent electron movement correctly using curved arrows.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Curved-arrow mechanisms
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows should indicate the movement of electron pairs from donor to acceptor throughout the mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should start arrows at electron sources and end them at the atoms or bonds that receive the electrons, consistent with each mechanistic step.
**Concept category:** mechanistic arrows / electron flow
**Population:** students in the activity
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "use curved arrows to draw a reaction mechanism."

## Misconception 138: Students struggle to identify incoming groups as nucleophiles and leaving groups in substitution reactions.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and leaving groups / Role identification
**Correct concept:** In substitution reactions, the nucleophile donates electrons to the electrophilic carbon and the leaving group departs with the bonding electron pair.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should examine which species attacks and which species leaves based on electron flow and bond polarity.
**Concept category:** reactant roles
**Population:** students in the laboratory study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "identify both the incoming group and the leaving group for each reaction."

## Misconception 139: Students struggle to understand inversion of stereochemistry in SN2 reactions.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry in SN2 / Inversion of stereochemistry
**Correct concept:** SN2 proceeds with backside attack and inversion of configuration at the reacting center.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should relate the backside approach to the three-dimensional arrangement of bonds and predict inversion at the stereocenter.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry
**Population:** students in the activity
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "inversion of stereochemistry"

## Misconception 140: Students memorize mechanisms, products, and reagent charts rather than reasoning from chemistry.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Mechanism versus product memorization
**Correct concept:** Students should use mechanistic principles to predict products and justify them with electron flow and structure-based reasoning.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should derive the product from the mechanism instead of recalling a chart or template.
**Concept category:** memorization / heuristic use
**Population:** student in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "I'm trying to remember what the chart says."

## Misconception 141: Students think SN1 reactions only cause inversion of stereochemistry, confusing SN1 with SN2 reasoning.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Stereochemistry of SN1
**Correct concept:** SN1 reactions proceed through a planar carbocation and usually lead to racemization, not simple inversion.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should recognize the planar carbocation intermediate and reason that nucleophilic attack can occur from either face.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "SN1 reactions only cause inversion of stereochemistry."

## Misconception 142: Students do not understand that SN2 is concerted and therefore may think it conserves stereochemistry.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Stereochemistry of SN2
**Correct concept:** SN2 is a one-step concerted reaction that produces inversion of configuration due to backside attack.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect simultaneous bond breaking/forming to inversion at the reaction center.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry / concerted mechanism
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "the concerted mechanism of SN2 reactions and its stereochemical implications"

## Misconception 143: Students memorize solvent associations instead of understanding why protic or aprotic solvents affect mechanisms.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Solvent effects
**Correct concept:** Solvent effects arise from stabilization of intermediates or nucleophiles and must be related to the mechanism being considered.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason about how solvent polarity and protic/aprotic character influence nucleophilicity, ionization, and pathway selection.
**Concept category:** reaction conditions
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "The presence of a protic solvent implies that a substitution reaction must be performed."

## Misconception 144: Students believe nucleophiles are defined simply by negative charge and electrophiles simply by positive charge.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Charge-based definitions
**Correct concept:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles are defined by electron-donating and electron-accepting behavior, which may or may not correlate with charge.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should look at electron density, lone pairs, orbital availability, and reactivity rather than charge alone.
**Concept category:** nucleophile/electrophile identification
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Nucleophiles donate electron pairs, they're negative, and electrophiles, positive."

## Misconception 145: Students prioritize structural cues like lone pairs or positive charges over actual electron-donating or electron-accepting function.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Structure over function
**Correct concept:** Nucleophile/electrophile identity depends on reaction function, not only visible structure.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should ask who donates electron density and who accepts it in the specific reaction context.
**Concept category:** surface features vs function
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students prioritize structure over function"

## Misconception 146: Students think any species with a lone pair is automatically a nucleophile and ignore other possible nucleophiles such as π bonds.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Lone pairs as the sole criterion
**Correct concept:** A nucleophile is any electron-rich species that can donate electron density, including lone pairs and π bonds depending on context.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should judge nucleophilicity from electron donation behavior in the reaction, not solely from the presence of lone pairs.
**Concept category:** nucleophile identification
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "the lone pair is the thing that makes you look at something and go, yeah, this is a nucleophile."

## Misconception 147: Students confuse nucleophiles with Brønsted bases and classify reactions by charge or deprotonation behavior instead of by electron-pair donation to an electrophile.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Confusing nucleophiles with Brønsted bases
**Correct concept:** Nucleophilicity and basicity are distinct: a nucleophile attacks an electrophile, whereas a base abstracts a proton.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should determine whether the reagent is attacking carbon or removing a proton and classify the reaction accordingly.
**Concept category:** nucleophilicity/basicity
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "failed to distinguish nucleophilic behavior from: Brønsted basic behavior."

## Misconception 148: Students use charges as shortcuts and create mechanisms mainly to generate charged species they can classify.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Charge-based shortcutting
**Correct concept:** Mechanistic reasoning should be based on chemically plausible electron movement and reaction feasibility, not on forcing charges to appear.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should decide whether a mechanism is possible, then use electron flow to explain it; charges are a consequence, not the goal.
**Concept category:** charge reasoning
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students merely used mechanisms to access charged species."

## Misconception 149: Students automatically treat any carbonyl carbon as electrophilic and ignore alternative reactivity such as acidic proton behavior.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Overgeneralizing carbonyl carbons as electrophiles
**Correct concept:** Electrophilicity depends on the specific reaction context; functional groups may participate in multiple reaction types.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should examine which atom is actually reactive in the given transformation instead of assuming the carbonyl carbon is always the target.
**Concept category:** electrophile identification
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students automatically assumed: any carbonyl carbon is electrophilic."

## Misconception 150: Students can state that the nucleophile attacks the electrophile but do not understand the causal reason for the attack.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Surface-level mechanism descriptions
**Correct concept:** The attack occurs because electron-rich regions are attracted to electron-poor regions, with bond changes driven by electron density and energetics.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain the electrostatic and electronic cause of attack rather than only narrating the sequence.
**Concept category:** descriptive vs causal reasoning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "The nucleophile attacks the electrophile which makes the leaving group leave."

## Misconception 151: Students draw SN1-like stepwise arrows for SN2, thinking leaving-group departure happens first and nucleophile attack happens second.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / SN2 arrow order
**Correct concept:** SN2 is concerted: nucleophilic attack and leaving-group departure occur in one coupled step, not as separate sequential steps.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should show simultaneous bond formation and bond breaking in the same elementary step.
**Concept category:** mechanism type and arrow-pushing
**Population:** students in the study
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students explained an SN1 reaction instead of an SN2"

## Misconception 152: Students do not consistently connect bond polarity and partial charges to SN2 reactivity.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Polarity and reactivity
**Correct concept:** Bond polarity and partial charges help identify the electrophilic center that is attacked in substitution reactions.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use δ+ and δ− information to locate where nucleophilic attack will occur and why.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / electrophilicity
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students do not consistently connect polarity to SN2 reactivity"

## Misconception 153: Students overuse formal charge reasoning and rarely reason with implicit electron density or electrostatic attraction.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Charges versus electron density
**Correct concept:** Reactivity depends on electron density, partial charges, electronegativity, and electrostatic attraction, not only formal charge.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should analyze how electrons are distributed and why electron-rich and electron-poor regions interact.
**Concept category:** electron density and charge
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students heavily rely on: formal charges, instead of: electron density distribution."

## Misconception 154: Students memorize mechanism pictures and reaction patterns without connecting them to chemical theory or why reactions occur.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Memorized mechanisms and surface features
**Correct concept:** Mechanistic drawings should reflect chemical theory, electron density, and causal reasoning.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain each line, arrow, and symbol in terms of electronic interactions and reaction logic.
**Concept category:** memorization / mechanistic representation
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "memorize the pictorial representations without connecting chemical theory to the lines, letters, and symbols"

## Misconception 155: Students explain reactions as if molecules 'want' stability or neutrality rather than as a consequence of electron movement and energetics.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Teleological reasoning
**Correct concept:** Reactions proceed because of energetic and electronic driving forces, not purpose or desire.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain the mechanism through electron flow, charge stabilization, and favorable energetics.
**Concept category:** causal reasoning / teleology
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "the compound wants to be neutral"

## Misconception 156: Students more easily identify nucleophiles than electrophiles and may fail to recognize the electron-deficient partner in a reaction.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Role imbalance
**Correct concept:** Both nucleophile and electrophile must be identified from their complementary electron-donor/electron-acceptor roles.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should determine which species donates electron density and which species accepts it before drawing the mechanism.
**Concept category:** nucleophile/electrophile identification
**Population:** students
