**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students identify nucleophiles more easily than electrophiles"

## Misconception 157: Students add curved arrows correctly in appearance but do not understand why electrons move.

**Topic:** Electron-pushing formalism / Arrows as decoration
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows should encode the causal movement of electrons between electron-rich and electron-poor sites.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain the reason for each arrow in terms of charge distribution, bond polarity, and reaction energetics.
**Concept category:** mechanistic arrows
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "as if decorating the structures"

## Misconception 158: Students treat proton-transfer steps as merely 'removing charge' or making products neutral rather than as acid-base steps driven by electron density.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Proton transfer meaning
**Correct concept:** Proton transfers are acid-base reactions in which a base donates a lone pair to a proton and the electron pair remains in the bond framework.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain proton transfer by identifying the base, the acidic proton, and the electron movement involved.
**Concept category:** acid-base / proton transfer
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "get rid of the charge" or "make the product neutral"

## Misconception 159: Students think success in mechanism problems depends on memorized patterns rather than flexible mechanistic analysis.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Mechanism choice by memorized patterns
**Correct concept:** Mechanism selection should be reasoned from substrate, reagent, solvent, and electronic factors in the specific problem.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate the actual reaction conditions and mechanistic plausibility instead of recalling a template.
**Concept category:** heuristic reasoning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students solve SN1/SN2 problems using: memorized heuristics, instead of: mechanistic analysis."

## Misconception 160: Students cannot reliably categorize reactions as SN1, SN2, E1, or E2.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / SN1/SN2/E1/E2 categorization
**Correct concept:** Students should classify reactions using mechanistic criteria such as intermediates, concertedness, substrate, and reagents.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare the evidence for substitution versus elimination and for unimolecular versus bimolecular pathways.
**Concept category:** reaction classification
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Categorizing the types of organic reactions as SN1, SN2, E1 and E2 mechanism"

## Misconception 161: Students struggle to write mechanisms for substitution reactions.

**Topic:** Substitution mechanisms / Writing mechanisms
**Correct concept:** Substitution mechanisms should be written as ordered electron-pushing steps with correct intermediates and products.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should represent each elementary step using curved arrows and identify all relevant species and bond changes.
**Concept category:** mechanistic representation
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Writing mechanism of reactions for substitution reactions"

## Misconception 162: Students do not have a strong background in visualizing three-dimensional molecular structures, which affects mechanistic understanding.

**Topic:** 3D molecular structure / Visualization
**Correct concept:** Students should be able to interpret and manipulate 3D structures to understand stereochemistry and reactivity.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should mentally rotate molecules and connect 2D drawings to 3D orientation, especially for SN2 backside attack.
**Concept category:** spatial reasoning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students usually have no good background in three-dimensional visualization"

## Misconception 163: Students struggle to navigate between 2D drawings and 3D structures.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Connecting 2D and 3D representations
**Correct concept:** Students should translate between symbolic structures and three-dimensional molecular geometry when reasoning about mechanisms.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use 2D drawings to infer 3D arrangement, stereochemistry, and attack orientation.
**Concept category:** representation translation
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "navigating between the two dimensional drawings ... and their three-dimensional structures"

## Misconception 164: Students memorize molecules and rules to fake understanding instead of developing conceptual reasoning.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Memorization of rules
**Correct concept:** Students should understand why reaction outcomes occur rather than relying on rote rules.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should integrate mechanism, structure, stereochemistry, and conditions to predict outcomes and explain them.
**Concept category:** memorization vs conceptual understanding
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students memorize a large vocabulary of molecules and rules to fake an understanding"

## Misconception 165: Students expect mechanisms to be solved by algorithms or fixed step-by-step procedures and struggle without them.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Algorithm expectation
**Correct concept:** Mechanism problems require conditional reasoning and flexible application, not just a fixed algorithm.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate the specific chemical context and choose the most plausible mechanistic pathway accordingly.
**Concept category:** problem solving / reasoning
**Population:** teachers reporting on students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "there are no algorithms (step by step problem solving) for solving Organic Chemistry exercises such as mechanisms"

## Misconception 166: Students can learn substitution concepts in familiar examples but fail to apply them in new situations.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Applying concepts in new situations
**Correct concept:** Mechanistic understanding should transfer to unfamiliar substrates and conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason from first principles about the mechanism rather than relying on memorized examples.
**Concept category:** transfer of learning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "fail to apply them in new situations while solving exercises."

## Misconception 167: Students confuse SN1 and SN2 when determining reactivity and apply the wrong substrate logic.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Model confusion
**Correct concept:** SN1 reactivity depends mainly on carbocation stability, while SN2 reactivity depends strongly on steric accessibility and backside attack.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish the two mechanisms by their rate-determining features and structural requirements.
**Concept category:** mechanism comparison
**Population:** fourth year chemistry students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Another explanation may be model confusion between SN1 and SN2 when determining reactivity."

## Misconception 168: Students incorrectly think the least hindered substrate is most reactive in SN1 because they apply SN2-like steric reasoning.

**Topic:** SN1 reaction reactivity / Least substituted is most reactive
**Correct concept:** SN1 reactivity is favored by greater carbocation stability, often increasing with substitution rather than decreasing with steric hindrance alone.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate the stability of the carbocation formed after leaving-group departure, not just steric accessibility.
**Concept category:** reactivity trends / model confusion
**Prevalence:** 7 students
**Population:** seven students among the interviewees
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "seven apparently confused SN2 and SN1 when they misidentified the least hindered substrate as the most reactive"

## Misconception 169: Students incorrectly apply steric accessibility or distance reasoning to SN1 reactions instead of carbocation stability reasoning.

**Topic:** SN1 reaction reactivity / Steric hindrance applied to SN1
**Correct concept:** SN1 reactivity is governed primarily by the stability of the carbocation intermediate and the ease of ionization.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should focus on whether the leaving group can depart to form a stable carbocation rather than on spatial distance from other groups.
**Concept category:** steric reasoning error
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "the farther away the Br is the more reactive it might be"

## Misconception 170: Students evaluate product stability rather than whether the mechanism is feasible.

**Topic:** SN1 reaction reactivity / Product stability instead of mechanism feasibility
**Correct concept:** Reaction outcomes should be predicted by mechanistic feasibility and intermediate stability, not only by how stable the product looks.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should ask whether the proposed pathway can occur and what intermediates are involved before judging the product.
**Concept category:** reactivity reasoning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Another common alternative conception in organic chemistry is the evaluation of the stability of products instead of the feasibility of the reaction mechanism."

## Misconception 171: Students treat curved arrows as physically meaningless symbols rather than as representations of electron movement.

**Topic:** Curved arrows / Physically meaningless symbols
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows represent the flow of electron pairs and should be interpreted mechanistically.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should trace the electron source and destination and explain the resulting bond changes.
**Concept category:** mechanistic arrows
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "the curved arrows were physically meaningless to the students."

## Misconception 172: Students focus on products and starting materials and disregard the 'hows' and 'whys' of the process.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / How and why versus what
**Correct concept:** Students should explain both the outcome and the mechanistic reasons behind it.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should link structural features to mechanistic steps and causal forces such as electron flow and stability.
**Concept category:** causal mechanistic reasoning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "generally disregarded the 'hows' and 'whys' of the process"

## Misconception 173: Students believe chemistry should have one correct answer and struggle when one starting material can give multiple products or pathways.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Multiple products and pathways
**Correct concept:** Many organic reactions have competing pathways and multiple plausible products depending on conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare all chemically reasonable outcomes and justify which pathway is favored under the given conditions.
**Concept category:** epistemological reasoning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Students found it difficult to accept that one starting compound treated with only one set of reagents could lead to more than one correct product."

## Misconception 174: Students struggle with the idea that multiple mechanistic pathways can exist for the same reaction.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Mechanistic ambiguity
**Correct concept:** Students should evaluate competing pathways and accept that reaction outcome depends on conditions and relative pathway viability.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare SN1 vs SN2 and E1 vs E2 possibilities rather than expecting a single deterministic answer.
**Concept category:** mechanism selection
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "multiple possibilities or multiple pathways could exist."

## Misconception 175: Students rely on memorized reagent-to-product patterns instead of mechanistic reasoning.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Memorized A→B patterns
**Correct concept:** Students should reason from mechanism, not from a memorized 'A yields B' pattern.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should determine which pathway is most viable and why, rather than recalling a fixed mapping.
**Concept category:** memorization vs reasoning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "A yields B, B yields C"

## Misconception 176: Students are uncomfortable deciding which mechanism is best or most viable among several possibilities.

**Topic:** Mechanistic decision-making / Choosing among pathways
**Correct concept:** Mechanism choice should be justified by comparing the viability of all plausible pathways under the given conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should assess substrate, reagent, solvent, and steric factors to select the most likely pathway.
**Concept category:** decision-making
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "discern the relative viability of these pathways."

## Misconception 177: Students expect memorization to be sufficient and find flexible mechanistic reasoning difficult.

**Topic:** SN1 and SN2 reaction mechanisms / Creativity in synthesis and mechanism
**Correct concept:** Organic chemistry problems require integrating multiple concepts creatively and flexibly.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should synthesize structure, conditions, and mechanism to reason through a problem rather than simply recall a rule.
**Concept category:** flexible reasoning
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "Having to put everything together and sort of use your creativity…is difficult."

## Misconception 178: Students struggle to differentiate SN1 and SN2 reactions.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Differentiating SN1 and SN2
**Correct concept:** SN1 and SN2 differ in rate law, intermediates, geometry, and conditions; these must be used to classify the mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should inspect substrate, nucleophile, solvent, and stereochemistry to determine whether SN1 or SN2 applies.
**Concept category:** mechanism classification
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "differentiate between SN1 and SN2 mechanisms of alcohols."

## Misconception 179: Students struggle to draw reasonable mechanisms for substitution/elimination reactions.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Mechanism drawing
**Correct concept:** Reasonable mechanisms should show proper electron flow, intermediates, and product formation.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use curved arrows and mechanistic steps to justify the product from the starting material and conditions.
**Concept category:** mechanistic representation
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "understand and draw reasonable mechanisms."

## Misconception 180: Students frequently employ rote memorization of flashcards and repeated structures instead of meaningful engagement with chemistry.

**Topic:** Reaction learning strategies / Rote memorization
**Correct concept:** Students should build conceptual and mechanistic understanding through active reasoning and discussion.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect concepts, not merely memorize forms and outcomes.
**Concept category:** learning strategy
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "students frequently employ rote memorization"

## Misconception 181: Students often fail to think critically or engage meaningfully with the chemistry in mechanism problems.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Critical thinking about mechanisms
**Correct concept:** Mechanistic problems should be approached through critical evaluation of structural and conditional factors.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should justify each mechanistic choice using chemistry principles rather than memorized rules.
**Concept category:** critical thinking
**Population:** students
**Source:** Challenges Faced by Teachers and Students in Teaching-Learning Reaction Mechanism (SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions/SN1_SN2_reactions_misconceptions.md)
**Evidence:** "often fail to think critically or otherwise to engage meaningfully with the chemistry at hand."

## Misconception 182: Students think rearranging equations is itself a conceptual topic they can solve only by memorizing procedures, and they struggle to manipulate equations as needed.

**Topic:** chemistry / equation manipulation
**Correct concept:** Equation rearrangement is a mathematical tool used to isolate the required variable and solve for unknowns in chemistry problems.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify the formula, determine which quantity is unknown, and algebraically rearrange the equation while preserving equality on both sides.
**Concept category:** mathematical manipulation
**Prevalence:** Reported by focus group students; also listed by staff as troublesome
**Population:** Forensic Science students in focus groups; academic staff teaching science at Nottingham Trent University
**Source:** Science Learning and Teaching Conference 2007 (Science_Learning_and_Teaching_Conference_2007_Titl/Science_Learning_and_Teaching_Conference_2007_Titl.md)
**Evidence:** "I agree with X about having the grasp of maths and rearranging equations, stuff like that, that would be good".

## Misconception 183: Students believe that in microscopy they only need the practical focusing procedure and do not need to understand the wave/particle nature of light.

**Topic:** physics / light and microscopy
**Correct concept:** Microscopy involves understanding the physical behavior of light, including its wave/particle properties, because these explain image formation and focusing.
**Expected reasoning:** Connect the operational adjustment of a microscope to the underlying optics of light and use the appropriate scientific model to explain why the instrument works.
**Concept category:** surface/practical procedure vs underlying concept
**Prevalence:** Reported by a student in focus groups
**Population:** Focus group student (Forensic Science programme)
**Source:** Science Learning and Teaching Conference 2007 (Science_Learning_and_Teaching_Conference_2007_Titl/Science_Learning_and_Teaching_Conference_2007_Titl.md)
**Evidence:** "you don't need to know that the light is behaving as a particle, all you need to know is that you have to adjust the thing to get it into focus"

## Misconception 184: Students believe they understand trends in the periodic table when in fact they do not perform well on questions about them.

**Topic:** chemistry / periodic table trends
**Correct concept:** Periodic table trends must be understood in terms of systematic changes across periods and down groups and applied accurately to questions.
**Expected reasoning:** Use atomic structure and periodic patterns to predict and explain trends rather than relying on familiarity or self-assessment of understanding.
**Concept category:** self-perception vs demonstrated understanding
**Prevalence:** Majority of students self-reported understanding, but this did not match test performance
**Population:** First year chemistry students (n=50)
**Source:** Science Learning and Teaching Conference 2007 (Science_Learning_and_Teaching_Conference_2007_Titl/Science_Learning_and_Teaching_Conference_2007_Titl.md)
**Evidence:** "The majority of students stated that they understood most aspects of Trends in Periodic Table, yet questions in this area produced the lowest scores of any in the test."

## Misconception 185: Students treat stereoelectronic effects as fixed rules that always apply the same way, regardless of reaction conditions or molecular environment.

**Topic:** Stereoelectronic effects / Context dependence of orbital effects
**Correct concept:** Stereoelectronic effects are context-dependent and can be influenced by solvent polarity, temperature, and conformational dynamics.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that orbital overlap and transition-state stabilization can change when molecular conformation or reaction environment changes, so the dominant effect must be evaluated for each specific system rather than applied as an absolute rule.
**Concept category:** misconceptions about stereoelectronic rules
**Population:** students and even practicing chemists
**Source:** Stereoelectronic Effects in Organic Chemistry: Influence on Mechanism and Selectivity (Stereoelectronic_Effects_Organic_Chemistry_Influence_Mechanism_Selectivity_Salah_2025/Stereoelectronic_Effects_Organic_Chemistry_Influence_Mechanism_Selectivity_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “students and even practicing chemists sometimes treat stereoelectronic rules as rigid rather than context-dependent”

## Misconception 186: Students assume reaction selectivity arises solely from spatial crowding or steric hindrance.

**Topic:** Stereoelectronic effects / Role of steric crowding versus orbital alignment
**Correct concept:** Reaction selectivity can be controlled by orbital alignment and stereoelectronic effects, not just steric crowding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should consider whether the reacting orbitals are properly aligned for overlap; if orbital alignment better explains the observed product distribution, then steric crowding alone is insufficient.
**Concept category:** orbital alignment vs steric effects
**Population:** students
**Source:** Stereoelectronic Effects in Organic Chemistry: Influence on Mechanism and Selectivity (Stereoelectronic_Effects_Organic_Chemistry_Influence_Mechanism_Selectivity_Salah_2025/Stereoelectronic_Effects_Organic_Chemistry_Influence_Mechanism_Selectivity_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “One frequent error is assuming that reaction selectivity arises solely from spatial crowding, when in reality, orbital alignment frequently dominates the outcome.”

## Misconception 187: Students confuse hyperconjugation and delocalization effects with classical resonance.

**Topic:** Stereoelectronic effects / Hyperconjugation and delocalization terminology
**Correct concept:** Hyperconjugation involves donation of electron density into antibonding orbitals, which is distinct from classical resonance.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between resonance, which is a delocalization description for π systems or lone pairs, and hyperconjugation, which involves orbital donation such as σ→σ* or n→σ* interactions.
**Concept category:** electron donation / hyperconjugation
**Population:** students and even practicing chemists
**Source:** Stereoelectronic Effects in Organic Chemistry: Influence on Mechanism and Selectivity (Stereoelectronic_Effects_Organic_Chemistry_Influence_Mechanism_Selectivity_Salah_2025/Stereoelectronic_Effects_Organic_Chemistry_Influence_Mechanism_Selectivity_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “the misuse of hyperconjugation and delocalization terminology, where effects due to electron donation into antibonding orbitals are confused with classical resonance”

## Misconception 188: Students struggle to visualize three-dimensional electron delocalization and phase interactions, leading to incomplete understanding of stereoelectronic effects.

**Topic:** Stereoelectronic effects / Visualization of orbital interactions
**Correct concept:** Orbital overlap, phase relationships, and electron delocalization must be represented and interpreted in three dimensions to understand stereoelectronic effects correctly.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use orbital diagrams or 3D models to check whether the donor and acceptor orbitals have the right orientation and phase for effective overlap, instead of relying only on two-dimensional structural formulas.
**Concept category:** orbital visualization
**Population:** students
**Source:** Stereoelectronic Effects in Organic Chemistry: Influence on Mechanism and Selectivity (Stereoelectronic_Effects_Organic_Chemistry_Influence_Mechanism_Selectivity_Salah_2025/Stereoelectronic_Effects_Organic_Chemistry_Influence_Mechanism_Selectivity_Salah_2025.md)
**Evidence:** “The lack of orbital visualization tools in education also contributes to difficulties in grasping three-dimensional electronic interactions.”

## Misconception 189: Students think elimination outcomes are determined only by steric hindrance, or they ignore the role of nucleophile/base strength in choosing the correct elimination reaction condition.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Elimination reactions
**Correct concept:** Correct elimination reasoning requires considering both steric hindrance and the base strength/nucleophilicity of the reagent when predicting whether elimination is favored.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the reaction conditions and identify how steric hindrance and base strength together influence the likelihood of elimination; stronger/bulkier bases and greater steric hindrance can favor elimination, but the correct mechanism choice depends on the full set of conditions.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanisms / elimination
**Population:** Students in the main and pilot studies (undergraduate chemistry students, N=55 pilot; N=357 main)
**Source:** The influence of representations on task difficulty in organic chemistry: an exploration using a novel paired-items test instrument (The influence of representations on task difficulty in organic chemistry  an exploration using a novel paired-items test instrument/The influence of representations on task difficulty in organic chemistry  an exploration using a novel paired-items test instrument.md)
**Evidence:** "The significance of the base strength of a nucleophile for an elimination is only considered for the solution with increased level of ability."

## Misconception 190: Students believe that under the cold-and-catalyst (ccc) rule, substitution occurs on the side chain rather than on the core.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Reaction selectivity rules for substitution reactions
**Correct concept:** The correct substitution selectivity must be applied to the appropriate part of the molecule; the side chain is not the site of substitution under the described conditions if the correct rule points to the core.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the functional site affected by the reaction conditions and apply the selectivity rule to the correct structural position rather than relying on an incorrect side-chain assumption.
**Concept category:** reaction selectivity / substitution reactions
**Population:** Students with the lowest understanding of the topic in the main study
**Source:** The influence of representations on task difficulty in organic chemistry: an exploration using a novel paired-items test instrument (The influence of representations on task difficulty in organic chemistry  an exploration using a novel paired-items test instrument/The influence of representations on task difficulty in organic chemistry  an exploration using a novel paired-items test instrument.md)
**Evidence:** "the answer choice B that states the misconception that the substitution occurs on the side chain and not on the core under the conditions of cold and catalyst (ccc rule)."

## Misconception 191: Students attribute unexpected lab results to faulty equipment or human error instead of interpreting the chemistry behind the result.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry laboratory learning / Explaining unexpected experimental results
**Correct concept:** Unexpected results should be explained by analyzing the chemical system, experimental design, and evidence, not assumed to be caused by error without support.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare observed data with their hypothesis, consider how the reaction conditions and mechanism explain the outcome, and only invoke equipment or human error when there is specific evidence for it.
**Concept category:** experimental reasoning
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** An S_N 1 - S_N 2 Lesson in an Organic Chemistry Lab Using a Studio-Based Approach (an-sn1-sn2-lesson-in-an-organic-chemistry-lab-using-a-studio-based-approach/an-sn1-sn2-lesson-in-an-organic-chemistry-lab-using-a-studio-based-approach.md)
**Evidence:** students become frustrated and attribute those differences to faulty equipment or some form of human error.

## Misconception 192: Students do not correctly draw or interpret reaction coordinate diagrams for SN2, E2, and SN1/E1 reactions.

**Topic:** Reaction coordinate diagrams / Generalized reaction coordinate diagrams
**Correct concept:** Reaction coordinate diagrams should represent reactants, transition states, intermediates if present, and products with the correct relative energies and number of steps for each mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify whether the mechanism is concerted or stepwise, place transition state(s) at energy maxima, include intermediates only for stepwise mechanisms, and show the correct relative energies for reactants and products based on the reaction profile.
**Concept category:** reaction coordinates
**Prevalence:** Preassessment correct response rate: 20.0%; postassessment: 73.7%
**Population:** N=20 preassessment, N=19 postassessment; junior/senior chemistry, medicinal chemistry, and biochemistry majors at two institutions
**Source:** Applying Density Functional Theory to Common Organic Mechanisms: A Computational Exercise (applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise/applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise.md)
**Evidence:** "Question 1. Draw the reaction coordinate diagram for a generalized S_N2 reaction."

## Misconception 193: Students do not correctly draw or interpret a reaction coordinate diagram for an E2 reaction.

**Topic:** Reaction coordinate diagrams / E2 reaction profile
**Correct concept:** An E2 reaction coordinate diagram should show a single concerted step with one transition state and no intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that E2 is concerted, so the diagram should contain one energy barrier and directly connect reactants to products through a single transition state.
**Concept category:** reaction coordinates
**Prevalence:** Preassessment correct response rate: 20.0%; postassessment: 73.7%
**Population:** N=20 preassessment, N=19 postassessment; junior/senior chemistry, medicinal chemistry, and biochemistry majors at two institutions
**Source:** Applying Density Functional Theory to Common Organic Mechanisms: A Computational Exercise (applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise/applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise.md)
**Evidence:** "Question 2. Draw the reaction coordinate diagram for a generalized E2 reaction."

## Misconception 194: Students do not correctly draw or interpret reaction coordinate diagrams for the core steps of SN1/E1 reactions.

**Topic:** Reaction coordinate diagrams / SN1/E1 reaction profile
**Correct concept:** SN1/E1 reaction coordinate diagrams should show a stepwise mechanism with at least two steps and a carbocation intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify carbocation formation as the first step, then nucleophilic substitution or elimination as the second step, and place an intermediate minimum between transition states.
**Concept category:** reaction coordinates
**Prevalence:** Preassessment correct response rate: 20.0%; postassessment: 68.4%
**Population:** N=20 preassessment, N=19 postassessment; junior/senior chemistry, medicinal chemistry, and biochemistry majors at two institutions
**Source:** Applying Density Functional Theory to Common Organic Mechanisms: A Computational Exercise (applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise/applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise.md)
**Evidence:** "Question 3. Draw the reaction coordinate diagram for the core steps of the generalized S_N1/E1 reactions."

## Misconception 195: Students cannot identify which mechanism a 3D transition-state model represents.

**Topic:** Transition-state recognition / Identifying mechanism from 3D transition-state structure
**Correct concept:** Students should distinguish transition states by their geometric features, such as backside attack for SN2 or partial bond breaking/forming patterns for E2, and match those features to the correct mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect the 3D structure, recognize the arrangement of bonds and atoms in the transition state, and map that arrangement to the mechanism it represents.
**Concept category:** transition states
**Prevalence:** Preassessment correct response rate: 30.0% (question 6) and 30.0% (question 7); postassessment: 68.4% (q6) and 89.5% (q7)
**Population:** N=20 preassessment, N=19 postassessment; junior/senior chemistry, medicinal chemistry, and biochemistry majors at two institutions
**Source:** Applying Density Functional Theory to Common Organic Mechanisms: A Computational Exercise (applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise/applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise.md)
**Evidence:** "The following figure is a 3-dimensional model of a transition state. This is an example of the transition state for what type of mechanism:"

## Misconception 196: Students do not correctly identify the most stable carbocation among simple alkyl carbocations.

**Topic:** Carbocation stability / Relative stability of carbocations
**Correct concept:** Carbocation stability increases with alkyl substitution; the tertiary carbocation is the most stable among the listed options.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare resonance/hyperconjugation and inductive stabilization from alkyl groups, concluding that the tertiary carbocation is most stable.
**Concept category:** carbocation stability
**Prevalence:** Preassessment correct response rate: 75.0%; postassessment: 78.9%
**Population:** N=20 preassessment, N=19 postassessment; junior/senior chemistry, medicinal chemistry, and biochemistry majors at two institutions
**Source:** Applying Density Functional Theory to Common Organic Mechanisms: A Computational Exercise (applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise/applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise.md)
**Evidence:** "Which is the most stable carbo-cation? a) CH_3^+ b) CH_2CH_2^+ c) CH(CH_3)_2^+ d) C(CH_3)_3^+ e) I don't know"

## Misconception 197: Students do not correctly determine which tert-butyl halide is more likely to undergo SN1 or E1 based on leaving-group ability.

**Topic:** Leaving group ability and reactivity / SN1/E1 substrate leaving-group effects
**Correct concept:** Better leaving groups promote SN1/E1 reactions more readily; iodide is a better leaving group than bromide, chloride, or fluoride.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should rank leaving groups by their ability to depart and then choose the substrate with the best leaving group as most likely to undergo SN1/E1.
**Concept category:** leaving group ability
**Prevalence:** Preassessment correct response rate: 30.0%; postassessment: 31.5%
**Population:** N=20 preassessment, N=19 postassessment; junior/senior chemistry, medicinal chemistry, and biochemistry majors at two institutions
**Source:** Applying Density Functional Theory to Common Organic Mechanisms: A Computational Exercise (applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise/applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise.md)
**Evidence:** "Which is more likely to undergo an S_N1 or E1 reaction? a) C(CH_3)_3C1 b) C(CH_3)_3F c) C(CH_3)_3Br d) C(CH_3)_3I e) I don't know"

## Misconception 198: Students cannot correctly write out the steps of an E2 reaction mechanism, including intermediates and transition states where appropriate.

**Topic:** Mechanism writing / E2 stepwise mechanism representation
**Correct concept:** E2 is a concerted elimination mechanism with a single transition state and no intermediates.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should depict simultaneous proton abstraction and leaving-group departure in one step, not separate intermediates.
**Concept category:** mechanism writing
**Prevalence:** Preassessment correct response rate: 25.0%; postassessment: 73.7%
**Population:** N=20 preassessment, N=19 postassessment; junior/senior chemistry, medicinal chemistry, and biochemistry majors at two institutions
**Source:** Applying Density Functional Theory to Common Organic Mechanisms: A Computational Exercise (applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise/applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise.md)
**Evidence:** "Given that the following reaction proceeds via an E2 mechanism, draw out the reaction mechanism. Include a structure for each intermediate and transition state if applicable:"

## Misconception 199: Students cannot correctly write out the steps of an SN2 reaction mechanism, including intermediates and transition states where appropriate.

**Topic:** Mechanism writing / SN2 stepwise mechanism representation
**Correct concept:** SN2 is a concerted substitution mechanism with one transition state and no intermediates.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should show backside attack and leaving-group departure occurring simultaneously in a single elementary step.
**Concept category:** mechanism writing
**Prevalence:** Preassessment correct response rate: 25.0%; postassessment: 78.9%
**Population:** N=20 preassessment, N=19 postassessment; junior/senior chemistry, medicinal chemistry, and biochemistry majors at two institutions
**Source:** Applying Density Functional Theory to Common Organic Mechanisms: A Computational Exercise (applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise/applying-density-functional-theory-to-common-organic-mechanisms-a-computational-exercise.md)
**Evidence:** "Given the following reaction proceeds via a Sn2 mechanism, draw out the reaction mechanism. Include a structure for each intermediate and transition state if applicable:"

## Misconception 200: Students explain the reaction as S_N1 (leaving group departs first to form a carbocation, then nucleophile bonds) when the reaction shown is S_N2.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / S_N1 vs S_N2 reaction pathway
**Correct concept:** The depicted reaction proceeds by a concerted S_N2 mechanism, with nucleophile attack and leaving-group departure occurring in the same step.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that hydroxide attacks the methyl carbon while bromide leaves simultaneously; there is no carbocation intermediate in the canonical mechanism shown.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanism / S_N1 vs S_N2
**Prevalence:** At Year 2—Time Point 1, 30% of Traditional students gave an S_N1 explanation and drew S_N1-like arrows; 7% of OCLUE students did so.
**Population:** Traditional OC and OCLUE organic chemistry students
**Source:** Arrows on the Page Are Not a Good Gauge: Evidence for the Importance of Causal Mechanistic Explanations about Nucleophilic Substitution in Organic Chemistry (arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about/arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about.md)
**Evidence:** "The bromine leaves and then the oxygen comes in and bonds."

## Misconception 201: Students draw curved arrows after deciding the product or as a decorative/confirmatory step rather than using arrows to reason from electron source to sink.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Electron flow as after-the-fact decoration
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows should represent the electron-pushing mechanism and be used to predict how the reaction occurs and the products formed.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should start from the electron source (lone pair on hydroxide) and follow electron movement to the electrophilic carbon, then from the C-Br bond to bromine, using arrows to reason mechanistically about product formation.
**Concept category:** electron-pushing formalism / arrow use
**Prevalence:** In a cited prior study, only about 50% of students used mechanistic arrows to predict products, and 20% of those who did drew the arrows after predicting a product.
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (prior study cited in this paper)
**Source:** Arrows on the Page Are Not a Good Gauge: Evidence for the Importance of Causal Mechanistic Explanations about Nucleophilic Substitution in Organic Chemistry (arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about/arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about.md)
**Evidence:** "students are drawing arrows to 'get them to the product' or 'decorating with arrows' after drawing a memorized product."

## Misconception 202: Students rely on memorized patterns or surface features rather than electrostatic attraction and electron movement to explain reactivity.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Surface-feature / memorized-pattern reasoning
**Correct concept:** Mechanistic reasoning should be based on structure-property relationships, electrostatic attraction between electron-rich and electron-deficient regions, and explicit electron movement.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that OH− is nucleophilic because it is electron-rich, the carbon in CH3Br is electrophilic because bromine withdraws electron density, and these features drive the substitution mechanism.
**Concept category:** pattern recognition / surface features
**Population:** Graduate students (cited prior study) and organic chemistry students broadly
**Source:** Arrows on the Page Are Not a Good Gauge: Evidence for the Importance of Causal Mechanistic Explanations about Nucleophilic Substitution in Organic Chemistry (arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about/arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about.md)
**Evidence:** "some students struggled to explain their mechanistic arrow use in terms of electrostatic attractions and often resorted to memorized patterns to draw the mechanism"

## Misconception 203: Students can describe electron movement in the mechanism without explaining why the electrons move that way (i.e., they give mechanistic but not causal reasoning).

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Ignoring electrostatic cause while giving electron-pushing steps
**Correct concept:** A complete explanation should include both electron movement and the electrostatic cause for that movement.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain that the carbon is partially positive because bromine withdraws electron density, which attracts hydroxide and causes electron flow that forms the new bond and breaks the C-Br bond.
**Concept category:** causal mechanistic reasoning / electrostatic attraction
**Population:** Organic chemistry students
**Source:** Arrows on the Page Are Not a Good Gauge: Evidence for the Importance of Causal Mechanistic Explanations about Nucleophilic Substitution in Organic Chemistry (arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about/arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about.md)
**Evidence:** "organic chemistry students tended to provide explanations ... in which they discussed electron movement but did not include a discussion of electrostatic attraction for the reaction—that is, why the electrons move in this way."

## Misconception 204: Students think that using the words 'nucleophile' and 'electrophile' shows they understand the mechanism, even when their reasoning lacks causal/mechanistic support.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Using nucleophile/electrophile vocabulary without understanding
**Correct concept:** Correct vocabulary must be paired with reasoning about electrostatic attraction and electron movement to demonstrate understanding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the labels nucleophile and electrophile to actual electronic structure: the nucleophile is electron-rich, the electrophile is electron-poor, and this drives bond formation and bond breaking.
**Concept category:** terminology without reasoning
**Prevalence:** For Year 2–OCLUE, 72% of noncausal mechanistic responses invoked terminology such as nucleophile and electrophile but did not demonstrate understanding of electrostatics or explicit electron movement.
**Population:** Year 2 OCLUE organic chemistry students
**Source:** Arrows on the Page Are Not a Good Gauge: Evidence for the Importance of Causal Mechanistic Explanations about Nucleophilic Substitution in Organic Chemistry (arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about/arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about.md)
**Evidence:** "the use of organic chemistry terminology such as nucleophile and electrophile is not necessarily accompanied by appropriate causal mechanistic reasoning."

## Misconception 205: Students draw the arrow from the C-Br bond to bromine first, implying the reaction is initiated by bond cleavage rather than nucleophile attack.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Misinterpreting arrow order as if bond cleavage initiates S_N2
**Correct concept:** For the canonical S_N2 mechanism, the nucleophile’s lone pair is the initial electron source; bond making and bond breaking occur concertedly.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should begin the mechanism with the oxygen lone pair attacking carbon and then show the C-Br bond electrons moving to bromine, reflecting simultaneous substitution.
**Concept category:** arrow-drawing / electron source-sink reasoning
**Prevalence:** At Year 2—Time Point 1, 17% of OCLUE students and 14% of Traditional students gave an S_N2 explanation but S_N1-like arrows; at Year 2—Time Point 2, 2% of OCLUE and 5% of Traditional students did so.
**Population:** OCLUE and Traditional organic chemistry students
**Source:** Arrows on the Page Are Not a Good Gauge: Evidence for the Importance of Causal Mechanistic Explanations about Nucleophilic Substitution in Organic Chemistry (arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about/arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about.md)
**Evidence:** "drawing the arrow from the carbon-bromine bond first indicates that the reaction is initiated by breaking of the carbon-bromine bond rather than the approach of the nucleophile"

## Misconception 206: Students conflate electronegativity or bond polarity with formal charges, or treat the carbon as positively charged in a simplistic way without distinguishing partial from formal charge.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Confusing partial positive charge with formal positive charge / over-reliance on electronegativity wording
**Correct concept:** The carbon in methyl bromide is only partially positive due to polarization of the C-Br bond; this is an electron-density explanation, not a formal cation unless a true carbocation forms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that bromine withdraws electron density from carbon through bond polarity, leaving carbon electron-poor but not formally cationic in S_N2.
**Concept category:** bond polarity / charge concepts
**Population:** Organic chemistry students
**Source:** Arrows on the Page Are Not a Good Gauge: Evidence for the Importance of Causal Mechanistic Explanations about Nucleophilic Substitution in Organic Chemistry (arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about/arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about.md)
**Evidence:** "students responses did include incorrect ideas and incorrect use of vocabulary such as conflating the terms electronegative and formal negative charge."

## Misconception 207: Students explain the reaction by saying the products are formed because they are 'more stable' or because the products 'want' to form, rather than by causal interaction of reactants.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Explaining reaction progress teleologically
**Correct concept:** The reaction should be explained by reactant interactions at the molecular level, especially electrostatic attraction and electron movement, not by teleological language.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain that the reactants interact because OH− is attracted to an electron-poor carbon and this electron transfer causes substitution; product stability alone does not cause the mechanism.
**Concept category:** teleological reasoning
**Population:** Organic chemistry students
**Source:** Arrows on the Page Are Not a Good Gauge: Evidence for the Importance of Causal Mechanistic Explanations about Nucleophilic Substitution in Organic Chemistry (arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about/arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about.md)
**Evidence:** "might activate reasons for why the products are more stable or other teleological reasons for why the products 'want' to form."

## Misconception 208: Some students describe a proton-transfer or other non-substitution process instead of nucleophilic substitution for the given reaction.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Explaining the wrong reaction type as a substitution mechanism
**Correct concept:** The reaction shown is a nucleophilic substitution of methyl bromide by hydroxide, not a proton transfer reaction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the reacting species and show hydroxide attacking carbon, bromide leaving, and C-O bond formation in the substitution mechanism.
**Concept category:** reaction identification / non-normative process
**Population:** Organic chemistry students
**Source:** Arrows on the Page Are Not a Good Gauge: Evidence for the Importance of Causal Mechanistic Explanations about Nucleophilic Substitution in Organic Chemistry (arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about/arrows-on-the-page-are-not-a-good-gauge-evidence-for-the-importance-of-causal-mechanistic-explanations-about.md)
**Evidence:** "Emmy's response 'the oxygen accepted a proton and formed O–H bond and Br leaves' was explaining a process other than a nucleophilic substitution."

## Misconception 209: Students do not grasp or incorrectly handle concepts that involve the molecular level because they are hard to visualize, and they struggle with topics involving electron behavior.

**Topic:** Chemistry threshold concepts / Molecular-level and electron behavior concepts
**Correct concept:** Chemical phenomena often need to be understood at the molecular and electron level using abstract models, not only macroscopic observation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect observable chemistry to particle-level models, use representations of molecules/electrons, and reason about how electron behavior explains chemical processes.
**Concept category:** threshold concepts / troublesome knowledge
**Population:** students, science teachers, and academic staff in Moss et al. (2007) identified problematic topics
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "topics which are involving the molecular level and so, are difficult to be visualized by students, are problematic, as well as anything which involves the behavior of electrons"

## Misconception 210: Students find chemistry topics involving equations or statistics troublesome, indicating difficulty applying quantitative tools in chemistry.

**Topic:** Chemistry threshold concepts / Equation and statistics use in chemistry
**Correct concept:** Chemical problems may require correct use of equations and statistics to model, calculate, and interpret data.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should select the relevant equation or statistical method, apply it to chemical data, and interpret the result in the chemistry context.
**Concept category:** threshold concepts / troublesome knowledge
**Population:** students, science teachers, and academic staff in Moss et al. (2007) identified problematic topics
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "the topics in Chemistry which involve equations or the utilization of statistics are also troublesome"

## Misconception 211: Students have alternative mental models of atomicity instead of the legitimized scientific model.

**Topic:** Atomicity / Model of atomicity
**Correct concept:** Atomicity should be understood according to the accepted scientific model of atoms and their behavior in matter.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare observations and representations to the scientific atomic model and revise naive or alternative models when they conflict with evidence.
**Concept category:** alternative mental models
**Population:** students in Park et al. (2009)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "students have alternative mental models of atomicity"

## Misconception 212: Students hold misconceptions about reaction mechanisms in organic chemistry.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Reaction mechanisms
**Correct concept:** Reaction mechanisms should be understood as stepwise electron-flow processes that explain how products form.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should trace electron movement with curved arrows, identify intermediates and bond changes, and justify each mechanistic step.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are 'reaction mechanisms"

## Misconception 213: Students hold misconceptions about acid-base chemistry in organic chemistry.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Acid-base chemistry
**Correct concept:** Acid-base behavior should be reasoned from proton transfer, acidity/basicity, and molecular structure effects.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the acid and base, track proton transfer, and relate stability and structure to reactivity.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are 'reaction mechanisms, acid-base chemistry"

## Misconception 214: Students hold misconceptions about synthesis in organic chemistry.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Synthesis
**Correct concept:** Synthesis requires planning a sequence of reactions based on functional group transformations and mechanism knowledge.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should work backward from the target molecule, choose reagents consistent with the needed transformations, and check feasibility of each step.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are 'reaction mechanisms, acid-base chemistry, synthesis"

## Misconception 215: Students hold misconceptions about stereochemistry in organic chemistry.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Stereochemistry
**Correct concept:** Stereochemistry concerns three-dimensional molecular arrangement and its effect on properties and reactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use spatial reasoning to distinguish stereoisomers and predict stereochemical outcomes from structure and mechanism.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are 'reaction mechanisms, acid-base chemistry, synthesis, stereochemistry"

## Misconception 216: Students hold misconceptions about resonance and electron delocalization.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Resonance / electron delocalization
**Correct concept:** Resonance represents delocalized electron structure and is used to describe bonding and stability, not a literal switching of structures.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should draw valid resonance contributors, keep atom positions fixed, and infer the delocalized electron distribution that best explains stability and reactivity.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are ... resonance (electron delocalization)"

## Misconception 217: Students hold misconceptions about molecular orbital theory.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Molecular orbital theory
**Correct concept:** Molecular orbital theory explains bonding and electron distribution through molecular orbitals rather than localized bonds alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should combine atomic orbitals into molecular orbitals, fill them according to electron configuration, and use them to explain bonding or antibonding effects.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are ... molecular orbital theory"

## Misconception 218: Students hold misconceptions about spectroscopy.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Spectroscopy
**Correct concept:** Spectroscopy is interpreted by linking spectral features to molecular structure and transitions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should match peaks or signals to structural features and explain how the spectrum provides evidence for a molecular structure.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are ... spectroscopy"

## Misconception 219: Students hold misconceptions about polarity.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Polarity
**Correct concept:** Polarity should be understood as an uneven charge distribution arising from bond polarity and molecular geometry.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should assess electronegativity differences and molecular shape to determine the net dipole and resulting interactions.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are ... polarity"

## Misconception 220: Students hold misconceptions about SN1 reactions.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / SN1 reactions
**Correct concept:** SN1 reactions proceed by a unimolecular rate-determining step via a carbocation intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize conditions favoring carbocation formation, predict rate dependence on substrate, and follow the stepwise mechanism.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are ... SN1, SN2, E1, E2 reactions"

## Misconception 221: Students hold misconceptions about SN2 reactions.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / SN2 reactions
**Correct concept:** SN2 reactions proceed through a single concerted substitution step with backside attack and inversion of configuration.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify nucleophile strength, substrate sterics, and the one-step mechanism to predict products and stereochemistry.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are ... SN1, SN2, E1, E2 reactions"

## Misconception 222: Students hold misconceptions about E1 reactions.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / E1 reactions
**Correct concept:** E1 reactions proceed by a two-step elimination via a carbocation intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should assess substrate stability, carbocation formation, and elimination conditions to determine whether E1 is likely.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are ... SN1, SN2, E1, E2 reactions"

## Misconception 223: Students hold misconceptions about E2 reactions.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / E2 reactions
**Correct concept:** E2 reactions proceed by a concerted elimination requiring a strong base and suitable anti-periplanar geometry.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate base strength, substrate structure, and stereochemical alignment to predict an E2 reaction.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are ... SN1, SN2, E1, E2 reactions"

## Misconception 224: Students hold misconceptions about curved-arrow formalism.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Curved-arrow formalism
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows represent electron movement and must follow correct electron-pushing conventions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should draw arrows from electron sources to electron sinks, preserving atom valence and mechanistic logic.
**Concept category:** organic chemistry misconceptions
**Population:** organic chemistry educators describing student misconceptions in Duis (2011)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Amongst the difficult topics of organic chemistry, are ... curved-arrow formalism"

## Misconception 225: Students identify inductive effects as a troublesome concept and likely misunderstand how electron-withdrawing or donating groups influence molecules.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Inductive effects
**Correct concept:** Inductive effects are the transmission of electron density through sigma bonds and must be used to explain reactivity and stability.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should analyze electronegativity and bond connectivity to determine how substituents alter electron density through the sigma framework.
**Concept category:** electron transfer / substituent effects
**Population:** first year Chemistry students in Moss et al. (2007)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "Some of the threshold concepts identified in the study of Moss (2007) by first year Chemistry students were SN1, SN2 reactions, inductive effects"

## Misconception 226: Students incorrectly understand topics involving the behavior of electrons, treating them as problematic rather than using them as the basis for explanations in chemistry.

**Topic:** Chemistry / Behaviour of electrons
**Correct concept:** Electron behavior should be used to explain bonding, reactivity, and molecular changes.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from electron movement, electron distribution, and electronic structure to explain chemical outcomes.
**Concept category:** electron behavior
**Population:** first year Chemistry students in Moss et al. (2007)
**Source:** THRESHOLD CONCEPTS APPROACH TO CURRICULUM DEVELOPMENT: A MISSING PIECE IN CHEMISTRY STUDENTS' RETENTION PUZZLE? (article_5_2_8/article_5_2_8.md)
**Evidence:** "anything which involves the behaviour of electrons"

## Misconception 227: Students think that reacting 1-chloropropane with sodium hydroxide produces a metal-containing product or that water must be added for the reaction to produce alcohol.

**Topic:** Organohalides / Nucleophilic substitution product prediction
**Correct concept:** A nucleophilic substitution of 1-chloropropane with hydroxide ion produces propan-1-ol and chloride ion as the leaving group/by-product.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify OH- as the nucleophile and Cl- as the leaving group; then predict substitution of Cl by OH on the carbon chain to form the corresponding alcohol, without introducing a metal product or extra water as a reactant.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reactions / nucleophilic substitution
**Prevalence:** 20 out of 33; also described as a significant number of PSCTs
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "I thought one of the products would be a metal or something"

## Misconception 228: Students think the substitution reaction used to synthesize propane-1-thiol is misconstrued; their responses indicated confusion about substitution reactions and the introduced functional group.

**Topic:** Organohalides / Nucleophilic substitution synthesis
**Correct concept:** Propane-1-thiol can be synthesized by SN2 substitution of a suitable propyl halide with SH- to replace the halogen with an -SH group.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that the thiol product comes from replacing the halogen in the alkyl halide with sulfur nucleophile SH-, preserving the three-carbon chain and introducing the -SH functional group.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reactions / substitution reactions
**Prevalence:** 21 out of 33
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "Figure 8 illustrates that PSCT8 had a misconception. This response and other similar responses by PSCTs indicated their misconceptions of substitution reactions."

## Misconception 229: Students do not understand the one-step SN2 mechanism and misconstrue the underlying mechanistic reaction, including the backside attack and inversion of configuration.

**Topic:** Organohalides / SN2 mechanism
**Correct concept:** SN2 occurs in a single concerted step with backside attack, a transition state with partial bond formation/breaking, and inversion of stereochemistry at the reacting carbon.
**Expected reasoning:** Track the nucleophile approaching from 180 degrees opposite the leaving group, note simultaneous bond formation and bond breaking in one transition state, and infer inversion at the stereocenter.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reactions / SN2
**Prevalence:** 23 out of 33
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "misconstrued the underlying mechanistic reaction"

## Misconception 230: Students give inaccurate answers because they do not grasp that organohalides react with magnesium in ether to form Grignard reagents, and they misjudge the chemistry of the resulting reagent.

**Topic:** Organohalides / Grignard reagent formation and base strength
**Correct concept:** An alkyl halide reacts with magnesium in dry ether to form a Grignard reagent (RMgX), which is a very strong base/nucleophile and must be handled under anhydrous conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify Mg insertion into the C-X bond, recognize RMgX as an organomagnesium reagent, and infer its strong basic/reactive nature from the polarized carbon-magnesium bond.
**Concept category:** organometallics / Grignard reagents
**Prevalence:** Most participants; the question remained unanswered by most, with only a limited number attempting it inaccurately
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "The limited number of participants who tried to address the question provided inaccurate answers, suggesting that they did not grasp the underlying concepts."

## Misconception 231: Students think the leaving group departs before the nucleophile arrives, rather than understanding the stepwise SN1 mechanism through carbocation formation followed by nucleophilic attack.

**Topic:** Organohalides / SN1 mechanism
**Correct concept:** SN1 proceeds in two steps: first, ionization to a planar carbocation; second, nucleophilic attack on the carbocation.
**Expected reasoning:** Separate the mechanism into a slow ionization step that forms a stable planar carbocation, then a faster nucleophilic attack on that intermediate from either side.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reactions / SN1
**Prevalence:** 9 out of 33
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "the leaving group will depart before the nucleophile arrives at the scene"

## Misconception 232: Students equate chirality with a molecule making the same image in a mirror, or base chirality solely on having an atom with four different substituents rather than the whole non-superimposable molecule.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Definition of chirality
**Correct concept:** A chiral molecule is non-superimposable on its mirror image.
**Expected reasoning:** Determine whether the entire molecular arrangement and its mirror image can be superimposed; the presence of four different substituents can indicate a stereogenic center but does not by itself define chirality in every case.
**Concept category:** chirality / mirror images
**Prevalence:** 9 out of 33 had difficulty; 21 out of 33 provided incorrect responses in the related chiral/achiral classification question
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "It is a forming the same image on the mirror as the original one is called to be chiral"

## Misconception 233: Students think that a molecule is chiral simply because they see symmetry, or because the molecule has a methyl group / lacks four groups attached to the central atom, instead of checking for a stereocenter and the absence of internal symmetry.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Identifying chirality centers and chirality of molecules
**Correct concept:** To decide chirality, identify stereocenters and check whether the molecule as a whole is non-superimposable on its mirror image; some molecules with stereocenters can still be achiral if they are meso or otherwise symmetric.
**Expected reasoning:** Inspect the three-dimensional structure for a carbon bonded to four different groups and then test the overall symmetry of the molecule; do not assume chirality from the presence of symmetry alone.
**Concept category:** chirality centers / symmetry
**Prevalence:** 14 out of 33 for cis-1,2-dimethylcyclobutane item; also discussed as a broader misconception area
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
