**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "I think this molecule has a chirality center because I see a plane of symmetry. Hence, I responded that it is chiral"

## Misconception 234: Students cannot correctly rank substituents and therefore assign the wrong R/S configuration, often because they orient the molecule incorrectly.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Assigning R/S configuration
**Correct concept:** R/S assignment requires ranking substituents by CIP priority, placing the lowest priority group away from the viewer, and reading the 1→2→3 sequence as clockwise for R and counterclockwise for S.
**Expected reasoning:** Apply CIP rules to rank substituents, rotate the molecule mentally or on paper so the lowest-priority group points away, and then read the order of the remaining groups to assign R or S.
**Concept category:** CIP priority / R-S configuration
**Prevalence:** 9 out of 33
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "Ranking the four substituents is my problem."

## Misconception 235: Students cannot reliably identify mirror-image relationships between molecules and end up guessing the enantiomer/diastereomer classification.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Enantiomers vs diastereomers
**Correct concept:** Enantiomers are non-superimposable mirror images; diastereomers are stereoisomers that are not mirror images of each other.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare the full 3D arrangements of the stereocenters, determine whether one molecule is the mirror image of the other, and classify accordingly rather than guessing from the number of stereocenters.
**Concept category:** stereoisomer relationships
**Prevalence:** 21 out of 33
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "I could see that these three molecules have two stereocenters. However, I cannot really tell which one is a mirror image of the other."

## Misconception 236: Students think chirality is explained by vague ideas such as a chiral center bonding with other molecules in a chain, or they treat symmetry as sufficient to decide achirality without proper stereochemical analysis.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Chirality of 3-methylhexane
**Correct concept:** Chirality depends on the molecule having a non-superimposable mirror image; 3-methylhexane is chiral because C-3 is attached to four different substituent groups.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify the tetrahedral carbon attached to four different groups, then decide whether the molecule and its mirror image are non-superimposable; do not use irrelevant bonding language or symmetry alone.
**Concept category:** chirality / stereocenter identification
**Prevalence:** 23 out of 33
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "The molecule is chiral because chiral molecules are molecules, which cannot be superimposed and the chiral center bonds with other molecules or compounds in their chain"

## Misconception 237: Students think the molecule is chiral simply because they see a plane of symmetry, or they fail to recognize the actual chirality centers on the ring carbons.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Chirality of cis-1,2-dimethylcyclobutane
**Correct concept:** Chirality must be assessed by the full three-dimensional arrangement; cis-1,2-dimethylcyclobutane has specific ring carbons as stereocenters, and symmetry arguments must be applied carefully because symmetry can also indicate achirality in some cases.
**Expected reasoning:** Locate the candidate stereocenters on the ring, evaluate whether each carbon has four distinct attachments in the molecular context, and then determine whether the molecule is overall chiral or meso from its symmetry elements.
**Concept category:** chirality centers / symmetry
**Prevalence:** 14 out of 33
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "I think this molecule has a chirality center because I see a plane of symmetry. Hence, I responded that it is chiral"

## Misconception 238: Students think no enantiomeric product forms, or they do not understand that resolving a racemate with a chiral reagent gives diastereomeric salts that can be separated by crystallization.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Resolution of racemates with chiral acids/bases
**Correct concept:** Resolution of a racemate with a chiral acid or base forms diastereomeric salts, which have different solubilities and can be separated by crystallization, followed by regeneration of the enantiomer with acid or base.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that reacting an enantiomeric mixture with a chiral resolving agent creates diastereomers; then separate them by differing physical properties such as crystallization and recover the pure enantiomer afterward.
**Concept category:** resolution / diastereomeric salts
**Prevalence:** Not quantified as a single count in the narrative; described as a misconception in the response to the resolution question
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156.md)
**Evidence:** "PSCTs responded to no enantiomer production. This assertion is a misconception, as the reaction will yield a combination of diastereomeric salts"

## Misconception 239: Students think substituting NaOH into a haloalkane requires adding water and/or that the reaction should produce a metal or an unclear non-alcohol product.

**Topic:** Organohalides / Nucleophilic substitution of 1-chloropropane with sodium hydroxide
**Correct concept:** A hydroxide ion acts as the nucleophile and replaces the halide leaving group to form an alcohol and a halide ion.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify OH- as the nucleophile, Cl- as the leaving group, and predict substitution of Cl by OH to give propan-1-ol plus chloride ion; no water or metal product is formed in the substitution product itself.
**Concept category:** substitution reactions
**Prevalence:** 20 out of 33 PSCTs had misconceptions on question 1.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “If I substitute the NaOH into this haloalkane to produce alcohol, I will have to add water H2O in this reaction” (PSCT20).

## Misconception 240: Students misidentify the product/group introduced in a substitution synthesis and misconstrue the reaction pathway for making propane-1-thiol.

**Topic:** Organohalides / Synthesis of propane-1-thiol by nucleophilic substitution
**Correct concept:** Propane-1-thiol can be synthesized by nucleophilic substitution of a leaving group in 1-bromopropane with HS- to introduce the -SH group.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that the thiol product comes from replacing bromide with HS- in an alkyl halide substrate, so the product should contain the -SH functional group, not an unrelated structure.
**Concept category:** substitution reactions
**Prevalence:** 21 out of 33 PSCTs had misconceptions on question 2.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “Question 2 pertains to the potential use of a substitution reaction within the context of synthesis ... PSCT8 gave the answers shown in Figure 8. Figure 8 illustrates that PSCT8 had a misconception.”

## Misconception 241: Students misconstrue the SN2 mechanism and do not correctly understand the single-step backside attack/transition state description.

**Topic:** Organohalides / SN2 mechanism
**Correct concept:** SN2 occurs in one concerted step with backside attack, a transition state, and inversion of configuration at the reacting carbon.
**Expected reasoning:** Reason from the nucleophile approaching 180° away from the leaving group, simultaneous bond making/breaking, and inversion at the stereocenter.
**Concept category:** SN2 mechanism
**Prevalence:** 23 out of 33 PSCTs had misconceptions on question 3.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “PSCTs were unsuccessful in assigning the configurations to both the substrate and the product.”

## Misconception 242: Students think the leaving group departs before the nucleophile arrives in SN1, rather than understanding the stepwise carbocation mechanism and subsequent nucleophilic attack.

**Topic:** Organohalides / SN1 mechanism
**Correct concept:** SN1 proceeds in two steps: first ionization to a carbocation, then nucleophilic attack on the planar carbocation.
**Expected reasoning:** Reason that the substrate ionizes first to form a carbocation; only after that does the nucleophile attack, giving the substitution product.
**Concept category:** SN1 mechanism
**Prevalence:** 9 out of 33 PSCTs had misconceptions on question 6.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “I think in the reaction, the leaving group will depart before the nucleophile arrives at the scene and the substrate will then react with the nucleophile, I guess.” (PSCT16)

## Misconception 243: Students think the reaction between an organohalide and sodium chloride gives alcohol, but they misinterpret the reagent/product details and by-product nature.

**Topic:** Organohalides / Chloride leaving group and product prediction
**Correct concept:** In substitution with sodium hydroxide, hydroxide replaces the halide to form an alcohol; sodium chloride is not the reagent that produces alcohol in this context.
**Expected reasoning:** Match the nucleophile to the leaving group: OH- substitutes for halide to form the alcohol product and halide leaves as an ion.
**Concept category:** substitution reactions
**Prevalence:** The paper reports a significant number, but no exact count in this sentence; question 1 had 20/33 misconceptions.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “the interaction between an organohalide and sodium chloride would result in the formation of alcohol.”

## Misconception 244: Students do not understand the formation and/or strength of ethyl Grignard reagent and give inaccurate answers about its behavior as a base.

**Topic:** Organohalides / Grignard reagent formation and basicity
**Correct concept:** Alkyl halides react with magnesium in dry ether to form Grignard reagents (RMgX), which are very strong bases/nucleophiles.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize the organohalide–magnesium insertion step and infer that the resulting organomagnesium compound is highly basic and reactive toward acidic protons.
**Concept category:** Grignard reagents
**Prevalence:** Most participants; no exact percentage given.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “Question 5 remained unanswered by most participants... the limited number of participants who tried to address the question provided inaccurate answers, suggesting that they did not grasp the underlying concepts.”

## Misconception 245: Students think chirality means a molecule forms the same image as its mirror image or treat chirality as a property of a single atom with four different groups rather than of the whole molecule.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Definition of chirality
**Correct concept:** A chiral molecule is non-superimposable on its mirror image and lacks an internal plane of symmetry; chirality is a property of the entire molecule.
**Expected reasoning:** Determine whether the whole structure and its mirror image can be superimposed; if not, the molecule is chiral.
**Concept category:** chirality
**Prevalence:** 21 out of 33 PSCTs had misconceptions on question 2 in stereochemistry.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “It is a forming the same image on the mirror as the original one is called to be chiral” (PSCT 16).

## Misconception 246: Students think a molecule is chiral simply because it cannot be superimposed and/or because a chiral center bonds with other molecules in a chain, without analyzing the structure correctly.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / 3-methylhexane chirality
**Correct concept:** Chirality must be determined by the molecular structure, including whether there is a stereocenter and whether the molecule is superimposable on its mirror image.
**Expected reasoning:** Inspect the structure for a stereocenter and symmetry; do not rely on vague statements about superimposability or bonding to other molecules.
**Concept category:** chirality
**Prevalence:** 23 out of 33 PSCTs were unable to provide a satisfactory explanation on question 3.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “The molecule is chiral because chiral molecules are molecules, which cannot be superimposed and the chiral center bonds with other molecules or compounds in their chain” (PSCT 12).

## Misconception 247: Students cannot correctly rank substituents and then assign the R or S configuration; they may orient the structure incorrectly and use the wrong sense of rotation.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Assigning R/S configuration
**Correct concept:** Assign priorities using CIP rules, orient the lowest priority group away, and read clockwise as R and anticlockwise as S.
**Expected reasoning:** Rank substituents by atomic number, place the lowest-priority group away from the viewer, then trace 1→2→3 to determine R or S.
**Concept category:** R/S configuration
**Prevalence:** 9 participants showed a lack of understanding; 20 out of 33 had misconceptions on question 4.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “Ranking the four substituents is my problem. If I have to place -H away from me, then the other substituents must be placed next to each other such that the direction of travel will be anticlockwise (left turn)” (PSCT 18).

## Misconception 248: Students cannot reliably determine which molecules are mirror images or identify stereochemical relationships, and resort to guessing.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Enantiomers and diastereomers
**Correct concept:** Enantiomers are non-superimposable mirror images; diastereomers are stereoisomers that are not mirror images.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify stereocenters, compare configurations/mirror relationships systematically, and then classify molecules as enantiomers or diastereomers based on that comparison.
**Concept category:** stereoisomer relationships
**Prevalence:** 21 out of 33 PSCTs experienced conceptual difficulties on question 7.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “I could see that these three molecules have two stereocenters. However, I cannot really tell which one is a mirror image of the other... I had no option but to just guess the answer” (PSCT 9).

## Misconception 249: Students think seeing a plane of symmetry means a molecule is chiral, or they fail to locate chirality centers correctly in the ring system.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / cis-1,2-dimethylcyclobutane and chirality centers
**Correct concept:** A chirality center is typically a carbon bonded to four different groups; the presence or absence of a plane of symmetry helps determine chirality, and symmetric molecules may be achiral or meso despite stereocenters.
**Expected reasoning:** Check each relevant carbon for four different substituents and assess whether the whole molecule is superimposable on its mirror image; a plane of symmetry indicates achirality, not chirality.
**Concept category:** chirality centers / symmetry
**Prevalence:** 14 out of 33 PSCTs showed lack of comprehension on question 8.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “I think this molecule has a chirality center because I see a plane of symmetry. Hence, I responded that it is chiral” (PSCT 20).

## Misconception 250: Students think the reaction of a racemate with a chiral resolving agent produces no enantiomeric/diastereomeric separation or that crystallization is not involved in the resolution process.

**Topic:** Stereochemistry / Resolution of racemates with chiral acids/bases
**Correct concept:** A racemic mixture can react with a chiral resolving agent to form diastereomeric salts, which can be separated by crystallization; the enantiomer can then be regenerated by acid/base treatment.
**Expected reasoning:** Recognize that interaction with a chiral acid/base converts enantiomers into diastereomeric salts with different solubilities, allowing separation and recovery of the pure enantiomer.
**Concept category:** resolution / diastereomeric salts
**Prevalence:** Not quantified exactly in the quoted sentence; the paper reports that many PSCTs had limited knowledge, while 10/33 and 21/33 showed understanding in questions 9 and 10 respectively.
**Population:** 33 pre-service chemistry teachers (PSCTs)
**Source:** Assessing conceptual difficulties experienced by pre-service chemistry teachers in organic chemistry (assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2/assessing-conceptual-difficulties-experienced-by-pre-service-chemistry-teachers-in-organic-chemistry-14156-2.md)
**Evidence:** “PSCTs responded to no enantiomer production. This assertion is a misconception, as the reaction will yield a combination of diastereomeric salts, which may be effectively isolated by means of crystallization.”

## Misconception 251: Students think lowest energy corresponds to a molecule being easiest to break apart, more reactive, or more strained/less stable.

**Topic:** Conformational analysis / Cyclohexane chair conformations and energy
**Correct concept:** Lowest energy corresponds to the conformation with the most favorable arrangement, here the equatorial substituent placement that minimizes repulsive interactions and is more stable.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the steric interactions in each chair conformation, recognize that equatorial substituents reduce 1,3-diaxial repulsions, and infer that lower repulsion means lower energy and greater stability.
**Concept category:** energy vs stability
**Prevalence:** 2 of 19 students
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "Lowest energy means easiest to break apart."

## Misconception 252: Students choose the trans product because they judge it to be the most stable or best steric arrangement, rather than using the reaction mechanism to predict a stereospecific syn addition product.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Stereospecific syn addition
**Correct concept:** A stereospecific syn addition gives the cis product, because both groups add to the same side of the ring according to the mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the reaction mechanism to track the face of addition and predict that syn addition produces substituents on the same side, independent of which product looks more stable.
**Concept category:** product stability instead of mechanism
**Prevalence:** 5 of 19 students chose trans; 7 of 19 chose correctly
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "I think it's going to be [trans] because I think it's the best distribution of the methyl groups"

## Misconception 253: Students evaluate the proposed products instead of the reaction mechanism when deciding which mechanism is correct.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / Curved-arrow notation / mechanism selection
**Correct concept:** Correct mechanism selection should be based on electron flow, bond making/breaking, and whether the proposed mechanism is chemically valid, not on whether a product looks stable or familiar.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should follow the curved arrows, check conservation of valence/octet rules, and determine whether the stepwise electron movement produces a feasible mechanism before judging any products.
**Concept category:** product evaluation instead of mechanism
**Prevalence:** Documented in multiple students' responses; exact count not given
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "Actually, I'm going to look at the products."

## Misconception 254: Students think the longest carbon chain or greater distance of the leaving group from a phenyl group makes the substrate more reactive in S_N1.

**Topic:** Reaction mechanisms / S_N1 reactivity
**Correct concept:** S_N1 reactivity depends on formation of the most stable carbocation; greater substitution at the reaction center increases reactivity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the carbon bearing the leaving group, determine the carbocation that would form, and choose the substrate that forms the most stable carbocation, typically the most substituted one.
**Concept category:** model confusion / carbocation stability
**Prevalence:** 5 respondents gave this reasoning; only 3 of 19 answered correctly
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "the longer the hydrocarbon chain the more reactive."

## Misconception 255: Students think the relative distance between substituents determines cyclohexane chair stability, rather than axial versus equatorial placement.

**Topic:** Conformational analysis / Cyclohexane chair stability
**Correct concept:** Chair stability is determined by whether substituents are axial or equatorial; equatorial placement is favored because it reduces steric interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify each substituent as axial or equatorial and compare the steric interactions, especially 1,3-diaxial repulsions, to decide which conformation is lower in energy.
**Concept category:** surface feature reasoning / axial-equatorial confusion
**Prevalence:** Documented in multiple students' responses; exact count not given
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "if the methyl groups are further apart, then they are lower in energy"

## Misconception 256: Students misapply axial and equatorial labels, using them incorrectly when describing substituent positions.

**Topic:** Conformational analysis / Cyclohexane chair terminology
**Correct concept:** Axial substituents point roughly perpendicular to the ring plane, while equatorial substituents project outward around the ring equator; stability depends on this correct identification.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should correctly identify the geometry of each substituent in the chair conformation and then use those labels to infer steric strain and stability.
**Concept category:** axial/equatorial designation
**Prevalence:** At least 2 students (Emil and Clara) plus Francesca misapplied the terms
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "I'll probably say [distracter with two axial groups] because it has methyl groups in the equatorial position, which would lead to 1,3 steric hindrance or something."

## Misconception 257: Students attribute aromaticity to non-aromatic six-membered rings with one double bond or use aromaticity as the explanation for resonance stabilization where aromaticity is not present.

**Topic:** Aromaticity and resonance / Mislabeling non-aromatic resonance-stabilized rings as aromatic
**Correct concept:** Aromaticity requires a specific conjugated cyclic π-electron system; resonance stabilization in a non-aromatic ring should not be described as aromaticity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should examine the ring structure for cyclic conjugation, planarity, and π-electron count; if these criteria are not met, the structure may have resonance stabilization but is not aromatic.
**Concept category:** aromaticity vs resonance
**Prevalence:** Documented in multiple students' explanations; exact count not given
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "Aromaticity is key to stability"

## Misconception 258: Students refer to organic substituents or functional groups as ligands.

**Topic:** Organic nomenclature and substituent terminology / Using inorganic chemistry term 'ligand' for organic substituents
**Correct concept:** In IUPAC naming and organic structure description, these groups are substituents or functional groups, not ligands unless discussing coordination chemistry.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the terminology appropriate to the chemical context: identify functional groups/substituents on an organic molecule and reserve 'ligand' for coordination compounds.
**Concept category:** cross-disciplinary terminology transfer
**Prevalence:** Documented in multiple responses; exact count not given
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "what the number one ligand is"

## Misconception 259: Students think a center with three bonded atoms and one lone pair could be square planar, applying an inorganic coordination geometry to a main-group atom.

**Topic:** Molecular geometry and hybridization / Central atom with three bonds and one lone pair
**Correct concept:** A main-group atom with three bonds and one lone pair has tetrahedral electron geometry and trigonal pyramidal molecular geometry, not square planar.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should count electron domains around the atom, assign the electron-domain geometry from VSEPR, and derive the molecular shape accordingly.
**Concept category:** cross-disciplinary geometry transfer
**Prevalence:** Documented in at least 2 students' responses
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "the electron pair should be tetrahedral, but it could be square planar"

## Misconception 260: Students treat the most stable species as the most reactive.

**Topic:** Molecular reactivity and stability / Relationship between stability and reactivity
**Correct concept:** For the questions here, greater stability generally means lower reactivity; in S_N1, the more stable carbocation-forming substrate is more reactive because the intermediate is stabilized.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between the stability of reactants/intermediates and the kinetics of reaction, then use the mechanism to decide which species reacts faster.
**Concept category:** energy-reaction relationship
**Prevalence:** Described as present among some students; exact count not given
**Population:** Fourth-year chemistry students (n=19)
**Source:** Alternative conceptions of organic chemistry topics among fourth year chemistry students (b806228p/b806228p.md)
**Evidence:** "there is evidence that the relationship between the relative energies and reactivity of molecules still remains unclear to some students, as some chose the most stable species as the most reactive."

## Misconception 261: Students can correctly predict the product of a nucleophilic substitution reaction without understanding how the product forms, and may treat the mechanism as unnecessary or beyond what they need to know.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Nucleophilic substitution product vs mechanism
**Correct concept:** A correct product prediction should be supported by understanding and drawing the full reaction mechanism, including electron movement and intermediates.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the reaction conditions to the reaction pathway, identify the nucleophile, leaving group, and mechanism type, then use curved arrows to show how bonds break and form to produce the product.
**Concept category:** mechanism understanding
**Prevalence:** In the traditional spring course, 90/111 (81%) answered the product question correctly, but only 7/28 who attempted the mechanism did so correctly; 75% did not attempt the mechanism.
**Population:** Undergraduate non-science majors in the traditional spring organic chemistry course
**Source:** Implementing POGIL in the lecture and the Science Writing Heuristic in the laboratory—student perceptions and performance in undergraduate organic chemistry (b806231p/b806231p.md)
**Evidence:** "students could predict the products of organic reactions, but struggled to provide reaction mechanisms for those same reactions"

## Misconception 262: Students may think that earning a perfect laboratory report score means they understand and can independently reproduce the reaction mechanism later on an exam.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Mechanism recall from lab reports
**Correct concept:** Understanding a mechanism requires being able to reconstruct it and apply it in a new context, not just reproduce a completed mechanism in a lab report.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the lab experience to understand why each step in the mechanism occurs, so that the mechanism can later be drawn from knowledge of reactivity rather than memorization or copying.
**Concept category:** mechanism understanding
**Prevalence:** 32/111 students received perfect lab reports; only 5/32 attempted the same mechanism on the exam and 2/5 were successful
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students in the traditional spring course
**Source:** Implementing POGIL in the lecture and the Science Writing Heuristic in the laboratory—student perceptions and performance in undergraduate organic chemistry (b806231p/b806231p.md)
**Evidence:** "32 students out of 111 received a perfect score on their laboratory report, which included a full mechanism ... Yet when asked to complete this same mechanism two weeks later on an exam, only five of these thirty-two students tried and only two of them were successful."

## Misconception 263: Students may believe that identifying the reaction outcome is enough, even when they cannot explain the pathway that leads to that outcome.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Mechanism explanation versus product recognition
**Correct concept:** Correct chemistry performance includes being able to explain the transformation mechanism, not only the final product.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from reactants and conditions to the mechanistic steps that justify the product, demonstrating causal understanding rather than recognition alone.
**Concept category:** mechanism understanding
**Prevalence:** During the traditional spring semester, 90/111 (81%) answered the product question correctly, but only 7/28 attempted mechanisms were correct
**Population:** Undergraduate students in the traditional spring organic chemistry course
**Source:** Implementing POGIL in the lecture and the Science Writing Heuristic in the laboratory—student perceptions and performance in undergraduate organic chemistry (b806231p/b806231p.md)
**Evidence:** "even though students could recognize reaction conditions and write the correct product, they did not understand how the product is formed."

## Misconception 264: Chemistry problems should have one definite right answer, with all other answers being wrong.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry problem solving / Nature of answers in chemistry
**Correct concept:** Organic chemistry often allows multiple valid products, mechanisms, or synthetic pathways, and students must evaluate which is most appropriate for the context.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that the correct outcome depends on reagents, conditions, and the problem context; they should compare alternatives rather than assume a single absolute answer exists.
**Concept category:** dualism / right-wrong thinking
**Prevalence:** At least 7 students described organic chemistry as 'straightforward'; multiple students expressed this view.
**Population:** 18 organic chemistry students in a spiral curriculum; examples from Charlie and Luna
**Source:** Perry's Scheme of Intellectual and Epistemological Development as a framework for describing student difficulties in learning organic chemistry (c005469k/c005469k.md)
**Evidence:** "there's right, there's right and wrong answers. There's not, like, \"how do you feel about this?\""

## Misconception 265: One starting compound treated with one set of reagents should lead to only one correct product.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Reaction outcomes
**Correct concept:** Substitution and elimination reactions can produce more than one correct product, including mixtures, depending on conditions and mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should analyze the mechanism and reaction conditions to determine whether SN1/SN2/E1/E2 pathways compete and whether multiple products are plausible.
**Concept category:** reaction prediction / multiple products
**Prevalence:** Reported as a common difficulty among many students; no exact percentage given.
**Population:** Organic chemistry students in the study, especially those discussing substitution/elimination reactions
**Source:** Perry's Scheme of Intellectual and Epistemological Development as a framework for describing student difficulties in learning organic chemistry (c005469k/c005469k.md)
**Evidence:** "students found it difficult to accept that *one* starting compound treated with only *one* set of reagents could lead to more than one correct product"

## Misconception 266: A reaction follows a simple one-to-one pattern such as A yields B, rather than potentially yielding multiple products such as A yields both B and C.

**Topic:** Substitution and elimination reactions / Reaction pathways and product multiplicity
**Correct concept:** Some organic reactions can yield multiple products from the same starting material depending on competing pathways and reaction conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should consider competing mechanisms and evaluate all feasible products rather than expecting a single linear transformation.
**Concept category:** product prediction / reaction outcomes
**Prevalence:** Described as a major source of difficulty for many students; no exact percentage given.
**Population:** Students in the organic chemistry sequence
**Source:** Perry's Scheme of Intellectual and Epistemological Development as a framework for describing student difficulties in learning organic chemistry (c005469k/c005469k.md)
**Evidence:** "students who had expected organic chemistry to follow a pattern of 'A yields B and then B yields C' faced a problematic transition to 'A yields both B and C'."

## Misconception 267: A synthesis problem should have one obvious route, so designing a synthesis is mainly a matter of remembering a single reaction rather than choosing among alternatives.

**Topic:** Organic synthesis / Number of possible routes
**Correct concept:** Synthesis problems often have multiple feasible routes, and students must judge which pathway or reagent set is best for the target compound and constraints.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should work backward or forward through several possible transformations, compare reagent sets, and select the most viable route based on selectivity, feasibility, and functional group compatibility.
**Concept category:** synthesis design / multiple pathways
**Prevalence:** Reported by Marietta and echoed by Luna as a major obstacle; no exact percentage given.
**Population:** Students in the spring semester of organic chemistry
**Source:** Perry's Scheme of Intellectual and Epistemological Development as a framework for describing student difficulties in learning organic chemistry (c005469k/c005469k.md)
**Evidence:** "Having to put everything together and sort of use your creativity, and knowing that I know everything solid to come up with a synthesis problem is difficult."

## Misconception 268: Knowing one reagent pair for a transformation is sufficient because the same reaction will always be the needed answer.

**Topic:** Organic synthesis / Reagent selection
**Correct concept:** Different substrates and target transformations may require different reagents, and students need to know several possible methods for similar transformations.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the functional group being transformed with the specific reaction goal and compare alternative reagents rather than memorize only one example.
**Concept category:** reagent choice / synthesis planning
**Prevalence:** Common enough to be discussed as a general struggle in synthesis; no exact percentage given.
**Population:** Students struggling with multi-step synthesis in organic chemistry
**Source:** Perry's Scheme of Intellectual and Epistemological Development as a framework for describing student difficulties in learning organic chemistry (c005469k/c005469k.md)
**Evidence:** "you can remember just that one but if you need five options just in case it's one of the other options that's given on the test"

## Misconception 269: If several reaction pathways are available, all are equally acceptable and the task is just to produce any route.

**Topic:** Organic synthesis / Best pathway selection
**Correct concept:** When multiple synthetic routes exist, students must evaluate which pathway is best or most viable for the target synthesis.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare pathways using criteria such as efficiency, selectivity, availability of reagents, and functional group compatibility to choose the best synthesis.
**Concept category:** route evaluation / relativism
**Prevalence:** Described as a frequent requirement; no exact percentage given.
**Population:** Students in the organic chemistry sequence, especially during spring synthesis work
**Source:** Perry's Scheme of Intellectual and Epistemological Development as a framework for describing student difficulties in learning organic chemistry (c005469k/c005469k.md)
**Evidence:** "students were frequently required to judge, as emerging organic chemists, which set of reagents or pathway was 'best'."

## Misconception 270: Students think that if the starting material contains a double bond, the reaction should be substitution rather than elimination.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: choosing between substitution and elimination
**Correct concept:** The presence of a double bond in the substrate does not determine whether substitution or elimination occurs; the mechanism is determined by reagent properties and reaction conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the reagent as a base and/or nucleophile, its strength, the substrate, leaving group, and solvent to decide whether SN1, SN2, E1, or E2 is favored, rather than using the mere presence of a double bond as a cue.
**Concept category:** surface features / mechanistic reasoning
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "the presence of a double bond in the starting material implies that a substitution is the reaction to be done."

## Misconception 271: Students think that the presence of a solvent means substitution should be performed.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: role of solvent
**Correct concept:** A solvent alone does not determine the mechanism; the full set of reactants and their chemical roles must be considered.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that solvent identity is only one factor, and should analyze whether the reagent is acting as a base or nucleophile and whether the conditions favor SN1/E1 or SN2/E2.
**Concept category:** surface features / mechanistic reasoning
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "the presence of a solvent in the starting materials implies that a substitution is the reaction to be done."

## Misconception 272: Students think that a protic solvent automatically means substitution must occur.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: protic solvent cue
**Correct concept:** A protic solvent can influence mechanism selection, but it does not by itself force substitution; the reagent strength and substrate also matter.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that protic solvents often favor carbocation pathways, but then still determine whether the reagent is a weak nucleophile/base or a strong nucleophile/base and which mechanism is actually favored.
**Concept category:** solvent effects / mechanistic reasoning
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "the presence of a protic solvent implies that a substitution reaction must be performed."

## Misconception 273: Students think that if methanol is present, substitution must be the reaction.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: methanol as reagent cue
**Correct concept:** Methanol can function as both a weak nucleophile and a weak base, so it can support SN1 and E1 pathways rather than forcing substitution only.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should classify methanol by its chemical role and strength, then infer that weak nucleophiles and weak bases can participate in SN1/E1 outcomes depending on the substrate and conditions.
**Concept category:** reagent classification / base-nucleophile distinction
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "the presence of CH3OH as a reagent always implies that a substitution reaction must be performed."

## Misconception 274: Students think SN1 only causes inversion of stereochemistry, like SN2.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: SN1 stereochemistry
**Correct concept:** SN1 proceeds through a planar carbocation intermediate and therefore can give racemization rather than simple inversion.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the carbocation intermediate in SN1, recognize that nucleophilic attack can occur from either face, and infer formation of both enantiomers.
**Concept category:** mechanism / stereochemistry
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "$S_N1$, it attacks from the backside so it flips and it makes it go out."

## Misconception 275: Students think a racemic mixture means one SN1 product and one E1 product.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: racemic mixture meaning
**Correct concept:** A racemic mixture means two enantiomeric products from the same substitution pathway, not one substitution product plus one elimination product.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect racemization to attack on a planar carbocation intermediate and realize that both products arise from SN1 substitution.
**Concept category:** mechanism / stereochemistry
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "That one of them is an $S_N1$, and the other one is an E1."

## Misconception 276: Students think methanol behaves as an Arrhenius base that supplies hydroxide ions.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: methanol as Arrhenius base
**Correct concept:** Methanol is a covalent molecule and, in this context, acts as a weak base/nucleophile; it does not produce hydroxide ions as an Arrhenius base would.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish ionic hydroxide-containing bases from covalent alcohols and identify methanol as a neutral molecule that can accept a proton only weakly.
**Concept category:** acid-base concepts
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "CH3OH behaves as an Arrhenius base"

## Misconception 277: Students think that if the nucleophile contains an OH group, the product must substitute in the OH group rather than the methoxy group.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: OH versus OCH3 in product
**Correct concept:** In methanol substitution, the oxygen-bearing methoxy group is incorporated; the product is not formed by simply adding hydroxide as if methanol were an ionic hydroxide source.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should track atom connectivity through the mechanism and recognize that methanol acts as CH3OH, not as free OH−, so the substituent introduced is OCH3 after proton transfer.
**Concept category:** structure / reagent identity
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "if the nucleophile has an OH group, that one is necessarily the group that substitutes"

## Misconception 278: Students think that if the starting material shows stereochemistry, the product must have inverted stereochemistry.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: stereochemistry cue
**Correct concept:** Whether inversion occurs depends on the mechanism; SN2 gives inversion, but SN1 does not necessarily do so.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first determine the mechanism, then apply the correct stereochemical consequence of that mechanism rather than using stereochemistry in the starting material as a blanket cue.
**Concept category:** mechanism / stereochemistry
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "If a starting material shows stereochemistry, the product must have an inverted stereochemistry."

## Misconception 279: Students think weak bases proceed by SN2 and strong bases proceed by SN1.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1a: weak/strong base and substitution mechanism
**Correct concept:** Strong bases generally favor elimination, while weak bases are more associated with SN1/E1 conditions; SN2 is favored by strong nucleophiles that are weak bases.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish base strength from nucleophilicity and match reagent properties to the correct mechanism instead of reversing the relationships.
**Concept category:** reagent classification / mechanism selection
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "weak bases proceed by $S_N$ 2 reactions and strong bases proceed by $S_N$ 1 reactions"

## Misconception 280: Students think SN2 conserves the stereochemistry of the starting material.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1b: SN2 stereochemistry
**Correct concept:** SN2 proceeds by backside attack and gives inversion of configuration.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize the concerted backside displacement in SN2 and predict inversion at the reacting stereocenter.
**Concept category:** mechanism / stereochemistry
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "$S_N2$ reactions conserve the stereochemistry of the starting material."

## Misconception 281: Students think elimination reactions are the only reactions that affect product stereochemistry.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1b: elimination versus stereochemistry
**Correct concept:** Substitution reactions such as SN2 also affect stereochemistry, specifically by inversion.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect stereochemical outcomes to the specific mechanism, not assume only elimination changes stereochemistry.
**Concept category:** mechanism / stereochemistry
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "Elimination reactions are the only ones that affect the stereochemistry of the product."

## Misconception 282: Students think an aprotic solvent means elimination will occur.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1b: aprotic solvent cue
**Correct concept:** Aprotic solvents often favor SN2 reactions by supporting concerted mechanisms and not strongly solvating nucleophiles; they do not signal elimination by themselves.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use solvent type as one factor in deciding mechanism and should relate polar aprotic solvents to SN2 rather than elimination.
**Concept category:** solvent effects / mechanism selection
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "The presence of an aprotic solvent implies that an elimination reaction is to be performed."

## Misconception 283: Students think a sodium cation can act as a nucleophile.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1b: sodium cation as nucleophile
**Correct concept:** Sodium cation is not a nucleophile; the nucleophilic species is iodide from NaI.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the negatively charged or electron-rich species as the nucleophile and recognize that Na+ is a spectator cation.
**Concept category:** reagent classification
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "A sodium cation can act as a nucleophile."

## Misconception 284: Students think E2 reactions have a carbocation intermediate.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: E2 with carbocation intermediate
**Correct concept:** E2 is a concerted elimination with no carbocation intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify that the base removes a proton as the leaving group departs simultaneously, producing an alkene through a single transition state.
**Concept category:** mechanism
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "E2 reactions have a carbocation intermediate"

## Misconception 285: Students think the hydrogen removed in elimination is the one on the carbon with the most hydrogens attached.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: hydrogen abstraction choice
**Correct concept:** The proton removed in E2 is determined by anti-coplanar geometry and product stability, not by the simple count of hydrogens on a carbon.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate which beta hydrogen is anti-coplanar to the leaving group and which elimination pathway is mechanistically allowed and favorable.
**Concept category:** E2 mechanism / regiochemistry
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "During an elimination reaction, the hydrogen atom is abstracted from the carbon atom that has the most hydrogen atoms bonded to it."

## Misconception 286: Students think that if stereochemistry is present in the alkyl halide, the least substituted alkene must be formed.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: stereochemistry cue for alkene position
**Correct concept:** Stereochemistry in the substrate does not by itself determine that the least substituted alkene forms; E2 regiochemistry depends on the anti-coplanar arrangement and available beta hydrogens.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use E2 geometry and product stability, not the mere presence of stereochemistry in the substrate, to predict the alkene product.
**Concept category:** surface features / regiochemistry
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "The presence of stereochemistry in the alkyl halide implies that the least substituted alkene must be formed as a product."

## Misconception 287: Students think elimination must happen as far away as possible from the most substituted carbon atom.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: elimination site choice
**Correct concept:** The elimination site is determined by the allowed E2 geometry and the stability of the resulting alkene, not by a general rule of being far from the most substituted carbon.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should locate the anti-coplanar beta hydrogen and then determine which alkene is actually formed from the permitted E2 pathway.
**Concept category:** E2 mechanism / regiochemistry
**Population:** Undergraduate organic chemistry students (22 interviewed)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "the elimination reaction must happen as far away as possible from the most substituted carbon atom."

## Misconception 288: Students think the most stable alkene will always be formed during elimination.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: most stable alkene always forms
**Correct concept:** The most stable alkene is not always the product; the actual product depends on mechanism, steric constraints, and stereochemical requirements such as anti-coplanarity in E2.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should weigh alkene stability against mechanistic feasibility and choose the product permitted by the reaction pathway under the given conditions.
**Concept category:** regiochemistry / product selection
**Prevalence:** 6 participants
**Population:** Sub-group 2a of question 1c (6 undergraduate organic chemistry students)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "The most stable alkene will always be formed during an elimination reaction."

## Misconception 289: Students think that if the alkyl halide does not already contain a double bond, elimination must be performed.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: absence of double bond cue
**Correct concept:** The presence or absence of a double bond in the starting material does not determine whether elimination occurs; elimination is determined by the reaction conditions and available beta hydrogens.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should analyze the reagent as a base and the substrate geometry rather than assuming elimination from the absence of a double bond.
**Concept category:** surface features / mechanism selection
**Population:** Sub-group 2a of question 1c (6 undergraduate organic chemistry students)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "the absence of a double bond in the alkyl halide implies that an elimination reaction is to be performed."

## Misconception 290: Students think any structurally viable alkene can be formed in an elimination reaction.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: any alkene is possible
**Correct concept:** Only alkenes consistent with the mechanism and stereochemical constraints of the reaction are possible; in this case E2 geometry restricts the product set.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should check which beta hydrogen is anti-coplanar and which alkene results from the allowed elimination pathway, rather than treating all possible alkenes as equally valid.
**Concept category:** product prediction / mechanistic constraints
**Population:** Sub-group 2a of question 1c (6 undergraduate organic chemistry students)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "Any structurally viable alkene is possible when performing an elimination reaction."

## Misconception 291: Students think that explicitly showing separate charges in an ionic reagent means substitution must occur.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: charges on ionic reagent cue
**Correct concept:** The charge depiction in a reagent does not by itself determine substitution; the reagent's chemical role and reaction conditions matter.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret sodium methoxide as a base/nucleophile and then decide between E2 and substitution based on sterics and mechanism, not on charge notation alone.
**Concept category:** surface features / reagent interpretation
**Population:** Sub-group 2b of question 1c (10 undergraduate organic chemistry students)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "The explicit depiction of separate charges in an ionic reagent implies that a substitution reaction must be performed."

## Misconception 292: Students think that if stereochemistry is shown in the alkyl halide, then SN2 must be performed.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: stereochemistry cue for SN2
**Correct concept:** Stereochemistry in the substrate does not automatically indicate SN2; the mechanism depends on base strength, steric hindrance, and other conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate whether backside attack is feasible and whether the reagent favors substitution or elimination, rather than using stereochemical notation as a shortcut to SN2.
**Concept category:** surface features / mechanism selection
**Population:** Sub-group 2b of question 1c (10 undergraduate organic chemistry students)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "The presence of stereochemistry in the alkyl halide implies that an $S_{\rm N}2$ reaction is to be performed."

## Misconception 293: Students think elimination reactions only happen with bulky bases.

**Topic:** Alkyl halide reactions / Question 1c: bulky base rule
**Correct concept:** Elimination can occur with both bulky and unhindered bases; base strength, sterics, and substrate control whether E2 is favored.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between the role of base strength and steric hindrance and avoid restricting elimination to bulky bases only.
**Concept category:** base strength / steric effects
**Population:** Sub-group 2b of question 1c (10 undergraduate organic chemistry students)
**Source:** Students' understanding of alkyl halide reactions in undergraduate organic chemistry (c3rp00089c/c3rp00089c.md)
**Evidence:** "Elimination reactions only happen with bulky bases."

## Misconception 294: A nucleophile must be negatively charged, and an electrophile must be positively charged.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Charge as defining criterion
**Correct concept:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles are defined by electron-pair donating or accepting behavior; charge can be a clue but is not required.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should determine whether a species can donate electron density to form a bond (nucleophile) or accept electron density into an empty/accepting orbital (electrophile), then use charge only as one possible indicator of that behavior.
**Concept category:** charges
**Prevalence:** 10 of 11 students mentioned charges in one or both definitions; 4 also considered partial charges.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students (11 interviewees)
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "Nucleophiles donate electron pairs, they're negative, and electrophiles, positive, they're the opposite."

## Misconception 295: Partial negative charge makes a species a nucleophile and partial positive charge makes a species an electrophile.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Partial charge as defining criterion
**Correct concept:** Partial charge can be one indicator of reactivity, but nucleophiles and electrophiles are identified by their ability to donate or accept electron pairs, not by charge alone.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should ask whether the species has electron density available for donation or an electron-deficient site/orbital available to accept electron density; partial charge may arise from structure but does not by itself define the species.
**Concept category:** charges
**Prevalence:** 4 students considered partial charges in their definitions.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "they're electron dense, so they would have a minus charge, or partial minus, and then electrophiles are just the opposite... they would have partial positive or a completely positive charge."

## Misconception 296: Students focus on structural features such as charge or lone pairs instead of the actual electron-donating/electron-accepting function when defining nucleophiles and electrophiles.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Structure over function
**Correct concept:** A correct definition requires linking structure to function: a nucleophile donates electron density, and an electrophile accepts electron density.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect structural features (charge, lone pairs, empty orbitals) to what the species does in a reaction, rather than using structure as the definition itself.
**Concept category:** structure vs function
**Prevalence:** Described as the dominant pattern in the study; 10 of 11 mentioned charges.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "students prioritize structure over function, relying primarily on charges to define and identify such species"

## Misconception 297: A nucleophile must have a lone pair of electrons.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Lone pair as essential feature of nucleophile
**Correct concept:** A nucleophile is any electron-pair donor; lone pairs are common but not required because electrons in sigma or pi bonds can also act as nucleophiles.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify the species that can donate electron density in the reaction, which may be a lone pair, a bond, or another electron-rich region.
**Concept category:** electron pairs
**Prevalence:** Barry distinguished this as his essential feature; the paper notes this idea was not fully correct because bond electrons can also be nucleophiles.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "A lone pair."

## Misconception 298: An electrophile must have a positive charge.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Electrophile requires positive charge
**Correct concept:** An electrophile is a species that accepts electron density; it may be positively charged, partially positive, or neutral if it has an electron-accepting orbital/site.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should look for electron deficiency and an available accepting site or orbital, not just an explicit positive charge.
**Concept category:** charges
**Prevalence:** At least 1 student (Barry) explicitly expressed uncertainty but still treated positive charge as the main sign; many others used positive charge similarly.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "I don't think it has to have a positive charge... I know positive charges are like usually a sign for them"

## Misconception 299: Smaller atoms or groups are more polarizable, and polarizability was confused with being a defining feature for nucleophiles/electrophiles.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Polarizability misunderstood as small size
**Correct concept:** Polarizability generally increases with larger, more diffuse electron clouds; it is relevant especially for nucleophilicity and does not mean smaller size.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should understand polarizability as the ease with which an electron cloud is distorted, which is greater for larger atoms or species with more diffuse electrons.
**Concept category:** polarizability
**Prevalence:** Only 2 students mentioned polarizability in their definitions.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "one of them had, it helps if they're small and polarizable."

## Misconception 300: Polarizability means electron density is pulled toward a more electronegative atom in a larger molecule.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Polarizability confused with induction/polarization
**Correct concept:** Polarizability is the distortion of an electron cloud by an external force; it is distinct from bond polarization by induction.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish between electron-cloud distortion in response to a perturbation and electron withdrawal caused by electronegativity differences in bonds.
**Concept category:** polarizability
**Prevalence:** 1 student (Phoebe) explicitly showed this confusion.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "if there's a more electronegative atom, then the electron density will be pulled... towards that atom"

## Misconception 301: Electrophiles are identified only by positive charge, without considering the need for an empty orbital or electron-accepting site.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Ignoring empty orbitals in electrophiles
**Correct concept:** An electrophile must have an electron-deficient site capable of accepting electron density, often an empty orbital.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect electron deficiency to an available orbital/site that can accept a pair of electrons, not merely to charge status.
**Concept category:** orbitals
**Prevalence:** Only 1 student (Phoebe) articulated the correct orbital-based idea; most did not.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "they don't necessarily need to have a positive charge, they just need to... have, like an orbital that can be, um, like an empty orbital"

## Misconception 302: Students must know or invent the reaction mechanism before they can identify whether a reaction involves a nucleophile and electrophile.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Mechanism-first reasoning
**Correct concept:** Whether a species is nucleophilic or electrophilic should be inferred from its electron-donating or electron-accepting behavior in the reaction, not from an already-drawn mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should examine the reactants and their reactivity directly; if they later use a mechanism, it should be checked for chemical feasibility rather than used as the sole basis for labels.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reasoning
**Prevalence:** 71% of the 108 reaction evaluations involved students proposing mechanisms first (77/108).
**Population:** 11 interviewed second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "students demonstrated that they needed to know the mechanism of a reaction before they were able to assess whether the reaction involved nucleophiles and electrophiles or not."

## Misconception 303: If a student proposes a mechanism, whatever species is attacked in that proposed mechanism must be the electrophile, even if the mechanism is chemically impossible.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Incorrect mechanism determines labels
**Correct concept:** Nucleophile and electrophile labels must be based on actual reaction chemistry and feasible mechanism, not on a guessed or incorrect arrow-pushing scheme.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should test whether the proposed mechanism is chemically plausible; only then should they assign nucleophile/electrophile roles to real reacting species.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reasoning
**Prevalence:** Documented repeatedly; e.g., 4 students proposed an S_N1 mechanism for reaction A and identified a nonexistent carbocation intermediate as electrophile.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "their identification of the nucleophile and electrophile was based on that mechanism. Hence if the mechanism was incorrect, the students identified nonexistent intermediates as nucleophiles or electrophiles"

## Misconception 304: Any negatively charged species is a nucleophile and any positively charged species is an electrophile, regardless of the reaction context.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Charged species automatically imply nucleophile/electrophile
**Correct concept:** Charge is only a clue; the species must actually function as an electron-pair donor or acceptor in the specific reaction.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should consider the full reaction context, including whether the species is acting as a base, acid, or something else, and whether electron donation/acceptance occurs.
**Concept category:** charges
**Prevalence:** Repeated across multiple reactions; for reaction B, 8 students proposed ionic mechanisms and then assigned nucleophile/electrophile by charge.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "anytime there's a negative charge it's gonna be a nucleophile"

## Misconception 305: Hydroxide is always a nucleophile, even in proton-transfer reactions where it is functioning as a Brønsted base.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Conflating nucleophiles with Brønsted bases
**Correct concept:** A Brønsted base is identified by proton acceptance; a nucleophile is an electron-pair donor in bond-forming attack, and the two roles are not interchangeable.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should determine whether the reaction is a proton transfer or a nucleophilic attack, then assign roles accordingly; hydroxide may be a base rather than a nucleophile.
**Concept category:** acid-base vs nucleophile-electrophile
**Prevalence:** Multiple instances; the paper notes 13 instances of identifying a nucleophile without an electrophile, several involving hydroxide in acid-base reactions.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "I think this [hydroxide] is a nucleophile, pretty much sticking to that, um... just because I think I memorized it as a nucleophile."

## Misconception 306: If a nucleophile attacks a proton in an acid-base reaction, the proton donor/carboxylic acid must be the electrophile of the reaction.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Conflating electrophiles with Brønsted acids
**Correct concept:** In acid-base proton transfer, the reaction should be understood as Brønsted acid-base chemistry, not as a nucleophile-electrophile mechanism in the organic sense used in this study.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should first recognize proton transfer and the acid/base roles; labels of nucleophile/electrophile should not be forced onto every proton-transfer reaction.
**Concept category:** acid-base vs nucleophile-electrophile
**Prevalence:** Observed in multiple students' responses to reaction E.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
