**Evidence:** "And I believe this [hydroxide] is a nucleophile. Which by default makes this [carboxylic acid] the electrophile of the reaction."

## Misconception 307: Any carbonyl carbon is automatically an electrophile, even in a carboxylic acid where the functional group behaves differently from an aldehyde or ketone.

**Topic:** Nucleophiles and electrophiles / Treating carbonyl carbon as electrophile by default
**Correct concept:** Electrophilicity depends on the full functional group and reaction context; a carboxylic acid should be recognized as a proton donor in acid-base chemistry, not just as a generic carbonyl electrophile.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should identify the whole functional group and its reactivity before assigning electrophile status, rather than focusing only on the carbonyl carbon’s partial positive charge.
**Concept category:** functional groups
**Prevalence:** Several students misclassified reaction E when products were omitted.
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** Organic chemistry students' ideas about nucleophiles and electrophiles: the role of charges and mechanisms (c5rp00113g/c5rp00113g.md)
**Evidence:** "These students were so focused on recognizing the carbonyl group, that they failed to recognize that the carbonyl here is not a simple aldehyde or ketone, but rather part of a larger functional group with very different reactivity."

## Misconception 308: Students do not correctly predict the product of a given alcohol substitution or elimination reaction, often confusing which product should form from the reagent and starting alcohol.

**Topic:** Alcohol reactions / Product prediction
**Correct concept:** The product depends on the alcohol structure and the reagent/reaction conditions; students should identify the correct substitution or elimination product from the reaction pathway.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect the starting alcohol and reagent, determine whether the conditions favor substitution or elimination, then apply the corresponding reaction logic to predict the major product structure before drawing it.
**Concept category:** product prediction
**Prevalence:** Quiz mean product prediction score increased from 2.04 to 3.43 after gameplay.
**Population:** 42 second-semester introductory undergraduate organic chemistry students
**Source:** CHEMCompete-II: An Organic Chemistry Card Game To Differentiate between Substitution and Elimination Reactions of Alcohols (chemcompete-ii-an-organic-chemistry-card-game-to-differentiate-between-substitution-and-elimination-reactions-of/chemcompete-ii-an-organic-chemistry-card-game-to-differentiate-between-substitution-and-elimination-reactions-of.md)
**Evidence:** students were asked to (1) predict the product of a given reaction

## Misconception 309: Students struggle to classify alcohol reactions as substitution versus elimination, and to distinguish among SN1, SN2, E1, and E2 outcomes.

**Topic:** Alcohol reactions / Reaction classification
**Correct concept:** Students should classify a reaction by analyzing the alcohol type and reagent conditions to decide whether the reaction proceeds by substitution or elimination, and which specific mechanism is appropriate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the substrate class and reagent type to infer the reaction pathway, then assign the correct reaction type based on the mechanistic conditions rather than guessing from surface features.
**Concept category:** reaction classification
**Prevalence:** Quiz mean reaction classification score increased from 1.76 to 2.90 after gameplay.
**Population:** 42 second-semester introductory undergraduate organic chemistry students
**Source:** CHEMCompete-II: An Organic Chemistry Card Game To Differentiate between Substitution and Elimination Reactions of Alcohols (chemcompete-ii-an-organic-chemistry-card-game-to-differentiate-between-substitution-and-elimination-reactions-of/chemcompete-ii-an-organic-chemistry-card-game-to-differentiate-between-substitution-and-elimination-reactions-of.md)
**Evidence:** students were asked to (2) classify the reaction

## Misconception 310: Students cannot readily draw a reasonable mechanism for alcohol substitution or elimination reactions.

**Topic:** Alcohol reactions / Mechanisms
**Correct concept:** Students should draw a mechanistically reasonable sequence of electron-pushing steps that matches the identified substitution or elimination pathway.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect the classified reaction type to the mechanistic steps, then use curved arrows and intermediates consistent with the chosen SN1, SN2, E1, or E2 pathway to complete the mechanism.
**Concept category:** mechanisms
**Prevalence:** Quiz mean mechanism score increased from 4.49 to 7.24 after gameplay.
**Population:** 42 second-semester introductory undergraduate organic chemistry students
**Source:** CHEMCompete-II: An Organic Chemistry Card Game To Differentiate between Substitution and Elimination Reactions of Alcohols (chemcompete-ii-an-organic-chemistry-card-game-to-differentiate-between-substitution-and-elimination-reactions-of/chemcompete-ii-an-organic-chemistry-card-game-to-differentiate-between-substitution-and-elimination-reactions-of.md)
**Evidence:** students were asked to (3) draw a reasonable mechanism

## Misconception 311: Students treat bond formation as requiring energy input, and use that to explain slower reactions.

**Topic:** Organic reaction energetics / Bond formation vs. bond breaking
**Correct concept:** Bond breaking requires energy input, whereas bond formation releases energy; reaction rate should not be justified by saying a reaction is slower because it requires energy to form a bond.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish endothermic bond breaking from exothermic bond formation and connect reaction rate to the overall energy profile and activation barrier, not to a blanket rule that bond formation needs energy.
**Concept category:** bond energetics
**Prevalence:** mentioned as an unproductive resource in the analyzed student responses; no count given for this specific misconception
**Population:** introductory organic chemistry laboratory students at the University of Michigan
**Source:** Analysis of organic chemistry students' developing reasoning elicited by a scaffolded case comparison activity (d4rp00021h/d4rp00021h.md)
**Evidence:** “the idea that Reaction A proceeds faster ‘because it requires less energy to form the bond’”

## Misconception 312: Students think a leaving group leaves because it ‘will require more energy to leave,’ treating leaving-group departure as something the molecule must energetically pay for without correctly linking this to bond breaking and the reaction coordinate.

**Topic:** Organic reaction energetics / Energetics of leaving groups
**Correct concept:** A leaving group departs when the reaction pathway and bond energetics make departure favorable; students should connect leaving-group ability to bond strength, stabilization of the departing group, and the activation barrier, rather than a vague idea that departure simply requires more energy.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that weaker bonds and better stabilization of the leaving group lower the energy barrier for departure, which can facilitate a faster reaction.
**Concept category:** bond energetics
**Prevalence:** explicitly identified as part of developing energetics accounts; no separate count given for this misconception
**Population:** introductory organic chemistry laboratory students at the University of Michigan
**Source:** Analysis of organic chemistry students' developing reasoning elicited by a scaffolded case comparison activity (d4rp00021h/d4rp00021h.md)
**Evidence:** “the methoxy leaving group ‘will require more energy to leave’”

## Misconception 313: Students use a simplistic rule that stronger bonds mean slower reactions because more energy is required to break them, without qualifying that bond strength must be considered within the mechanism and energy landscape.

**Topic:** Organic reaction energetics / Bond strength and reaction speed
**Correct concept:** Bond breaking requires energy, and weaker bonds are generally easier to break, but reaction rate depends on the overall mechanism and activation energy, not just a single blanket bond-strength rule.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect bond strength to the activation barrier and show how bond weakening can lower the energy needed to reach the transition state.
**Concept category:** bond energetics
**Prevalence:** found in the developing energetics account example; no percentage beyond the example provided
**Population:** introductory organic chemistry laboratory students at the University of Michigan
**Source:** Analysis of organic chemistry students' developing reasoning elicited by a scaffolded case comparison activity (d4rp00021h/d4rp00021h.md)
**Evidence:** “The more energy required to break a bond, the slower the reaction.”

## Misconception 314: Students think a reaction is faster if it requires less energy to form a new bond, implying bond formation energy is the key determinant of rate in these mechanisms.

**Topic:** Organic reaction energetics / Reaction speed vs. energy to form a bond
**Correct concept:** Reaction rate should be explained through the activation energy of the rate-determining step and the overall energy profile, not by a simplified claim that less energy to form a bond automatically makes the reaction faster.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should relate the rate to the energy barrier to reach the transition state and identify which structural features reduce that barrier.
**Concept category:** bond energetics
**Prevalence:** embedded in the developing energetics account discussion; no count given
**Population:** introductory organic chemistry laboratory students at the University of Michigan
**Source:** Analysis of organic chemistry students' developing reasoning elicited by a scaffolded case comparison activity (d4rp00021h/d4rp00021h.md)
**Evidence:** “it requires less energy to form the bond to the oxygen of the alcohol group”

## Misconception 315: Students believe that a larger halogen atom is automatically a better leaving group without necessarily connecting size to a deeper explanation.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Leaving group size and leaving group ability
**Correct concept:** Leaving group ability should be justified by how structural and electronic properties affect bond breaking and stabilization of the departing group, not by size alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the halogens by asking whether a larger atomic radius leads to weaker C–X bonding and/or better stabilization of negative charge in the conjugate base, then relate those properties to easier departure.
**Concept category:** leaving group ability / size
**Prevalence:** 4 students selected Reaction 2 activated size
**Population:** Four second-semester organic chemistry students (Ivy, Shea, Ben, and Willam)
**Source:** That's not a super important point: secondsemester organic chemistry students' lines of reasoning when comparing substitution reactions (d4rp00086b/d4rp00086b.md)
**Evidence:** "Bromine is larger than chlorine, and that would make [the bromine] a better leaving group"

## Misconception 316: Students think the periodic trend in atomic radius by itself is sufficient to decide leaving group ability.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Leaving group size and periodic trend
**Correct concept:** Periodic trends are only useful when linked to bond strength and charge stabilization; size alone is not the endpoint of the explanation.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the fact that bromine is lower in the group to infer a larger radius, then explain how the larger atom better stabilizes the negative charge and weakens the C–Br bond, making leaving easier.
**Concept category:** surface features / periodic trends
**Prevalence:** 3 of the 4 students who reasoned with size used an undeveloped line of reasoning
**Population:** Second-semester organic chemistry students interviewed in the case-comparison task
**Source:** That's not a super important point: secondsemester organic chemistry students' lines of reasoning when comparing substitution reactions (d4rp00086b/d4rp00086b.md)
**Evidence:** "[The] atomic radius increases down the column. [The bromine] would be farther away from the carbon [and] easier to [...] leave."

## Misconception 317: Students believe that if chlorine is more electronegative than bromine, chlorine is automatically the better leaving group, or that electronegativity alone decides leaving group ability.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Electronegativity and leaving group ability
**Correct concept:** Electronegativity should be connected to charge stabilization and bond polarization/bond strength before concluding anything about leaving group ability.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that chlorine’s higher electronegativity can polarize the C–Cl bond and stabilize negative charge, then reason that this can weaken the bond and influence leaving group ability.
**Concept category:** electronegativity / leaving group ability
**Prevalence:** seen in Ivy's explanation; 2 students activated electronegativity for Reaction 2
**Population:** Ivy and Shea, second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** That's not a super important point: secondsemester organic chemistry students' lines of reasoning when comparing substitution reactions (d4rp00086b/d4rp00086b.md)
**Evidence:** "chlorine is more electronegative than bromine" and that "electronegativity could make [the chlorine] a better leaving group"

## Misconception 318: Students stop at saying an atom is more electronegative and treat that as a complete explanation.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Electronegativity without deeper links
**Correct concept:** Electronegativity is only the starting point; students should follow it through bond polarization, charge stabilization, bond strength, and ultimately leaving group ability or electrophilicity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should move from electronegativity to electron withdrawal, then to a more polarized/weaker bond or greater charge stabilization, and only then infer easier substitution or leaving group departure.
**Concept category:** electronegativity / developed reasoning
**Prevalence:** 5 students activated electronegativity for Reaction 1
**Population:** Brooke, Jade, Naomi, Chad, and Gigi, second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** That's not a super important point: secondsemester organic chemistry students' lines of reasoning when comparing substitution reactions (d4rp00086b/d4rp00086b.md)
**Evidence:** "Chlorine [...] is more electronegative. The bond between the chlorine and the carbon [is] more polarized and, probably as a result, easier to break."

## Misconception 319: Students think the carbonyl's induction effect can be treated as a vague charge shift without tracing how it makes the reacting carbon more electrophilic.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Induction effects and carbonyl reactivity
**Correct concept:** Induction effects should be used to explain electron withdrawal, charge stabilization/partial positive charge, and increased electrophilicity of the reacting carbon.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that the carbonyl withdraws electron density, increases partial positive character at the reacting carbon, and therefore makes the substrate more electrophilic and easier for the nucleophile to attack.
**Concept category:** induction effects / electrophilicity
**Prevalence:** 2 students (Violet and Brooke) noticed the carbonyl group and activated induction effects
**Population:** Violet and Brooke, second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** That's not a super important point: secondsemester organic chemistry students' lines of reasoning when comparing substitution reactions (d4rp00086b/d4rp00086b.md)
**Evidence:** "[The] oxygen [draws] negative charge away from the carbon [...]"

## Misconception 320: Students use steric hindrance or bulkiness as a standalone reason that one substrate is better or worse without linking it to the reaction pathway or bond breaking mechanism.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Sterics as a standalone justification
**Correct concept:** Steric effects must be tied to how crowding affects nucleophilic approach, transition state accessibility, or reaction pathway feasibility.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain how steric hindrance interferes with the incoming nucleophile’s access to the electrophilic carbon and thus affects activation energy or reaction rate.
**Concept category:** steric effects
**Prevalence:** 4 students activated steric effects for Reaction 1; 1 student activated steric effects for Reaction 2
**Population:** Brooke, Chad, Gigi, Morgan, and Willam
**Source:** That's not a super important point: secondsemester organic chemistry students' lines of reasoning when comparing substitution reactions (d4rp00086b/d4rp00086b.md)
**Evidence:** "[the sterics of the carbonyl] is not a super important point"

## Misconception 321: Students conflate steric effects with simple atom size, treating 'bigger' as equivalent to 'more hindered' without further justification.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Sterics and size conflation
**Correct concept:** Steric hindrance is a spatial accessibility issue, while size is an atomic/property trend; they are related but not identical and should be linked to reaction consequences explicitly.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish between atomic size and steric crowding, then explain whether bulk obstructs nucleophilic attack or leaving group departure in the given mechanism.
**Concept category:** surface features / steric effects
**Prevalence:** reported in students who activated undeveloped lines of reasoning for steric effects
**Population:** Gigi and Morgan, second-semester organic chemistry students
**Source:** That's not a super important point: secondsemester organic chemistry students' lines of reasoning when comparing substitution reactions (d4rp00086b/d4rp00086b.md)
**Evidence:** "[bromine is] just one atom"

## Misconception 322: Students think resonance creates a direct attraction between specific atoms that itself determines the outcome, rather than using resonance to explain delocalization and bond/electronic effects.

**Topic:** Substitution reactions / Resonance effects and charge attraction
**Correct concept:** Resonance should be interpreted as delocalization that changes charge distribution and electron density, which can alter reactivity indirectly.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should draw resonance forms, identify how electron density is delocalized, and then infer how that affects charge stabilization, bond character, or electrophilicity.
**Concept category:** resonance effects
**Prevalence:** 1 student (Morgan) activated resonance effects
**Population:** Morgan, second-semester organic chemistry student
**Source:** That's not a super important point: secondsemester organic chemistry students' lines of reasoning when comparing substitution reactions (d4rp00086b/d4rp00086b.md)
**Evidence:** "[A]ttraction happens" between the negative oxygen and the positive chlorine.

## Misconception 323: Students treat the substrate's substitution level as the main or only determinant of whether a reaction is SN1, SN2, E1, or E2, overriding other factors such as nucleophile/base strength, solvent, and temperature.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Substrate-centered reasoning
**Correct concept:** Reaction mechanism depends on a combination of substrate structure, nucleophile/base strength, solvent, temperature, and leaving group behavior, not substrate substitution level alone.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the full set of reaction conditions together: identify the substrate class, assess nucleophile/base strength and size, consider solvent and temperature, and then determine which mechanism best fits the combined evidence.
**Concept category:** substrate effects
**Prevalence:** Observed across all interviews; e.g., “five of the nine students cited the primary substrate as their justification” in the NaOH prompt.
**Population:** 9 Organic Chemistry I students interviewed
**Source:** Student conceptualizations and predictions of substitution and elimination reactions: what are they seeing on the page? (d4rp00204k/d4rp00204k.md)
**Evidence:** “students placed a greater emphasis on the importance of the starting substrate in the outcome of a reaction”

## Misconception 324: Students can identify a halogen or a leaving group on the substrate but do not understand the underlying reasons it is a good leaving group, treating the label itself as sufficient evidence.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Leaving-group reasoning
**Correct concept:** A good leaving group is effective because it can stabilize the charge after departure; recognizing a halogen is not enough without connecting to its electronic/stability properties.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain leaving-group ability in terms of stability of the departing species and its ability to accommodate negative charge, rather than just naming the halogen or saying it is a leaving group.
**Concept category:** leaving group function
**Prevalence:** Described as the dominant pattern for substrate reasoning; “most students simply noted the halogen's role as a leaving group”
**Population:** 9 Organic Chemistry I students interviewed
**Source:** Student conceptualizations and predictions of substitution and elimination reactions: what are they seeing on the page? (d4rp00204k/d4rp00204k.md)
**Evidence:** “students could identify leaving groups as strong or weak but often needed more support to explain the reasoning behind their judgments”

## Misconception 325: Students overgeneralize dissociation and think all nucleophiles should be split into ions, including weak or neutral species such as methanol, and then use the dissociated anion as the reactive nucleophile/base.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Nucleophile/base identification
**Correct concept:** Only species that actually dissociate in the reaction mixture should be treated as ions; a neutral reagent like methanol remains methanol and does not automatically become methoxide.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should determine the actual form of the species under the given conditions, identify which form is present in solution, and avoid assigning a charged nucleophile unless dissociation is justified.
**Concept category:** species dissociation / charge
**Prevalence:** Reported as a “common strategy” and “frequently” observed in the predict-the-product tasks.
**Population:** 9 Organic Chemistry I students interviewed
**Source:** Student conceptualizations and predictions of substitution and elimination reactions: what are they seeing on the page? (d4rp00204k/d4rp00204k.md)
**Evidence:** “students tended to over-apply this approach, dissociating all species-including weak acids like methanol”

## Misconception 326: Students assume methanol functions as methoxide in the reaction because they expect the product to contain the substituted alkoxy group, rather than identifying the actual nucleophilic species in solution.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Nucleophile identity
**Correct concept:** The nucleophile must be the species actually present and reactive in the reaction conditions; methanol is not automatically methoxide simply because the product contains an OCH3 group.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should separate the reagent listed from the reactive species generated under the conditions and identify the true nucleophile before predicting the mechanism.
**Concept category:** nucleophile identity
**Population:** Observed in the HOCH3 task; no exact count given.
**Source:** Student conceptualizations and predictions of substitution and elimination reactions: what are they seeing on the page? (d4rp00204k/d4rp00204k.md)
**Evidence:** “with methanol, although methoxide is not the nucleophilic species, students assumed it would be because it ultimately substitutes onto the compound”

## Misconception 327: Students use the presence of sodium or potassium as a shortcut for identifying SN2 or E2, instead of analyzing the nucleophile/base and conditions themselves.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Metal cation heuristic
**Correct concept:** Metal cations are not mechanistic indicators by themselves; the mechanism must be inferred from the actual nucleophile/base, substrate, solvent, and temperature.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should ignore the counterion as a mechanism cue and instead evaluate the reacting anion, substrate, and reaction conditions to determine whether substitution or elimination is favored.
**Concept category:** heuristic / surface feature
**Population:** A subset of the 9 Organic Chemistry I students interviewed
**Source:** Student conceptualizations and predictions of substitution and elimination reactions: what are they seeing on the page? (d4rp00204k/d4rp00204k.md)
**Evidence:** “some students associated S_N2 and E2 reactions with metal cations, such as sodium or potassium”

## Misconception 328: Students infer the reaction mechanism from whether the product contains a double bond, treating the presence or absence of a double bond as the primary confirmation of mechanism.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Double-bond as mechanism cue
**Correct concept:** The mechanism should be inferred from the substrate and reaction conditions; product unsaturation is a consequence to be explained, not the sole criterion for identifying the pathway.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the starting material and conditions to predict whether substitution or elimination is favored, then check whether the resulting product is consistent with that mechanism.
**Concept category:** product features / surface cues
**Prevalence:** Primary indicator used by several students; “several students inferred that an SN2 reaction had taken place ... simply because there was no double bond present in the product”
**Population:** 9 Organic Chemistry I students interviewed
**Source:** Student conceptualizations and predictions of substitution and elimination reactions: what are they seeing on the page? (d4rp00204k/d4rp00204k.md)
**Evidence:** “students consistently focused on the presence or absence of double bonds when reasoning about each reaction mechanism”

## Misconception 329: Students think that a primary substrate automatically means SN2, without considering whether the nucleophile is strong enough or whether other conditions might favor a different pathway.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Primary substrate heuristic
**Correct concept:** Primary substrates often favor SN2, but the mechanism still depends on the nucleophile/base and the full set of reaction conditions; it is not guaranteed solely by substrate degree.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the substrate as primary, then evaluate whether the nucleophile/base and solvent conditions support SN2 versus other pathways before deciding.
**Concept category:** substrate effects
**Prevalence:** One explicit example quoted; also “five of the nine students” used primary substrate as justification in the NaOH prompt.
**Population:** 9 Organic Chemistry I students interviewed
**Source:** Student conceptualizations and predictions of substitution and elimination reactions: what are they seeing on the page? (d4rp00204k/d4rp00204k.md)
**Evidence:** “Pam's dialogue suggested ... ‘this is a first degree, like primary carbocation, or like carbon... If it's on a first degree, it has to be S_N2.’”

## Misconception 330: Students justify their mechanism choice by pointing to the mechanism's own product or steps, rather than using independent evidence from substrate and conditions.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Circular justification
**Correct concept:** Mechanism choice should be justified from observable reaction features and chemical principles, not by circularly restating the mechanism outcome.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain why the substrate and reaction conditions favor a particular mechanism, and then derive the product from that mechanism rather than using the product as proof.
**Concept category:** circular reasoning / mechanistic explanation
**Prevalence:** Described as a “noteworthy observation” and “common trend” across both predict-the-product tasks.
**Population:** 9 Organic Chemistry I students interviewed
**Source:** Student conceptualizations and predictions of substitution and elimination reactions: what are they seeing on the page? (d4rp00204k/d4rp00204k.md)
**Evidence:** “students often based their explanations of the reaction mechanism on features of the substrate” and “the use of the predicted product itself to retroactively justify their choice of mechanism”

## Misconception 331: Students overlook nucleophile strength in mechanism choice and rely mainly on substrate features instead, even when nucleophile strength is critical.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Strength of nucleophile/base
**Correct concept:** Nucleophile/base strength is a key factor in deciding among SN1, SN2, E1, and E2 and must be considered alongside the substrate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare nucleophile/base strength, size, and concentration with substrate class and solvent/temperature to determine whether substitution or elimination is favored.
**Concept category:** nucleophile/base properties
**Prevalence:** “Only four students specifically considered nucleophile strength” in predict-the-product tasks; “Ben and Jan accounted for six out of the eight total references to nucleophile strength”
**Population:** 9 Organic Chemistry I students interviewed
**Source:** Student conceptualizations and predictions of substitution and elimination reactions: what are they seeing on the page? (d4rp00204k/d4rp00204k.md)
**Evidence:** “most students overlooked one of the nucleophile's critical features—its strength, which significantly influences its function in a reaction”

## Misconception 332: Students think the substrate alone determines the mechanism, especially by using only whether the carbon is primary, secondary, or tertiary, without attending to the nucleophile/base.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Substrate vs. reaction outcome
**Correct concept:** Reaction mechanisms are determined by the combination of substrate and reaction conditions, including nucleophile/base strength and other factors.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should examine both the substrate and the reagent conditions, then reason about how substrate substitution and nucleophile/base behavior together favor SN1, SN2, E1, or E2.
**Concept category:** substrate features
**Prevalence:** Observed across the sample; substrate was the most common feature students attended to, with 5/9 associating SN2 with a primary substrate and 6/9 associating SN1 with a tertiary substrate.
**Population:** 9 students in semi-structured interviews
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "students' focus is still largely centered only on the substrate of a reaction"

## Misconception 333: Students overgeneralize and dissociate the nucleophile/base into ions even when that is not chemically appropriate, such as treating methanol as methoxide.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Nucleophile/base dissociation
**Correct concept:** Students should identify the actual reacting species in solution and only dissociate species when the chemistry supports it.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should consider whether the reagent is actually ionic or neutral and whether dissociation into ions is chemically justified under the reaction conditions before using that as evidence for mechanism.
**Concept category:** reaction conditions
**Prevalence:** Described as a common nonnormative strategy seen in multiple students; the paper does not give an exact count.
**Population:** Multiple students in the interview sample
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "students tended to overapply this strategy and dissociate all species including weak acids like methanol"

## Misconception 334: Students identify leaving groups as strong or weak without being able to explain why, effectively treating the label as sufficient evidence.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Leaving group strength
**Correct concept:** A good leaving group is one whose stability and electronic properties allow it to depart readily; the label should be justified by chemical reasoning.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect leaving-group ability to underlying properties such as anion stability and the ability to accommodate negative charge, not just memorize that a halogen is a leaving group.
**Concept category:** leaving group properties
**Prevalence:** A large portion of students; exact percentage not given.
**Population:** Students in the 9-person interview sample
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "students generally were unable to identify why they were strong or weak, instead simply identifying them as strong or weak"

## Misconception 335: Students rely on memorized recognition that halides are good leaving groups instead of reasoning from underlying function.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Leaving group identity as a memorized heuristic
**Correct concept:** Leaving-group ability should be inferred from the species' ability to depart stably and its electronic structure.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should link the identity of the leaving group to stability and charge distribution rather than just recognizing halides by pattern.
**Concept category:** leaving group properties
**Prevalence:** Reported as a broad pattern in the data; no exact count given.
**Population:** Students in the interview sample
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "students have been trained to recognize halides as good leaving groups because of the common presence of them in reactions"

## Misconception 336: Students use pKa cutoffs or a memorized cutoff of five as a shortcut for deciding whether a reagent is strong or weak, rather than reasoning from the species itself.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Base/nucleophile strength
**Correct concept:** Strength of a base/nucleophile should be evaluated from the reagent's chemical properties and context, not by a single memorized cutoff alone.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should assess conjugate-acid pKa, charge, electronegativity, and structure in context to judge strength and likely role in the mechanism.
**Concept category:** reaction conditions
**Prevalence:** A common feature among students discussing reaction conditions, but exact percentage not provided.
**Population:** Students in the interview sample
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "students determined this largely based on the pKa of the species, specifically pointing to the cutoff of five that was given during lecture"

## Misconception 337: Students think the presence of sodium or potassium ions by itself indicates an SN2 or E2 reaction.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Metal cation heuristic
**Correct concept:** Metal cations are spectators; mechanism depends on the reactive nucleophile/base and the substrate, not the counterion alone.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should focus on the actual nucleophilic or basic species and the substrate, using the counterion only as a clue to reagent identity when relevant.
**Concept category:** heuristic cues
**Prevalence:** A few students
**Population:** A few students in the interview sample
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "a few students who specifically associate SN2 and E2 reactions with metal cations like sodium and potassium"

## Misconception 338: Students infer elimination solely from the presence of a double bond in the product, or infer SN2 solely from the absence of a double bond.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Double-bond cue for elimination
**Correct concept:** A double bond is a product feature of elimination, but mechanism should be inferred from multiple pieces of evidence including substrate and reagent behavior.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should integrate substrate structure, reagent strength, and product features rather than using double-bond presence or absence as a single निर्णायक cue.
**Concept category:** product features
**Prevalence:** Common across many students; exact percentage not provided.
**Population:** Students discussing case comparison tasks
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "if a double bond is being formed it has to be elimination, because that's what elimination does"

## Misconception 339: Students use the mechanism steps or the reaction type itself to justify their answer, creating circular reasoning instead of evidence-based explanation.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Mechanistic steps as justification
**Correct concept:** Students should justify a mechanism by citing substrate and reagent features that lead to that pathway, not by restating the mechanism name or steps as the reason.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain why the starting materials favor a particular pathway and then describe the steps as consequences of those features.
**Concept category:** mechanistic reasoning
**Prevalence:** Observed across the figure summaries and discussion; exact count not given.
**Population:** Multiple students in the exam-type tasks
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "students used the steps of the mechanism to justify why they chose a specific reaction type"

## Misconception 340: Students treat SN1 and E1 as interchangeable or hard to distinguish, sometimes selecting one from general memory rather than mechanistic evidence.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / SN1 and E1 relation
**Correct concept:** SN1 and E1 are distinct pathways that share some conditions but differ in product and mechanistic details.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare substrate, nucleophile/base strength, and product formation to determine whether substitution or elimination is favored.
**Concept category:** mechanistic process
**Prevalence:** Reported for some students in the mechanistic-process summary; exact count not fully specified.
**Population:** Students in the case comparison tasks
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "Hard to distinguish from E1"

## Misconception 341: Students fail to use nucleophile/base strength to distinguish pathways, instead relying mostly on substrate cues even when the reagent is the crucial factor.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / SN2/E2 versus nucleophile strength
**Correct concept:** Nucleophile/base strength is a key variable in deciding whether substitution or elimination occurs.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate reagent strength and size together with substrate substitution to decide whether SN2, E2, SN1, or E1 is favored.
**Concept category:** reaction conditions
**Prevalence:** Most students did not use nucleophile strength; only 4/9 attended to it in exam-type tasks, with Ben and Jan accounting for 6 of 8 references to nucleophile strength.
**Population:** 9 students in exam-type tasks
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "most of them are not seeing one of the most important features about the nucleophile, its strength"

## Misconception 342: Students think a less strong or protonated nucleophile automatically means SN1 or E1 without considering the rest of the mechanism.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Weak nucleophile heuristic
**Correct concept:** A weak nucleophile/base can favor SN1 or E1 under the right substrate and solvent conditions, but the conclusion must be based on the full set of reaction features.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should connect weak nucleophile/base behavior to carbocation-forming pathways only when the substrate and conditions support that route.
**Concept category:** reaction conditions
**Prevalence:** Illustrated in individual student reasoning; not quantified.
**Population:** Individual students such as Ben and others in the interviews
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "we have methanol. And in each case, we have a tertiary carbon here that the leaving group is attached to. So, that Br is going to leave initially"

## Misconception 343: Students use protic/aprotic labels as a heuristic for mechanism choice without connecting those labels to nucleophile function or solvent effects.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Protic/aprotic shortcut
**Correct concept:** Protic and aprotic environments affect nucleophile behavior and reaction pathways through their effects on solvation and reactivity.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should explain how solvent properties influence nucleophile strength and mechanism, rather than using the labels as standalone rules.
**Concept category:** reaction conditions
**Prevalence:** Seen in Phyllis and other students; exact count not given.
**Population:** Individual students in the case comparison tasks
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "She does not describe any features of each substrate or nucleophile with its ultimate function in the mechanism."

## Misconception 344: Students infer that a dashed bond means the stereochemistry will change, using the drawing convention as outcome evidence.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Stereochemistry from dash/wedge notation
**Correct concept:** Stereochemical outcome depends on the mechanism and substitution pattern, not simply on whether the starting structure contains a dashed bond.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should use the mechanism to reason about stereochemical change, then check whether the product's stereochemistry matches the expected pathway.
**Concept category:** stereochemistry
**Prevalence:** Observed in at least one student example (Lindsay).
**Population:** Individual student example
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "the dash becomes wedged"

## Misconception 345: Students think the absence of oxygen or nitrogen groups means hydrogen bonding is impossible, and use that alone to choose SN2 or E2.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Hydrogen-bonding cue
**Correct concept:** Hydrogen bonding depends on the actual species and solvent context; absence of O or N does not by itself determine the mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should analyze the actual reagent and solvent interactions, then relate those to nucleophile/base behavior and mechanism choice.
**Concept category:** reaction conditions
**Prevalence:** Individual student example; not quantified.
**Population:** Individual student example (Anne)
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "In this situation, hydrogen bonding is not possible. So that leaves SN2, E2."

## Misconception 346: Students believe a larger nucleophile/base automatically implies SN1 because it is too large to react in one step.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Species-size heuristic
**Correct concept:** Molecular size alone does not determine whether a reaction proceeds by SN1, SN2, E1, or E2; substrate structure and reagent strength/function matter.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should evaluate steric hindrance, nucleophile strength, and substrate substitution rather than using size as a standalone rule.
**Concept category:** heuristic cues
**Prevalence:** Individual student example; not quantified.
**Population:** Individual student example (Lucille)
**Source:** What are they seeing? How students predict and explain substitution and elimination reactions (document-6/document-6.md)
**Evidence:** "because, as she later explains, compounds that are too large cannot substitute in one step and need a second step to attack"

## Misconception 347: Students think SN1 and SN2 are not competing alternative mechanisms for the same reaction, but instead propose only one pathway and do not consider that both could be possible.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Reaction pathway structure
**Correct concept:** For a given reaction, more than one mechanistic pathway can be possible in principle; SN1 and SN2 are alternative mechanisms that may both occur depending on conditions and substrate.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare conditions, substrate structure, and kinetics to determine whether a stepwise or concerted pathway is more plausible, and recognize that mechanistic alternatives can coexist as possibilities rather than forcing a single absolute answer.
**Concept category:** duality of mechanisms
**Prevalence:** 0 groups out of 19 groups in Data Collection C explicitly discussed both mechanisms as possible
**Population:** 55 German high school students (23 groups overall; 19 groups evaluated the pre-made SMAs)
**Source:** Using Stop Motion Animations to Activate and Analyze High School Students' Intuitive Resources about Reaction Mechanisms (education-13-00759-v2/education-13-00759-v2.md)
**Evidence:** "no group discussed whether both mechanisms could be possible"

## Misconception 348: Students think reaction steps should happen one after another rather than simultaneously; concerted movement of nucleophile and leaving group is avoided or not recognized.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Concertedness
**Correct concept:** In an SN2 mechanism, nucleophile attack and leaving-group departure occur at the same time in a concerted process.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason that bond formation and bond breaking can be coupled in one elementary step, so the nucleophile approaches as the leaving group departs simultaneously.
**Concept category:** concertedness
**Prevalence:** 4 SMAs in category X showed simultaneous movement; 6 SMAs in BA showed sequential movement; 10 SMAs in BX/BA showed O approach and Br departure, but often not simultaneously
**Population:** 32 student-generated SMAs from 22 groups
**Source:** Using Stop Motion Animations to Activate and Analyze High School Students' Intuitive Resources about Reaction Mechanisms (education-13-00759-v2/education-13-00759-v2.md)
**Evidence:** "The simultaneous movement of Br and O is thus completely avoided."

## Misconception 349: Students think the nucleophile attacks from the same side where the leaving group is attached, rather than from the backside.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Backside attack
**Correct concept:** SN2 reactions proceed by backside attack due to steric/electrostatic requirements, leading to inversion at the carbon center.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should consider spatial arrangement and steric repulsion, and reason that the nucleophile approaches opposite the leaving group to allow bond formation while the leaving group leaves.
**Concept category:** steric effects / backside attack
**Prevalence:** All student-generated SN2-like SMAs showed same-side approach; backside attack was never shown
**Population:** 32 student-generated SMAs from 22 groups
**Source:** Using Stop Motion Animations to Activate and Analyze High School Students' Intuitive Resources about Reaction Mechanisms (education-13-00759-v2/education-13-00759-v2.md)
**Evidence:** "In contrast, in all student-generated SMAs, O approaches from the same side of the black ball that Br is bound to."

## Misconception 350: Students ignore or do not expect any movement of hydrogen atoms during carbocation formation or nucleophilic attack.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Hydrogen-atom movement
**Correct concept:** When molecular geometry changes during reaction, attached hydrogens may reposition as the bonding environment changes; mechanistic representations should account for all atom movements relevant to the structure change.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should track the geometry of the whole molecule, not only the leaving group and nucleophile, and recognize that all substituents on the carbon can change position when the central geometry changes.
**Concept category:** atom movement / geometry change
**Prevalence:** Hydrogen movement was not shown in the student-generated SMAs except in the OTHER category
**Population:** 32 student-generated SMAs from 22 groups
**Source:** Using Stop Motion Animations to Activate and Analyze High School Students' Intuitive Resources about Reaction Mechanisms (education-13-00759-v2/education-13-00759-v2.md)
**Evidence:** "none of the student-generated SMAs—except those in the 'OTHER' category that depict bond cleavage—shows any movement of the Hs."

## Misconception 351: Students think bonding can happen only at fixed, pre-existing 'places' or 'sites' on the atom surface, so a new bond must form exactly where another bond was removed.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Bonding sites on atoms
**Correct concept:** Bond formation is determined by molecular orbital/electrostatic interactions and geometry, not by fixed surface 'slots'; in SN2, the nucleophile approaches from the backside while the leaving group departs.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason from 3D molecular geometry and electron distribution, recognizing that atom surfaces do not contain fixed bond sockets and that reactive approach direction matters.
**Concept category:** bonding sites / surface features
**Prevalence:** 6 groups discussed 'bonding sites' or fixed places for bonds
**Population:** 14 groups mentioned chemical bonds in any context during Data Collection C
**Source:** Using Stop Motion Animations to Activate and Analyze High School Students' Intuitive Resources about Reaction Mechanisms (education-13-00759-v2/education-13-00759-v2.md)
**Evidence:** "many students somehow expect bonding to 'happen' only at specific places on an atomic surface."

## Misconception 352: Students think a carbon atom cannot transiently have five bonds, so any mechanism with five bonds is impossible.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Octet rule and pentavalency
**Correct concept:** In an SN2 reaction, the transition state can involve partial bonds and an apparent five-coordinate arrangement; the octet rule applies to stable structures, not necessarily to transition states.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should distinguish stable intermediates from transition states and understand that temporary bond formation/breaking can produce a high-energy arrangement that is not a stable pentavalent carbon compound.
**Concept category:** oxidation numbers / octet rule / bonding count
**Prevalence:** 4 groups focused on the number of chemical bonds; this argument was used both for and against SN2 mechanisms
**Population:** 14 groups mentioned chemical bonds in any context
**Source:** Using Stop Motion Animations to Activate and Analyze High School Students' Intuitive Resources about Reaction Mechanisms (education-13-00759-v2/education-13-00759-v2.md)
**Evidence:** "temporarily there are five bonds"

## Misconception 353: Students think bromine leaving without an obvious external cause is unrealistic or impossible, so a stepwise SN1-like departure seems implausible.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Causal plausibility of leaving-group departure
**Correct concept:** Leaving-group departure can occur because of internal energetic and electronic factors and stabilization by the environment; a visible external 'cause' is not always required in the model of the elementary step.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should reason from energetic favorability, bond polarization, and stabilization of the developing ion rather than from everyday 'cause-and-effect' expectations alone.
**Concept category:** plausibility / commonsense causality
**Prevalence:** 12 groups judged probability by plausibility; this argument was only used against SN1
**Population:** 19 groups that evaluated the pre-made SMAs
**Source:** Using Stop Motion Animations to Activate and Analyze High School Students' Intuitive Resources about Reaction Mechanisms (education-13-00759-v2/education-13-00759-v2.md)
**Evidence:** "it is not 'realistic' that bromine would leave 'just like that'"

## Misconception 354: Students treat alternative reaction pathways mainly as different intermediate-containing pathways rather than recognizing that concerted pathways can be alternatives too.

**Topic:** Nucleophilic substitution mechanisms / Intermediate formation as alternative pathway
**Correct concept:** Alternative mechanistic pathways can differ in whether they proceed stepwise or concertedly; not all alternatives require a distinct intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** Students should compare whether bond making and bond breaking happen together or in separate steps and should not assume that every alternative route must feature a new intermediate.
**Concept category:** intermediate formation
**Prevalence:** 7 of 32 SMAs were in the OTHER category; 5 of these involved intermediates
**Population:** 32 student-generated SMAs from 22 groups
**Source:** Using Stop Motion Animations to Activate and Analyze High School Students' Intuitive Resources about Reaction Mechanisms (education-13-00759-v2/education-13-00759-v2.md)
**Evidence:** "students thinking about alternative reaction pathways first of all think about other intermediates"

## Misconception 355: Students believe that organic chemistry is inherently too difficult to understand or succeed in.

**Topic:** organic chemistry / overall difficulty and success
**Correct concept:** Organic chemistry can be learned successfully with effective instruction, practice, and support; difficulty does not mean impossibility.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should treat difficulty as a sign to seek strategies such as early practice, help-seeking, and concept review rather than assuming they cannot learn the subject.
**Concept category:** general difficulty
**Population:** undergraduate students in organic chemistry (general discussion in the paper)
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "students still have difficulty understanding and comprehending the subject material"

## Misconception 356: Educators/students believe that instruction should be matched to a student's preferred learning style (visual, auditory, tactile) for effective learning.

**Topic:** learning styles / personalized instruction
**Correct concept:** The learning styles theory is not supported by empirical evidence; instruction should not be tailored on the assumption that students learn best only through one style.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that people can learn through multiple modes and that evidence does not justify assigning instruction by claimed learning style.
**Concept category:** learning styles
**Population:** educators and students discussed in the review of learning styles research
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "The theory of learning styles posits that there are specific ways that students learn best."

## Misconception 357: Students think that if a lesson does not match their learning style, they will not learn as effectively.

**Topic:** learning styles / effectiveness of style-matched instruction
**Correct concept:** Students can learn effectively through different instructional methods; matching instruction to a claimed learning style does not improve learning outcomes in the evidence cited.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should focus on engaging with the material and using multiple approaches rather than assuming only one preferred mode will work.
**Concept category:** learning styles
**Population:** participants in learning-styles research discussed in the paper
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "Theoretically, if a student learns best through one method, then they should not be able to learn as effectively using other methods."

## Misconception 358: Students believe intelligence is fixed and cannot be improved with effort.

**Topic:** growth mindset / intelligence as fixed trait
**Correct concept:** Intelligence can improve with effort, practice, and perseverance; a growth mindset is beneficial.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret difficulty as something to work through and improve upon rather than as proof of a permanent limit.
**Concept category:** mindset
**Population:** students with fixed mindset (general discussion)
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "those with a fixed mindset feel that intelligence is a fixed trait that cannot be improved"

## Misconception 359: Students think that struggling in a subject means they are not smart or capable enough to understand it.

**Topic:** growth mindset / self-judgment in learning
**Correct concept:** Struggle is part of learning; students can become capable through persistence, practice, and support.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should attribute setbacks to the need for more practice or better strategies, not to a lack of innate ability.
**Concept category:** mindset
**Population:** students in traditional learning environments (general discussion)
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "students can begin to think that they are just not smart or capable enough to understand such material."

## Misconception 360: People believe gamers typically play because they are lonely, have low self-esteem, or want to escape a miserable life.

**Topic:** gaming stereotypes / reasons for gaming
**Correct concept:** Gaming motivations are varied and often social, competitive, or enjoyable rather than reflecting social dysfunction.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should avoid inferring personality deficits from gaming and instead consider the diversity of motives for play.
**Concept category:** gaming stereotypes
**Population:** internet users/gamers as discussed in cited stereotype literature
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "those who lacked friendships, had low self-esteem, and looked to the internet as a means to escape a miserable life"

## Misconception 361: Students can succeed in organic chemistry without properly understanding foundational general chemistry topics such as acid-base chemistry, orbital hybridization, bonding, Lewis dot structures, and 3-D structures.

**Topic:** organic chemistry misconceptions / general chemistry foundations
**Correct concept:** Success in organic chemistry depends on understanding these foundational concepts first.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect organic chemistry mechanisms and structures back to prerequisite chemistry ideas before trying to memorize reactions.
**Concept category:** foundational concepts
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "students will face many challenges throughout the rest of the course"

## Misconception 362: Students think SN1 and SN2 reactions are simple to understand once they are shown the reactions, without needing to sort through the reactants, products, and variations carefully.

**Topic:** organic chemistry / SN1 and SN2 reactions
**Correct concept:** SN1 and SN2 mechanisms must be determined from the specific reactants, substrates, and conditions; the mechanism is not obvious without analysis.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare substrate structure, nucleophile, leaving group, and conditions to decide which substitution pathway applies.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanisms
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "It is pretty simple to understand what happens in the reactions once one determines which types of reaction takes place."

## Misconception 363: Students think E1 and E2 elimination reactions are essentially the same as SN1 and SN2 substitution reactions.

**Topic:** organic chemistry / E1 and E2 reactions
**Correct concept:** E1 and E2 are elimination reactions and differ from substitution reactions in mechanism and products.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish whether the reaction removes groups to form a double bond or substitutes one group for another, then analyze the mechanism accordingly.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanisms
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "E1 and E2 reactions are similar to S1 and S2 except they are two different kinds of elimination reactions."

## Misconception 364: Students think nomenclature is mainly about memorizing word parts rather than using them to systematically name structures by reading structural changes.

**Topic:** organic chemistry / nomenclature
**Correct concept:** Nomenclature is a systematic naming process based on recognizing structure, functional groups, and how changes in a molecule alter its name.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the structural features of a molecule and apply naming rules rather than rely on rote memorization alone.
**Concept category:** nomenclature
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "It can be difficult for students to remember word parts."

## Misconception 365: Students think Lewis dot structures can be drawn and understood without correctly accounting for valence electrons.

**Topic:** organic chemistry / Lewis dot structures
**Correct concept:** Lewis dot structures require correct counting and placement of valence electrons.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first determine the total valence electrons and then distribute them according to bonding and lone-pair rules to make a correct Lewis structure.
**Concept category:** electron structure
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "Without a proper understanding of valence electrons, lewis dot structures can be difficult to create and understand."

## Misconception 366: Students think 3-D molecular structures can be understood easily from 2-D drawings without needing to visualize the molecule in three dimensions.

**Topic:** organic chemistry / 3-D structures
**Correct concept:** Students must mentally transform and visualize structures in three dimensions from 2-D representations.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use spatial reasoning to interpret wedge/dash or other projections and connect 2-D depictions to the actual 3-D arrangement.
**Concept category:** spatial reasoning
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "Organic chemistry requires spatial intelligence in understanding how 3D structures look when drawn in 2D."

## Misconception 367: Students think functional groups are just add-ons to benzene rings and may not realize that adding them changes both structure and name.

**Topic:** organic chemistry / functional groups
**Correct concept:** Functional groups are structural features whose presence and position affect a molecule's structure and nomenclature.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the functional group, determine where it is attached, and then apply the naming rules that reflect that structural change.
**Concept category:** functional groups
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Game-Based Learning in Science: Can Video Games Simplify Organic Chemistry? (fulltext/fulltext.md)
**Evidence:** "When a functional group is added to a benzene ring, its name changes."

## Misconception 368: Students judge an SN1 reaction mainly by steric hindrance of the starting material, treating tertiary steric bulk as the reason a pathway is favored or disfavored in this SN1 context.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / SN1 feasibility and evidence selection
**Correct concept:** For SN1 plausibility, the key evidence is carbocation stability and the resulting activation energy of the rate-determining step, not steric hindrance of the starting material.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare the two substrates in terms of the stability of the carbocation intermediate that would form. A more stable carbocation lowers the activation energy for the rate-determining step, making the SN1 pathway more likely.
**Concept category:** reaction pathway selection / steric hindrance
**Prevalence:** Incorrect claims were often supported this way; the paper does not give a percentage for this misconception.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Getting Past the Rules and to the WHY: Causal Mechanistic Arguments When Judging the Plausibility of Organic Reaction Mechanisms (getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction/getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction.md)
**Evidence:** "Incorrect claims were often supported by causal arguments but these arguments were incorrectly based on drawing a causal relationship between the relative steric hindrance of the starting materials and activation energy."

## Misconception 369: Students use a memorized rule that tertiary alkyl halides react via SN1 and primary alkyl halides react via SN2, without reasoning about why the mechanism is favored in the specific problem.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / SN1 vs SN2 rule-based reasoning
**Correct concept:** Students should justify mechanism choice by reasoning from relevant mechanistic factors, especially carbocation stability and activation energy for SN1, rather than only applying a surface rule.
**Expected reasoning:** Use the reaction features to explain why a particular intermediate or transition state is more or less favorable. For SN1, explain that a tertiary substrate forms a more stable carbocation and therefore has a lower activation barrier than a primary substrate.
**Concept category:** rules-based reasoning / mechanism selection
**Prevalence:** Reported as one of the two common relational argument forms; no percentage given for this specific misconception.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Getting Past the Rules and to the WHY: Causal Mechanistic Arguments When Judging the Plausibility of Organic Reaction Mechanisms (getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction/getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction.md)
**Evidence:** "These arguments were rule-based in nature, generally explaining that tertiary alkyl halides react via an $S_N1$ mechanism while primary alkyl halides react via an $S_N2$ mechanism, without giving a reason why."

## Misconception 370: Students think a tertiary carbocation is more stable than a primary carbocation but do not connect that stability to a cause, such as hyperconjugation.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Carbocation stability
**Correct concept:** Tertiary carbocations are more stable because adjacent alkyl groups donate electron density through hyperconjugation; primary carbocations have fewer such stabilizing interactions.
**Expected reasoning:** Compare the number of adjacent C–H sigma bonds available for hyperconjugation. More adjacent methyl groups means more overlap with the empty p orbital, greater stabilization, and thus greater carbocation stability.
**Concept category:** carbocation stability / granularity
**Prevalence:** Few arguments invoked hyperconjugation; the paper does not report an exact percentage.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Getting Past the Rules and to the WHY: Causal Mechanistic Arguments When Judging the Plausibility of Organic Reaction Mechanisms (getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction/getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction.md)
**Evidence:** "Few arguments invoked hyperconjugation to explain why the tertiary carbocation is more stable than the primary; in other words, few arguments went down to the expected level of granularity."

## Misconception 371: Students explain the activation energy difference by saying it takes less energy to form a tertiary carbocation than a primary carbocation, without explicitly linking that to carbocation stability or to the transition state of the rate-determining step.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Activation energy and carbocation formation
**Correct concept:** Activation energy is connected to transition-state energy; for SN1, the transition state resembles the carbocation-like species, so greater carbocation stability lowers the activation barrier.
**Expected reasoning:** Reason from Hammond's postulate: because the rate-determining step is endergonic, the transition state resembles the carbocation intermediate. A more stable carbocation corresponds to a lower-energy transition state and a lower activation energy.
**Concept category:** activation energy / causal linkage
**Prevalence:** Described as one of the two common relational argument forms; no percentage given for this specific misconception.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Getting Past the Rules and to the WHY: Causal Mechanistic Arguments When Judging the Plausibility of Organic Reaction Mechanisms (getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction/getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction.md)
**Evidence:** "The other common relational argument discussed carbocation substitution and either energy or activation energy, generally explaining that it takes less energy to form a tertiary carbocation than a primary carbocation, without invoking stability as a cause."

## Misconception 372: Students argue that steric hindrance makes the activation energy barrier to product formation in reaction B higher, even though the prompt states both reactions proceed by SN1.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / Energy barrier for incorrect pathway
**Correct concept:** For the SN1 comparison in this question, the correct pathway is determined by carbocation stability and the associated activation barrier, not by steric hindrance leading to an SN2-style rationale.
**Expected reasoning:** Identify the relevant SN1 features, compare the carbocation intermediates that would form, and use their relative stability to infer the activation energy for the rate-determining step.
**Concept category:** steric hindrance / incorrect mechanistic rationale
**Prevalence:** The most common causal argument for incorrect claims; no exact percentage given.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Getting Past the Rules and to the WHY: Causal Mechanistic Arguments When Judging the Plausibility of Organic Reaction Mechanisms (getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction/getting-past-the-rules-and-to-the-why-causal-mechanistic-arguments-when-judging-the-plausibility-of-organic-reaction.md)
**Evidence:** "the most common causal argument was centered on the idea of steric hindrance, which was incorrectly used to justify why the activation energy barrier to the formation of the product in reaction B would be higher"

## Misconception 373: Curved arrows are just a means to get from reactants to products and have no physical meaning.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / purpose of curved-arrow notation
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows represent electron flow and are meant to explain the mechanistic process by which reactants are transformed into products.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret each arrow as showing movement of electrons from a source to a sink and use it to justify why each bond breaks or forms in a chemically plausible sequence.
**Concept category:** electron-pushing formalism
**Prevalence:** described as the dominant pattern across the participants; no exact percentage given
**Population:** 14 graduate students in a first-semester graduate-level organic chemistry course
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "The curved arrows used in the electron-pushing formalism held no physical meaning for the graduate students involved in this study."

## Misconception 374: Students think mechanism solving is just 'playing around' or forcing steps until they fit the product.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / mechanism construction strategy
**Correct concept:** Mechanism proposal should be based on chemically justified electron flow and reaction principles, not trial-and-error forcing.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should test whether each proposed step is consistent with reactivity, charge movement, and known mechanistic pathways before accepting it.
**Concept category:** problem-solving strategy
**Population:** Graduate students interviewed in the study
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "It's basically just playing around"

## Misconception 375: Students will accept nonsensical or highly unstable intermediates if those intermediates seem to lead toward the product.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / intermediate selection
**Correct concept:** Intermediates must be chemically reasonable and consistent with reaction energetics and known mechanisms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reject intermediates that are too unstable or incompatible with the reaction conditions, even if they superficially connect reactants to products.
**Concept category:** intermediate reasoning
**Prevalence:** all of the participants
**Population:** 14 graduate students
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "all of the participants generated the highly unstable phenyl cation intermediate"

## Misconception 376: Students think protonation should occur on the ester oxygen rather than the carbonyl oxygen in ester saponification.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / ester saponification
**Correct concept:** In these reactions, protonation of the carbonyl oxygen is expected because it better stabilizes the resulting cationic intermediate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the site where protonation gives the more stabilized cation and better activates the substrate for subsequent reaction.
**Concept category:** regioselectivity in protonation
**Prevalence:** most of the participants; exact percentage not given
**Population:** Graduate students interviewed on two ester saponification problems
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "Most of the participants in this study protonated the ester oxygen in both questions that dealt with ester saponification reactions. Protonation at the carbonyl oxygen, however, would have been expected in both cases"

## Misconception 377: Students treat an ester as a carbonyl attached to an ether rather than as a single functional unit with its own characteristic reactivity.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / ester structure and reactivity
**Correct concept:** An ester should be recognized as one functional group whose reactivity is governed by the whole ester structure, not as two independent fragments.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should analyze the ester's overall functional-group behavior and apply ester-specific reactivity patterns when predicting mechanism steps.
**Concept category:** functional group recognition
**Prevalence:** frequently
**Population:** Graduate students in the study
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "the students frequently talked about the ester as if it were a carbonyl attached to an ether, rather than as a single unit that would have a characteristic reactivity."

## Misconception 378: Students try to use vinyl group electrons to attack a cyclobutane ring and open it directly.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / bond reorganization in rearrangements
**Correct concept:** The correct pathway requires a preparatory electrocyclic ring opening before the Diels–Alder step; the vinyl electrons should not be used to directly break the unactivated C–C bond.
