**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize when a reaction requires an initial reorganization step that generates the reactive intermediate needed for the next transformation.
**Concept category:** reaction pathway selection
**Prevalence:** 12 of the 14 participants
**Population:** 14 graduate students
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "12 of the 14 participants tried to do the same thing, using the vinyl group electrons to attack the four-membered ring."

## Misconception 379: Students ignore preparatory steps because those steps do not immediately generate product.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / preparatory steps
**Correct concept:** Some mechanisms require preparatory transformations before the key bond-forming step can occur.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should consider whether a seemingly indirect step is necessary to set up the next mechanistic event, even if it does not itself resemble the final product change.
**Concept category:** planning and sequence of steps
**Population:** Graduate students in the study
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "Because they focused upon only those steps that would generate some aspect of the product, the participants were unlikely to take the necessary steps used to prepare for another, often key, step."

## Misconception 380: Students stop after deprotonation if they cannot immediately see that it leads to product formation.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / deprotonation as preparatory chemistry
**Correct concept:** Deprotonation can be a necessary preparatory step that enables a later mechanistic event, such as acting as a base in the subsequent step.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should ask what role the deprotonated species will play next rather than dismissing the step because it is not immediately productive.
**Concept category:** preparatory steps
**Population:** Graduate students in the study
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "Then I don't know, it's going to reprotonate or something. That's not, but that's not. What I'm saying is that's not getting me towards a product."

## Misconception 381: Students think atoms in starting material and product are static and cannot move or be reoriented in space.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / molecular mapping
**Correct concept:** Molecules can be rotated, flipped, and conformationally reoriented when mapping starting materials to products; atom correspondences are not fixed by the drawing.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that structural drawings can represent the same molecule in different orientations and should map atoms based on connectivity and transformation, not on fixed page positions.
**Concept category:** molecular mapping
**Population:** Graduate students in the study
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "I automatically labeled this oxygen 1 and oxygen 2 like they couldn't move. And that's silly."

## Misconception 382: Students cannot determine the first intermediate or 'cue' for how to begin connecting arrows and therefore struggle to start the problem.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / mechanism initiation
**Correct concept:** A mechanism should begin with a chemically justified first step based on substrate reactivity and reaction conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the initial reactive event from the structure and conditions, then build the mechanism from that first plausible intermediate.
**Concept category:** mechanism initiation
**Population:** Graduate students in the study
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "The students' inability to determine the first intermediate ... was one reason why participants often found it hard to even start a problem."

## Misconception 383: Students identify a reaction type first and then use arrows to force the mechanism to match that label.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / reaction-type labeling
**Correct concept:** Reaction type should be inferred from the actual chemistry and then used to justify each step, not imposed mechanically on the substrate.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the substrate and reagents to determine whether a proposed elimination, substitution, or cycloaddition is chemically appropriate before drawing arrows.
**Concept category:** reaction classification
**Population:** Graduate students in the study
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "I knew I had to get rid of a hydrogen, through an elimination reaction to form this double bond."

## Misconception 384: Students do not correlate curved arrows with underlying chemical principles; they see bonds moving rather than electron flow and mechanism.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / arrow symbolism and chemical concepts
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows should be tied to fundamental chemical concepts such as nucleophilicity, basicity, proton transfer, and bond-making/bond-breaking events.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use the concepts behind the arrows to explain why each electron movement is allowed and what driving force makes the step work.
**Concept category:** conceptual interpretation of arrows
**Population:** Graduate students in the study
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "they need to correlate the curved arrows they draw with the fundamental principles of chemistry"

## Misconception 385: Students can define Brønsted bases and nucleophiles but cannot distinguish them in applying them to mechanism problems.

**Topic:** Organic reaction mechanisms / basicity vs nucleophilicity
**Correct concept:** Basicity and nucleophilicity are different concepts and must be applied appropriately depending on whether the step involves proton abstraction or attack on an electrophilic atom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should decide whether a species is acting as a base or a nucleophile by examining the reaction context and the target of attack, not by memorized definitions alone.
**Concept category:** acid-base vs nucleophile distinctions
**Prevalence:** often; exact count not given
**Population:** Graduate students in the study
**Source:** "It Gets Me to the Product": How Students Propose Organic Mechanisms (it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms/it-gets-me-to-the-product-how-students-propose-organic-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "they could readily define the concepts of Brønsted bases and nucleophiles but were often hard pressed when asked to differentiate between the two while solving a problem"

## Misconception 386: Students think a halide is a good leaving group simply because it is a halide.

**Topic:** Leaving group ability / Halide leaving groups
**Correct concept:** Leaving group ability depends on the stability of the species after departure, especially whether the conjugate base is weak and stabilized by size, charge distribution, resonance/induction, and other structural factors.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate why the conjugate base is stable and weakly basic, then use structural/electronic factors to compare leaving groups rather than labeling a halide as good by name alone.
**Concept category:** leaving group ability
**Prevalence:** Several students; described as a commonly offered explanation. The paper does not give an exact count for this misconception.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students (n=36)
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Understandings of What Makes a Good Leaving Group (organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group/organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group.md)
**Evidence:** "because it's a halide. I just know that halides are very good leaving groups."

## Misconception 387: Students think electronegativity is the primary or sole reason halides are good leaving groups.

**Topic:** Leaving group ability / Halide leaving groups
**Correct concept:** Electronegativity can matter in some comparisons, but for halides size is more important; fluoride is actually the worst leaving group among halides despite being most electronegative.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare halides by considering size first, recognizing that the larger halides better stabilize negative charge and form weaker C–X bonds; electronegativity alone is not sufficient across atoms of very different sizes.
**Concept category:** electronegativity
**Prevalence:** Nineteen students provided explanations showing this or related incomplete reasoning about halide leaving groups.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students who discussed halide leaving groups
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Understandings of What Makes a Good Leaving Group (organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group/organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group.md)
**Evidence:** "students, however, argued that electronegativity was the primary consideration to explain why halides are good leaving groups"

## Misconception 388: Students believe good leaving groups are good because they gain an octet when they leave, implying octet acquisition is the key stabilizing factor.

**Topic:** Leaving group ability / Halide leaving groups
**Correct concept:** Halides already have an octet before leaving; leaving group ability is not explained by 'gaining an octet' but by how stable and weakly basic the leaving species is after bond cleavage.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should realize that halide ions already satisfy the octet rule and should instead reason about charge stabilization, size, and bond strength in the conjugate base and the C–X bond.
**Concept category:** octet rule / stability
**Prevalence:** Discussed as a common explanation among students; the paper does not provide an exact count.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Understandings of What Makes a Good Leaving Group (organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group/organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group.md)
**Evidence:** "they thought that a covalently bonded bromine atom was not as stable because it 'shared' electrons, rather than 'having all of the electrons to itself once it has left'"

## Misconception 389: Students think halides are good leaving groups because they acquire a full octet and become stable anions after departure, even though the halide already had an octet while bonded.

**Topic:** Leaving group ability / Halide leaving groups
**Correct concept:** The departing halide already has an octet; the correct explanation is that the resulting anion is weakly basic and stabilized by size and other factors, not that octet completion occurs upon leaving.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should track electron counts before and after bond cleavage and recognize that the electronic stabilization comes from being a weak base with a stabilized negative charge, not from octet completion.
**Concept category:** octet rule / stability
**Prevalence:** Multiple students; the paper identifies this as another commonly offered explanation.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Understandings of What Makes a Good Leaving Group (organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group/organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group.md)
**Evidence:** "if it gets one more electron to fill the second shell, it becomes more stable than if it had seven in that second valence shell when it was neutral."

## Misconception 390: Students think water is a good leaving group simply because it is uncharged.

**Topic:** Leaving group ability / Water as a leaving group
**Correct concept:** Water is a good leaving group because protonation converts hydroxide into neutral water, which is the conjugate base of a strong acid and is therefore a weak base and relatively stable after departure.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect protonation to formation of neutral water, then reason that the neutral product is the weak conjugate base of a strong acid and therefore can leave more readily.
**Concept category:** charge and stability
**Prevalence:** Several students focused on this idea; no exact count is given.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Understandings of What Makes a Good Leaving Group (organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group/organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group.md)
**Evidence:** "water, which can be a pretty good leaving group, because it's pretty stable itself because it's an uncharged species."

## Misconception 391: Students think water is a good leaving group because it gains an octet when it leaves.

**Topic:** Leaving group ability / Water as a leaving group
**Correct concept:** Water leaving ability should be explained by the stability and weak basicity of the neutral leaving group after protonation, not by octet gain.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should see that water already has a filled valence shell and that the important issue is conversion of a poor leaving group (OH-) into a better one (H2O) by protonation.
**Concept category:** octet rule / stability
**Prevalence:** Several students focused on this idea; no exact count is given.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Understandings of What Makes a Good Leaving Group (organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group/organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group.md)
**Evidence:** "when it's protonated it becomes, it's easier to leave... it would not have enough electrons to fulfill an octet."

## Misconception 392: Students think leaving group ability is an absolute binary property: a species is simply 'good' or 'bad' rather than varying along a continuum.

**Topic:** Leaving group ability / Relative comparison of leaving groups
**Correct concept:** Leaving group ability is relative; some leaving groups are extremely poor, some are very good, and their abilities depend on comparative stability and basicity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare leaving groups against each other using relative factors such as conjugate-base strength, charge stabilization, and structure, not memorize fixed labels.
**Concept category:** dualistic thinking / relative reasoning
**Prevalence:** More than 30% of the students in the sample were limited to making assertions without explanation and expressed ideas in absolute terms.
**Population:** Study participants (n=36), especially those making unsupported assertions about leaving groups
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Understandings of What Makes a Good Leaving Group (organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group/organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group.md)
**Evidence:** "They did not discuss the relative nature of leaving group ability, e.g., the continuum from extremely poor leaving groups (very strong bases) to very good leaving groups (very weak bases)."

## Misconception 393: Students believe that if they learned a species was a good or bad leaving group in class, that memorized label is sufficient and no explanation is needed.

**Topic:** Leaving group ability / Unsupported memorized labels
**Correct concept:** Students should be able to justify leaving group ability using underlying chemical reasoning, not only recall taught labels.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should explain the chemical basis for the classification, such as weak basicity and stabilization of the departing group, rather than stopping at 'that's what we learned.'
**Concept category:** rote memorization
**Prevalence:** Ten students made assertions about leaving groups but were unable to provide any explanation.
**Population:** Organic Chemistry II students
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Understandings of What Makes a Good Leaving Group (organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group/organic-chemistry-students-understandings-of-what-makes-a-good-leaving-group.md)
**Evidence:** "It's just these certain things that you are taught and you don't really remember why they are the way they are."

## Misconception 394: Students believe that if a molecule has no formal charges, it will not react; formal charge presence/absence is taken as the direct determinant of reactivity.

**Topic:** organic reactivity / formal charge as the only criterion for reactivity
**Correct concept:** Reactivity depends on the distribution of electron density, nucleophilicity/electrophilicity, and electrostatic interactions; neutral molecules can still react if partial charges and polar bonds create a favorable reactive site.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should examine bond polarity, partial charges, electron-rich and electron-poor sites, and leaving group ability to decide whether a reaction is possible rather than requiring explicit formal charges.
**Concept category:** formal charges
**Prevalence:** 5 students consistently invoked formal charges to determine chemical reactivity.
**Population:** 17 first-semester organic chemistry students interviewed at a research-intensive university
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Use of Electrostatics to Explain Reactivity (organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity/organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity.md)
**Evidence:** "if you don't have any positive or negative charges, nothing's going to move... and if everything's already neutral, it has no reason to move and react."

## Misconception 395: Students believe that a nucleophile/reactant is strong enough to react only when it bears a formal charge, often linked to metal counterions; neutral nucleophiles are treated as too weak or not reactive.

**Topic:** organic reactivity / formal charge as a proxy for nucleophile strength
**Correct concept:** Nucleophilicity and reactivity are not determined solely by formal charge; neutral species can be nucleophilic if their electron density and bonding environment make them able to attack an electrophile.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate lone pairs, electron density, bond polarity, leaving groups, and the electrophile rather than equating nucleophile strength with the presence of a formal charge or metal association.
**Concept category:** formal charges
**Prevalence:** 5 students consistently invoked formal charges to determine chemical reactivity.
**Population:** 17 first-semester organic chemistry students interviewed at a research-intensive university
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Use of Electrostatics to Explain Reactivity (organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity/organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity.md)
**Evidence:** "NH2 isn't like a strong base in this case because it's not like with a metal."

## Misconception 396: Students believe that a molecule without formal charges is automatically stable and therefore unreactive.

**Topic:** organic reactivity / stability equated with no formal charges
**Correct concept:** Molecular stability and reactivity depend on the full electrostatic and structural context, including partial charges, electronegativity, and the presence of electrophilic sites; neutral molecules may still react.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should not stop at charge neutrality; they should identify whether there are polar bonds, partial charges, leaving groups, or other features that create a reaction pathway.
**Concept category:** formal charges
**Prevalence:** 5 students consistently invoked formal charges to determine chemical reactivity.
**Population:** 17 first-semester organic chemistry students interviewed at a research-intensive university
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Use of Electrostatics to Explain Reactivity (organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity/organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity.md)
**Evidence:** "it's stable on its own. There's nothing that can really attack"

## Misconception 397: Students believe that nitrogen-containing nucleophiles are only reactive when bonded to a metal and carrying a negative formal charge; neutral nitrogen species are not viewed as reactive.

**Topic:** organic reactivity / formal charge only on metalated species
**Correct concept:** Nitrogen-containing species can be reactive in their neutral form depending on lone pair availability, protonation state, and the electrophile; a metal counterion is not required for all nucleophilic reactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should assess the availability of a lone pair and the electron density on nitrogen, then compare it with the electrophilic center, instead of requiring a metal-derived formal charge.
**Concept category:** formal charges
**Prevalence:** Observed in the formal-charge approach; specific student example Bailey.
**Population:** 17 first-semester organic chemistry students interviewed at a research-intensive university
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Use of Electrostatics to Explain Reactivity (organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity/organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity.md)
**Evidence:** "NH2 isn't like a strong base in this case because it's not like with a metal."

## Misconception 398: Students believe reactivity is something to recall from class experience or memorized patterns rather than infer from chemical principles.

**Topic:** organic reactivity / memorized reaction patterns instead of conceptual reasoning
**Correct concept:** Reaction likelihood should be reasoned from mechanistic principles such as nucleophile/electrophile interactions, bond polarity, leaving group ability, and partial charges.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use structural features and electrostatics to predict reactivity, then justify the reaction using mechanistic logic rather than saying it is familiar from prior examples.
**Concept category:** memorized reaction patterns
**Prevalence:** 4 students consistently invoked memorized heuristics when determining reactivity.
**Population:** 17 first-semester organic chemistry students interviewed at a research-intensive university
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Use of Electrostatics to Explain Reactivity (organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity/organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity.md)
**Evidence:** "it's just something that I think that might happen... just from experience, just like doing and going to class"

## Misconception 399: Students treat electronegativity alone as the explanation for intermolecular attraction, often using it as a direct cause of dipole-dipole forces without articulating partial charges or dipoles clearly.

**Topic:** intermolecular forces / electronegativity directly causes dipole-dipole attraction without explicit partial-charge reasoning
**Correct concept:** Electronegativity differences create bond polarity and partial charges, which produce molecular dipoles; those dipoles are what underlie dipole-dipole intermolecular forces.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect electronegativity differences to partial charge development and then to the attraction between opposite partial charges on neighboring molecules.
**Concept category:** electronegativity / partial charges
**Prevalence:** Many students used electronegativity for intermolecular forces; specific example in Bailey's and others' explanations.
**Population:** 17 first-semester organic chemistry students interviewed at a research-intensive university
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Use of Electrostatics to Explain Reactivity (organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity/organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity.md)
**Evidence:** "I think it's the electron density, like, cloud moving towards the more electronegative because it has, like, it takes more."

## Misconception 400: Students think the electrostatics used for intermolecular forces do not apply to reactivity; they separate formal-charge reasoning for reactivity from partial-charge reasoning for intermolecular forces.

**Topic:** organic reactivity / formal charges and partial charges treated as separate, non-transferable ideas
**Correct concept:** The same electrostatic principles underpin both intermolecular forces and reactivity; partial charges, formal charges, and electron density are related and can all inform where reactions occur.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should transfer the idea that opposite charges attract and electron density matters from intermolecular forces into reaction reasoning to locate nucleophilic and electrophilic sites.
**Concept category:** context transfer / electrostatics
**Prevalence:** Described as a major pattern; most students varied their approaches between contexts, while only 3 students used electrostatics consistently across both.
**Population:** 17 first-semester organic chemistry students interviewed at a research-intensive university
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Use of Electrostatics to Explain Reactivity (organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity/organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity.md)
**Evidence:** "students varied their approaches between the two contexts"

## Misconception 401: Students believe that neutral molecules have no reason to move or react because they lack charges.

**Topic:** organic reactivity / neutrality as the reason molecules cannot react
**Correct concept:** Neutral molecules can react when there is an electron-rich site and an electron-poor site; reactivity is driven by local partial charges and orbital/electrostatic complementarity, not by the mere absence of net charge.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should examine the distribution of electrons and partial charges within neutral molecules and identify reactive centers even when both molecules are overall neutral.
**Concept category:** formal charges
**Prevalence:** Observed in the change-in-approach group, especially Trenton early in the interview.
**Population:** 17 first-semester organic chemistry students interviewed at a research-intensive university
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Use of Electrostatics to Explain Reactivity (organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity/organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity.md)
**Evidence:** "If you don't have any positive or negative charges, nothing's going to move to become neutral. And if everything's already neutral, it has no reason to move and react."

## Misconception 402: Students infer that symmetrical molecules or electron density spread across a molecule means the molecule will not react.

**Topic:** organic reactivity / symmetry or spread-out electrons imply no reactivity
**Correct concept:** Symmetry can affect how charges are distributed, but it does not by itself determine whether a molecule is reactive; reactivity depends on the specific presence and accessibility of electrophilic and nucleophilic sites.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use symmetry only as one clue about charge distribution and then check for electrophilic centers, leaving groups, and partial charges rather than concluding nonreactivity from symmetry alone.
**Concept category:** electron density / symmetry
**Prevalence:** Two students who changed approaches discussed the nonpolar set this way.
**Population:** 17 first-semester organic chemistry students interviewed at a research-intensive university
**Source:** Organic Chemistry Students' Use of Electrostatics to Explain Reactivity (organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity/organic-chemistry-students-use-of-electrostatics-to-explain-reactivity.md)
**Evidence:** "I don't think they would react [...] symmetrical [...] I'm seeing a lot of electron density [...] all of those electrons [are] more spread out."

## Misconception 403: Students think that if an intermediate is 'stable' it will be unreactive and therefore stop the reaction from proceeding.

**Topic:** organic reaction mechanisms / stable intermediates and reaction progress
**Correct concept:** Reaction pathways are favored by energetically accessible intermediates and lower activation barriers, not by assuming a stable intermediate cannot continue reacting.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare energy barriers and recognize that an intermediate can be relatively low in energy yet still react further if the next step is accessible; stability does not mean inertness.
**Concept category:** energetics/intermediates
**Population:** students in an introductory first-year organic chemistry course
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "many students get confused by the idea of a 'stable intermediate', thinking that if it is stable then it will somehow be unreactive, and therefore stop the reaction proceeding."

## Misconception 404: Students move the curly arrow directly to the positive charge on a heteroatom, treating it like a carbocation or protonated carbon.

**Topic:** arrow-pushing formalism / positively charged heteroatoms
**Correct concept:** A positively charged heteroatom already has an octet; the correct mechanism is usually stepwise, attacking an adjacent atom and then restoring the lone pair on the heteroatom.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use formal charge and octet reasoning: the heteroatom is electron-poor but not octet-deficient, so electrons are not pushed 'into' the positive charge directly; instead, they attack the adjacent electrophilic site and then flow back to the heteroatom.
**Concept category:** formal charge / electron-pushing
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "Many students want to move the curly arrow directly to the positive charge (as with  $C^+$  and  $H^+$ )—indeed, this is a major mechanistic misconception among students."

## Misconception 405: Students think a base should attack a positively charged carbon directly in electrophilic aromatic substitution, or that it can remove the hydrogen attached to the positively charged carbon to complete deprotonation.

**Topic:** reaction selectivity / nucleophiles vs bases attacking electrophilic sites
**Correct concept:** In electrophilic aromatic substitution, the base removes the appropriate proton to restore aromaticity; it does not directly attack the cationic carbon or remove the proton in a way that merely reproduces the same 6-electron cation without progressing the mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish base behavior from nucleophile behavior and use mechanism progression: the base abstracts a proton that allows electrons to re-form the aromatic system, rather than attacking the positively charged carbon itself.
**Concept category:** nucleophile/base distinction
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "A common mistake here is to attack C<sup>+</sup> directly with the base... A second common mistake is to remove the hydrogen attached to C+ ."

## Misconception 406: Students assume that more electronegative atoms are better electron donors / more reactive nucleophiles because they are associated with charge.

**Topic:** nucleophilicity and electronegativity / electronegativity as a predictor of electron donation
**Correct concept:** Less electronegative atoms are generally better electron pair donors and thus more reactive nucleophiles because they hold electron density less tightly and overlap orbitals more effectively.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that nucleophilicity depends on how readily a lone pair can be donated; higher electronegativity means stronger attraction to the electron pair and poorer donation, while lower electronegativity makes donation easier.
**Concept category:** nucleophilicity/selectivity
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "Students find this intuitively very surprising, as they associate electronegativity with charge and hence with reactivity. Indeed, this is a significant misconception in mechanistic chemistry."

## Misconception 407: Students think bulky electron-rich species are equally good nucleophiles and bases, or do not distinguish steric effects on nucleophilic attack from proton abstraction.

**Topic:** reaction selectivity / steric effects on nucleophiles versus bases
**Correct concept:** Bulky reagents are less effective nucleophiles because steric hindrance impedes approach to carbon, but they may still act as bases because proton abstraction is less sterically demanding.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the size of the reacting site: nucleophilic attack on carbon is kinetically hindered by bulk, whereas removal of a small proton is less affected, so bulky reagents preferentially behave as bases.
**Concept category:** sterics/nucleophile-base competition
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "In general, less bulky reagents will be better able to donate a pair of electrons at the start of a mechanism."

## Misconception 408: Students think amides are at least as reactive as esters, or do not recognize that amides are significantly less reactive toward nucleophilic attack.

**Topic:** functional group reactivity / amide vs ester electrophilicity
**Correct concept:** Amides are less reactive than esters because the nitrogen lone pair donates by mesomeric effect into the carbonyl, decreasing the partial positive character of the carbonyl carbon.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should inspect resonance/mesomeric donation from nitrogen into the carbonyl and conclude that this lowers carbonyl electrophilicity relative to esters, making amides less reactive.
**Concept category:** mesomeric effects / carbonyl reactivity
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "electronegative atoms are better electron pair donors (i.e., an amide is significantly less reactive than an ester)."

## Misconception 409: Students may choose the wrong epoxide carbon to attack, not recognizing that the less substituted carbon is preferred under the basic conditions shown.

**Topic:** epoxide opening / site of nucleophilic attack
**Correct concept:** Under basic conditions, nucleophilic opening of an epoxide occurs preferentially at the less substituted carbon because steric and electronic effects both favor that site.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the two epoxide carbons and choose the less hindered, more accessible carbon for attack when the epoxide is opened under basic conditions.
**Concept category:** sterics/electrophilic site selection
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "the steric and electronic effects reinforce one another and lead to attack at the less-substituted carbon."

## Misconception 410: Students fail to prefer the pathway that forms the more stable carbocation intermediate, or do not use carbocation stability to predict regioselectivity.

**Topic:** electrophilic addition / carbocation intermediate stability and regioselectivity
**Correct concept:** When different carbocation intermediates are possible, the more stable carbocation is favored and determines the major product.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare the possible carbocations, assess inductive/hyperconjugative stabilization, and choose the pathway leading to the energetically more accessible intermediate and product.
**Concept category:** intermediate stability / regioselectivity
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "there is a choice of primary or secondary carbocation intermediates... Electronic effects (in this case, +I/hyperconjugation) favor the secondary carbocation intermediate"

## Misconception 411: Students forget to add or remove formal charges appropriately during curved-arrow mechanisms, especially after forming a bond from a neutral lone pair or after a heteroatom regains its lone pair.

**Topic:** charge balancing in mechanisms / formal charges after bond formation or heteroatom deprotonation
**Correct concept:** Chemical equations and mechanisms must conserve charge; formal charges need to be updated whenever electrons move or a bond forms/breaks.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should track electron pairs and valence counts stepwise, assigning a positive charge when a neutral donor forms a bond and removing positive charge when the heteroatom regains its lone pair.
**Concept category:** formal charge bookkeeping
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "students must ensure they balance charges. Most commonly, students forget to introduce a positive charge when using a neutral lone pair to form a bond"

## Misconception 412: Students do not distinguish when a nucleophile should attack carbon versus when a base should attack hydrogen.

**Topic:** mechanistic reasoning / attack on carbon vs hydrogen in ambiguous cases
**Correct concept:** Nucleophiles attack carbon electrophiles, whereas bases remove protons; the reaction pathway depends on whether the reagent is acting as a nucleophile or a base.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the reagent role first and then apply the corresponding site preference: carbon attack for nucleophiles, proton abstraction for bases.
**Concept category:** nucleophile/base distinction
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "if the attacking species is a nucleophile, it will attack the carbon, whereas if it is a base, it will attack the hydrogen."

## Misconception 413: Students assume that all substitution reactions proceed by SN2-style concerted attack and leaving-group loss, even when the substrate and conditions favor SN1.

**Topic:** mechanistic order of steps / SN1 versus SN2
**Correct concept:** Some reactions proceed by SN1 when leaving-group departure to form a stabilized carbocation is favored and the nucleophile is weak; the order of steps can differ from SN2.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate nucleophile strength and carbocation stability; if carbocation formation is energetically accessible, loss of leaving group can occur first, followed by nucleophilic attack.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanism classification
**Population:** students
**Source:** Priority and Selectivity Rules To Help Students Predict Organic Reaction Mechanisms (priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms/priority-and-selectivity-rules-to-help-students-predict-organic-reaction-mechanisms.md)
**Evidence:** "the priority rules would predict a plausible mechanism and the correct product, but would always suggest an SN2 mechanism... rather than SN1"

## Misconception 414: The student expects the unrearranged substitution product 2-bromo-3,3-dimethylbutane instead of the rearranged product.

**Topic:** Organic reactions / SN1 substitution rearrangements
**Correct concept:** SN1 reactions can proceed through a carbocation intermediate that may rearrange to form a more stable carbocation before nucleophilic attack, leading to the rearranged product 2-bromo-2,3-dimethylbutane.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first identify ionization to form a carbocation, then consider possible hydride or alkyl shifts that produce a more stable carbocation, and finally show bromide attack at the rearranged carbocation to give the observed product.
**Concept category:** carbocation rearrangement
**Population:** students in the organic chemistry course completing dialogue assignments
**Source:** The Role of Multiple Teaching Strategies in Promoting Active Learning in Organic Chemistry (s00897990297a/s00897990297a.md)
**Evidence:** "2-bromo-2,3 dimethylbutane is the observed product, not 2-bromo-3,3 dimethylbutane, which is what I expected."

## Misconception 415: Students struggle to identify 2,2,4-trimethylpentane as an isomer of octane and confuse constitutional isomers.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Structural isomerism
**Correct concept:** 2,2,4-trimethylpentane is a constitutional isomer of octane because it has the same molecular formula as octane but a different arrangement of atoms.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare molecular formulas first; if they match, then check whether the atoms are connected differently. Different connectivity with the same formula means constitutional isomerism.
**Concept category:** structural isomerism
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "One among common misconception identifed was structural isomerism. In this context, students may struggle to identify and diferentiate between constitutional isomers, such as 2,2,4-trimethylpentane (Q12) and other alkanes, causing confusion when tasked with classifying it as an isomer of octane."

## Misconception 416: Students confuse addition and substitution reactions, and may classify the reaction of ethyne with bromine as substitution rather than addition.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Reaction types: alkynes with halogens
**Correct concept:** Ethyne reacts with bromine by electrophilic addition, producing a dibromoalkene, not by substitution.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should recognize that unsaturated hydrocarbons like alkynes undergo addition at the multiple bond. Bromine adds across the triple bond, so the product and mechanism indicate addition, not substitution.
**Concept category:** reaction types
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "students may confuse substitution and addition reactions, leading to incorrect identifcation of the reaction type. In this regards, many students misinterpreted the reaction of alkynes with halogens."

## Misconception 417: Students think C2H2 + Br2 is a substitution reaction analogous to ethane bromination.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Reaction of ethyne with bromine
**Correct concept:** C2H2 + Br2 is an addition reaction that forms CHBr=CHBr (a dibromoalkene).
**Expected reasoning:** A student should note that ethyne is unsaturated, so bromine adds to the multiple bond. The expected product structure confirms addition across the triple bond rather than replacement of H atoms.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanisms
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "In the case of the reaction between ethyne (C₂H₂) and bromine (Br₂), several students incorrectly identifed it as a substitution reaction rather than an electrophilic addition reaction."

## Misconception 418: Students overgeneralize alkane reactions to alkynes, treating bromine addition to ethyne like substitution in ethane.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Reaction of alkynes with bromine
**Correct concept:** Alkynes undergo addition with bromine; alkanes undergo substitution under radical conditions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should distinguish saturated from unsaturated hydrocarbons. The presence of a multiple bond in an alkyne means addition across that bond, whereas substitution is characteristic of alkanes under appropriate conditions.
**Concept category:** reaction types
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "This misconception likely stems from overgeneralization of alkane reactions."

## Misconception 419: Students think all bromine-containing compounds give a white precipitate with alcoholic AgNO3.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Alkyl halides and alcoholic silver nitrate test
**Correct concept:** Only alkyl halides that can react under the test conditions give the precipitate; aryl halides such as bromobenzene do not react because the C–Br bond in aromatic compounds is more stable.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify whether the compound is an alkyl halide or an aryl halide before predicting the test result. Bond type and reactivity determine whether AgBr precipitate forms.
**Concept category:** alkyl vs aryl halides
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "Many students may incorrectly predict that all bromine containing compounds will give a white precipitate. However, bromobenzene (an aryl halide) does not react with alcoholic AgNO₃"

## Misconception 420: Students cannot distinguish SN1 from SN2 and may think methyl chloride reacts faster than tert-butyl chloride because it is less sterically hindered.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / SN1 vs SN2 mechanisms
**Correct concept:** tert-Butyl chloride reacts rapidly via SN1 because tertiary carbocation formation is favored; methyl chloride does not react faster in this context.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should first determine which mechanism is plausible from substrate structure. For SN1, carbocation stability controls rate, so a tertiary substrate reacts faster than a methyl substrate. Steric hindrance is not the deciding factor for SN1 rate.
**Concept category:** reaction mechanisms
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "students may struggle to diferentiate between SN1 and SN2 mechanisms (Q36), particularly when predicting the reaction rate of various alkyl halides."

## Misconception 421: Students think smaller or less sterically hindered halides react faster in SN1 because they are smaller.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / SN1 reactivity and carbocation stability
**Correct concept:** SN1 rate depends mainly on carbocation stability, so tertiary halides react faster than primary or methyl halides.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the stability of the carbocation intermediate formed in the rate-determining step. More stable carbocations form more readily and increase the SN1 reaction rate.
**Concept category:** carbocation stability
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "students may mistakenly think that a smaller molecule like methyl chloride would react faster because it is less sterically hindered. This misconception arises from an incomplete understanding of carbocation stability"

## Misconception 422: Students confuse the product of acetonitrile reduction with ethylamine or dimethylamine instead of methylamine.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Reduction of nitriles with LiAlH4
**Correct concept:** LiAlH4 reduces nitriles to primary amines; acetonitrile (CH3CN) gives methylamine (CH3NH2).
**Expected reasoning:** A student should track the carbon skeleton during reduction and remember that the nitrile carbon remains part of the product as one carbon unit, yielding a primary amine with the same carbon count plus one from the nitrile carbon arrangement.
**Concept category:** reduction reactions
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "When acetonitrile (CH₃CN) is reduced with LiAlH₄, it forms methylamine (CH₃NH₂) (Q37). However, students may confuse the product with ethylamine or dimethylamine"

## Misconception 423: Students classify dehydrohalogenation of 2-bromobutane with strong base as SN1 or E1 instead of E2.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / Elimination mechanisms
**Correct concept:** The reaction proceeds by E2 elimination, a one-step process in which the strong base removes a β-hydrogen while the leaving group departs.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should use reaction conditions to identify the mechanism: a strong base with a secondary alkyl halide favors E2. They should look for concerted elimination rather than stepwise ionization.
**Concept category:** elimination mechanisms
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "students may incorrectly identify the mechanism as SN1 or E1, assuming that all elimination reactions follow these pathways. In reality, this reaction proceeds via the E2 mechanism"

## Misconception 424: Students do not understand regioselective elimination in E2 and therefore cannot predict the major Saytzeff product.

**Topic:** Organic chemistry / E2 regioselectivity and Saytzeff product
**Correct concept:** E2 elimination is regioselective, often favoring the more substituted alkene (Saytzeff product) when conditions permit.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare possible elimination products and apply regioselectivity rules to identify which alkene is more substituted and therefore the major product under typical E2 conditions.
**Concept category:** regioselectivity
**Population:** students
**Source:** Investigating the impact of using web‑based discussion tools on students' conceptual understanding and 21st century skills development in organic chemistry education (s44217-025-00548-w/s44217-025-00548-w.md)
**Evidence:** "students may not grasp the concept of regioselective elimination in the E2 mechanism, leading to errors when predicting the major product (i.e., Saytzef product formation)."

## Misconception 425: Students use the EPF mainly to connect reactants and products or as an afterthought rather than as a tool for mechanistic reasoning.

**Topic:** Electron-pushing formalism / Curved arrow meaning and function
**Correct concept:** Curved arrows are used to represent electron flow and explain why and how a reaction occurs.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify electron-rich and electron-poor sites, then use curved arrows to show the movement of electron pairs that accounts for bond changes and product formation.
**Concept category:** electron transfer
**Population:** undergraduate and graduate students
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** students need to know why thinking mechanistically is a useful long-term goal and why identifying areas of high and low electron density will assist mechanistic thinking

## Misconception 426: Students think the goal of mechanism problems is to reach the product rather than explain the process of the reaction.

**Topic:** Mechanism problem solving / Product-oriented thinking
**Correct concept:** Mechanism problem solving should explain the process, including why each step occurs based on chemical properties.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason from reactivity and electron flow step by step, evaluating whether each intermediate and step is chemically feasible before moving toward the product.
**Concept category:** product-oriented thinking
**Population:** students in organic chemistry mechanism studies
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** many students had the product-oriented mindset: "I need something to do that in a following step, so we should generate an entity that can do it"

## Misconception 427: Students believe a mechanistic step occurs because it makes the reaction go forward or because it is needed for the next step, rather than because of chemical principles.

**Topic:** Mechanism problem solving / Teleological reasoning
**Correct concept:** Each step should be justified by underlying chemical properties such as acidity/basicity, nucleophilicity, electrophilicity, and stability.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should ask what electron-density changes make the step favorable and whether the step follows from acid-base or nucleophile-electrophile interactions.
**Concept category:** product-oriented thinking
**Population:** students and course instructor discourse reported in a study of mechanism solving
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** the reason for a proton-transfer step occurring at a particular site was because "it makes the reaction go forward"

## Misconception 428: Students think they can solve mechanism problems by recalling memorized products or reagent-product pairings without mechanism-based reasoning.

**Topic:** Mechanism problem solving / Rote or memorized mechanisms
**Correct concept:** Students should use mechanistic reasoning to infer products or pathways, especially for unfamiliar reactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should identify the likely reactive sites and use electron movement and molecular properties to determine the outcome instead of relying on recall alone.
**Concept category:** memorization
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** students often recognize the features of familiar reactions and simply recall the product or reagents, either correctly or incorrectly, without considering the reaction mechanism

## Misconception 429: Students treat visible structural features such as charges, lone pairs, and functional groups as bookkeeping cues rather than as indicators of chemical behavior and reactivity.

**Topic:** Mechanism problem solving / Structural-feature focus
**Correct concept:** Structural features should be interpreted in terms of their chemical implications for reactivity, such as acidity/basicity, nucleophilicity, electrophilicity, and stability.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect visible features to electron distribution and use those inferred properties to justify a mechanistic step or compare reactivity.
**Concept category:** surface features
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** many students focus on explicit, surface-level features (such as charges, visible lone electron pairs, or functional groups), even when engaging with the EPF, to justify depicting mechanistic steps

## Misconception 430: Students assume that molecules with similar visible structures must have similar reactivity.

**Topic:** Mechanism problem solving / Structural similarity equals reactivity
**Correct concept:** Reactivity depends on implicit properties and context, not just structural similarity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare relevant properties such as acidity/basicity, resonance, and substituent effects rather than relying only on surface similarity.
**Concept category:** surface features
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** structural similarity does not always indicate similarity in reactivity

## Misconception 431: Students do not recognize structures on paper as representations of the molecular world and instead manipulate them as drawings detached from chemical meaning.

**Topic:** Representations in organic chemistry / Chemical drawings as mere pictures
**Correct concept:** Lewis structures, bond-line structures, and related diagrams represent real molecular structures and processes.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should interpret each symbol and drawing as conveying submicroscopic information about atoms, bonds, charges, and electron movement in actual molecules.
**Concept category:** representational competence
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** students often do not consider the structures on paper to be representations of the molecular world

## Misconception 432: Students fail to account for implicit atoms or implicit lone electron pairs in chemical formulas or bond-line structures.

**Topic:** Representations in organic chemistry / Implicit atoms and lone pairs omitted from bond-line drawings
**Correct concept:** Students must infer all omitted atoms, hydrogens, and lone pairs from the representation when reasoning mechanistically.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reconstruct the complete Lewis picture mentally before deciding where electrons can move and which sites are reactive.
**Concept category:** representational competence
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** students struggle to fully interpret representations, such as not considering implicit atoms or implicit lone electron pairs

## Misconception 433: Students think reaction coordinate diagrams show only the major reactants, intermediates, or products.

**Topic:** Reaction coordinate diagrams / Major species only
**Correct concept:** Reaction coordinate diagrams represent the energy changes along a pathway, including transition states and the relationship among all steps in the mechanism.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should read the diagram as an energy profile for the reaction pathway, not as a list of only major species.
**Concept category:** reaction coordinate diagrams
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** students often think that only the major reactants, intermediates, or products of mechanisms are depicted on RCDs

## Misconception 434: Students misinterpret the peaks, valleys, and axes of reaction coordinate diagrams.

**Topic:** Reaction coordinate diagrams / Surface features of RCDs
**Correct concept:** The peaks, valleys, and axes have specific meanings related to transition states, intermediates, and energy changes.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should connect each graphical feature to the corresponding energetic and mechanistic meaning in the reaction pathway.
**Concept category:** reaction coordinate diagrams
**Population:** organic chemistry students
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** students' difficulties with understanding the surface features of RCDs (e.g., the peaks, valleys, and axes)

## Misconception 435: Students judge reactivity or choose mechanisms by focusing on charge alone.

**Topic:** Mechanistic reasoning / Charge as sole determinant of reactivity
**Correct concept:** Reactivity must be evaluated using multiple factors, including electron density, stability, acid-base behavior, and molecular context.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should consider charge as one clue among several and determine whether electron movement and the overall mechanism are chemically plausible.
**Concept category:** multivariate reasoning
**Population:** students in studies of mechanism and reactivity reasoning
**Source:** Students' Strategies, Struggles, and Successes with Mechanism Problem Solving in Organic Chemistry: A Scoping Review of the Research Literature (students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the/students-strategies-struggles-and-successes-with-mechanism-problem-solving-in-organic-chemistry-a-scoping-review-of-the.md)
**Evidence:** students who focused on charge alone to propose reaction mechanisms were less successful than those who focused on electron movement and electron density

## Misconception 436: Students think H2SO4 should be used for elimination because its anion acts as a base, and HCl should be used for substitution because Cl− is a stronger nucleophile; they also frame the choice mainly as whether the anion is or is not nucleophilic, overlooking the role of water and the full reaction environment.

**Topic:** Alcohol elimination and substitution reactions / Acid choice for elimination vs substitution
**Correct concept:** The acid choice is tied to which reaction pathway is favored under the experimental conditions: concentrated H2SO4 favors dehydration/elimination, while HCl favors substitution by providing Cl− as nucleophile under conditions that suppress elimination.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason by comparing reaction conditions and products: in acidic conditions, cyclohexanol can dehydrate to cyclohexene with H2SO4 at high temperature, whereas tert-butyl alcohol can undergo substitution to tert-butyl chloride with HCl at lower temperature. The acid/anions matter in the context of the whole competing reaction system, not only as isolated nucleophile/base labels.
**Concept category:** acid selection / nucleophile-base competition
**Prevalence:** Most participants could explain this choice with their reasons (N=19 of 29).
**Population:** 29 undergraduate students in Organic Chemistry Laboratory I
**Source:** Teaching Some Features of Competitive Elimination and Substitution Reactions of Alcohols through a Guided Inquiry Laboratory Activity (teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry/teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry.md)
**Evidence:** "In reaction 1 (elimination reaction), we should use H2SO4 as the acid. Because of its anion stability, HSO4− acts as a base...We should use HCl as the acid in reaction 2 (substitution reaction). In the Cl− anion, the charge is on a single atom, so the Cl− anion has greater nucleophilic strength than the HSO4− anion."

## Misconception 437: Students think the elimination reaction happens at high temperature because 'entropy increases' or because the reaction shifts to products, and that substitution should simply be run cold to prevent alkene formation.

**Topic:** Alcohol elimination and substitution reactions / Temperature effects on competing pathways
**Correct concept:** Higher temperature favors elimination over substitution in this system, but the correct explanation should involve the temperature dependence of the entropy term (TΔS) and the competing reaction equilibrium/thermodynamics, not just a vague statement that entropy itself increases.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that raising temperature increases the importance of the TΔS term, making the elimination pathway more favorable; lowering temperature reduces elimination and helps substitution dominate. The explanation should connect temperature to equilibrium and the driving force of the specific competing reactions.
**Concept category:** temperature / entropy / equilibrium
**Prevalence:** All participants decided the temperature correctly (N=29); 6/14 in the first year and 13/15 in the second year justified it.
**Population:** 29 undergraduate students in Organic Chemistry Laboratory I
**Source:** Teaching Some Features of Competitive Elimination and Substitution Reactions of Alcohols through a Guided Inquiry Laboratory Activity (teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry/teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry.md)
**Evidence:** "In reaction 1, the experiment environment should be hot. As the temperature increases, the entropy increases. The reaction shifts toward the products...However, the temperature must be low for reaction 2 to occur. If it is hot, then the elimination product is formed. It should be at a low temperature to prevent the formation of the elimination product."

## Misconception 438: Students think concentrated acid is required mainly because it keeps the reaction environment anhydrous or because dilution would push equilibrium left by increasing water, treating the issue as simply 'less water is better' rather than understanding the role of concentration in favoring product formation in these competing reactions.

**Topic:** Alcohol elimination and substitution reactions / Acid concentration and water content
**Correct concept:** Concentrated acid conditions favor the desired reaction pathway by minimizing water and supporting the product-favoring equilibrium conditions for these alcohol reactions.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that concentrated H2SO4/HCl provides a more anhydrous medium, which favors formation of the desired product and suppresses reversal/back reaction. The reasoning should connect concentration with the reaction equilibrium and product yield in the specific elimination/substitution system.
**Concept category:** acid concentration / equilibrium / water content
**Prevalence:** Almost all participants selected the appropriate acid concentration (N=28); 6/14 in the first year and 15/15 in the second year justified it.
**Population:** 29 undergraduate students in Organic Chemistry Laboratory I
**Source:** Teaching Some Features of Competitive Elimination and Substitution Reactions of Alcohols through a Guided Inquiry Laboratory Activity (teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry/teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry.md)
**Evidence:** "The acid used must be concentrated to keep the environment anhydrous. If we use diluted acid, the amount of water formed will increase, and the chemical balance will shift to the left to reduce the amount of product."

## Misconception 439: Students think the driving force of elimination is simply 'entropy' or the number of molecules, rather than understanding the temperature-dependent entropy term and how it relates to reaction spontaneity.

**Topic:** Alcohol elimination and substitution reactions / Driving force for elimination
**Correct concept:** The driving force for elimination is the favorable entropy contribution at higher temperature, more properly expressed through TΔS, not just a raw count of molecules.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that elimination produces more particles than the reactant side, increasing disorder, and that the effect becomes more important at higher temperature through TΔS. The explanation should connect molecular count to entropy change and temperature dependence.
**Concept category:** driving force / entropy
**Prevalence:** 7/14 in the first year and 15/15 in the second year provided correct decisions about the driving force; 12 students gave justifications.
**Population:** 29 undergraduate students in Organic Chemistry Laboratory I
**Source:** Teaching Some Features of Competitive Elimination and Substitution Reactions of Alcohols through a Guided Inquiry Laboratory Activity (teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry/teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry.md)
**Evidence:** "The driving force of this reaction is entropy because, in the products, there are more number of molecules."

## Misconception 440: Students think the driving force for substitution is simply that the products have lower energy than the reactants, without explicitly connecting that idea to enthalpy change in the competing reaction context.

**Topic:** Alcohol elimination and substitution reactions / Driving force for substitution
**Correct concept:** The driving force for substitution is the favorable enthalpy change associated with forming lower-energy products.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that if the products are at lower potential energy than the reactants, the enthalpy change is favorable and supports the substitution pathway under the given conditions.
**Concept category:** driving force / enthalpy
**Prevalence:** 7/14 in the first year and 15/15 in the second year provided correct decisions about the driving force; 12 students gave justifications.
**Population:** 29 undergraduate students in Organic Chemistry Laboratory I
**Source:** Teaching Some Features of Competitive Elimination and Substitution Reactions of Alcohols through a Guided Inquiry Laboratory Activity (teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry/teaching-some-features-of-competitive-elimination-and-substitution-reactions-of-alcohols-through-a-guided-inquiry.md)
**Evidence:** "The energy of the products is lower than the energy of the reactants."

## Misconception 441: Students think that a leaving group is simply 'good' or 'bad' without being able to explain why, or they rely on memorized examples rather than underlying factors.

**Topic:** Leaving group departure / Leaving group ability
**Correct concept:** Leaving group ability depends on the stability of the anion formed after departure, especially size/polarizability and electronegativity.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should evaluate the conjugate base that would form after departure and compare how well its negative charge can be stabilized. Larger, more polarizable anions and more electronegative atoms are better at bearing negative charge, so they are better leaving groups.
**Concept category:** leaving group ability
**Source:** Using the Research Literature to Develop an Adaptive Intervention to Improve Student Explanations of an S_N1 Reaction Mechanism (using-the-research-literature-to-develop-an-adaptive-intervention-to-improve-student-explanations-of-an-sn1-reaction/using-the-research-literature-to-develop-an-adaptive-intervention-to-improve-student-explanations-of-an-sn1-reaction.md)
**Evidence:** "students describe leaving groups as 'good leaving groups' but are unable to explain why a leaving group is 'good.'"

## Misconception 442: Students think leaving group ability is determined by a single factor instead of considering both size/polarizability and electronegativity.

**Topic:** Leaving group departure / Leaving group ability
**Correct concept:** Leaving group ability is multivariate; both size/polarizability and electronegativity matter depending on the comparison.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should compare candidate leaving groups using size first when atomic sizes differ, then electronegativity when sizes are similar, because both affect how stable the departing anion will be.
**Concept category:** leaving group ability
**Source:** Using the Research Literature to Develop an Adaptive Intervention to Improve Student Explanations of an S_N1 Reaction Mechanism (using-the-research-literature-to-develop-an-adaptive-intervention-to-improve-student-explanations-of-an-sn1-reaction/using-the-research-literature-to-develop-an-adaptive-intervention-to-improve-student-explanations-of-an-sn1-reaction.md)
**Evidence:** "size and electronegativity must both be considered when evaluating leaving group ability"

## Misconception 443: Students treat the 'number of substituents' as a heuristic rule rather than understanding why more substituted carbocations are more stable.

**Topic:** Carbocation stability / Substitution trend
**Correct concept:** Carbocation stability is explained by electron donation from substituents and associated electron density effects; tertiary > secondary > primary is a trend, not just a count.
**Expected reasoning:** A student should reason that electron-donating groups stabilize the positive charge on a carbocation by distributing electron density, so more substituted carbocations tend to be more stable because they have more electron donation.
